rel|1|l1-1|k-2|Days That Matter — Families keep different special days, and each day matters to them.
rel|1|l1-2|k-2|Saying "Some Families" — We say "some families" or "Muslims celebrate" to speak fairly about each day.
rel|1|l1-3|k-2|Christmas and Hanukkah — Christians keep Christmas and Jewish families keep Hanukkah, often in December.
rel|1|l1-4|k-2|Diwali, Festival of Lights — Many Hindus, Sikhs and Jains keep Diwali with small lamps and bright colors.
rel|1|l1-5|k-2|Eid al-Fitr — Muslims celebrate Eid al-Fitr at the end of Ramadan, a month of fasting.
rel|1|l1-6|k-2|Lunar New Year and Vaisakhi — Many families mark a new year in winter or spring, like Lunar New Year and Vaisakhi.
rel|1|l1-7|k-2|Good Questions — We can ask a friend kindly about their special day and listen well.
rel|1|l1-8|k-2|Being Together — Most special days share lights, food, songs and being together.
rel|1|l2-1|k-2|A Place to Gather — A place of worship is where a group meets to pray, sing, read and learn.
rel|1|l2-2|k-2|Holy Days to Gather — Many groups gather on a special day each week.
rel|1|l2-3|k-2|Church and Synagogue — Christians gather in churches; Jewish people gather in synagogues.
rel|1|l2-4|k-2|The Mosque — Muslims pray in a mosque, often on soft rugs, facing toward Mecca.
rel|1|l2-5|k-2|A Hindu Temple — Hindus visit a temple to pray and see images of the gods.
rel|1|l2-6|k-2|The Gurdwara — Sikhs gather at a gurdwara, where anyone can share a free meal.
rel|1|l2-7|k-2|Guest Manners — A good guest uses a quiet voice and follows the rules of the place.
rel|1|l2-8|k-2|A Walk in Chicago — Chicago has many kinds of places of worship, close together.
rel|10|l19-1|6-8|Arabia Before Islam — Before Islam, Arabia was a land of trading towns and desert tribes, with Mecca and its Kaaba at the center of trade and worship.
rel|10|l19-2|6-8|The Cave and the Message — Muslims believe that around 610 CE, the angel Gabriel brought Muhammad the first words of the Qur'an in the cave of Hira.
rel|10|l19-3|6-8|From Mecca to Medina — In 622 Muhammad and his followers moved from Mecca to Medina, a journey called the hijra that starts the Islamic calendar.
rel|10|l19-4|6-8|Return to Mecca — In 630 Muhammad returned to Mecca, and Muslims rededicated the Kaaba to one God; he died in Medina in 632.
rel|10|l19-5|6-8|A Book Meant to Be Recited — Muslims believe the Qur'an is God's word in Arabic, first memorized and recited, then gathered into one written book.
rel|10|l19-6|6-8|Surahs, Ayahs and the Sunnah — The Qur'an has 114 surahs made of ayahs, and Muslims also learn from the Sunnah, the example of Muhammad recorded in hadith.
rel|10|l19-7|6-8|Faith and Prayer — The first two pillars are the shahada, the statement of faith, and salat, prayer five times a day.
rel|10|l19-8|6-8|Giving and Fasting — Zakat is giving part of one's wealth to people in need, and sawm is fasting from dawn to sunset in the month of Ramadan.
rel|10|l19-9|6-8|The Pilgrimage to Mecca — The hajj is a pilgrimage to Mecca that Muslims who are able should make once in a lifetime.
rel|10|l19-10|6-8|Sunni and Shia — Sunni and Shia Muslims share the Qur'an and the Five Pillars but disagree about who should have led after Muhammad.
rel|10|l19-11|6-8|The Mosque and the Moon — Muslims gather at the mosque, especially for Friday prayer, and follow a lunar calendar whose months move through the seasons.
rel|10|l20-1|6-8|The First Four Caliphs — After Muhammad, four caliphs led the Muslim community from 632 to 661, and Muslim rule spread quickly beyond Arabia.
rel|10|l20-2|6-8|The Umayyads of Damascus — The Umayyad caliphs ruled from Damascus from 661 to 750, over an empire stretching from Spain to Central Asia.
rel|10|l20-3|6-8|The Abbasids of Baghdad — The Abbasids ruled from 750 and built Baghdad, a round city that became one of the largest in the world.
rel|10|l20-4|6-8|The House of Wisdom — In Abbasid Baghdad, scholars of many faiths translated Greek, Persian and Indian books into Arabic and added new ideas.
rel|10|l20-5|6-8|Algebra and the Stars — Scholars in the Muslim world advanced math and astronomy; the word algebra comes from al-Khwarizmi's book.
rel|10|l20-6|6-8|Medicine and Paper — Muslim-ruled cities built hospitals, produced great medical books and spread paper-making from China to the West.
rel|10|l20-7|6-8|Al-Andalus and Córdoba — Muslims ruled much of Spain, called al-Andalus, for centuries, and Córdoba became a great city of learning.
rel|10|l20-8|6-8|Mali, Mansa Musa and Timbuktu — Islam spread to West Africa along trade routes, and Mali's ruler Mansa Musa made Timbuktu a center of learning.
rel|10|l20-9|6-8|The Ottoman Empire — The Ottomans took Constantinople in 1453 and ruled a large Muslim empire until after World War I.
rel|10|l20-10|6-8|Islam in South and Southeast Asia — Today most Muslims live in Asia, and Indonesia has more Muslims than any other country.
rel|10|l20-11|6-8|Muslims in America — Muslims have been in America since the time of slavery, and today Chicago has one of the country's largest Muslim communities.
rel|11|l21-1|6-8|The Vedas and the Upanishads — The Vedas are the oldest Hindu scriptures, and the Upanishads within them explore the link between the self and ultimate reality.
rel|11|l21-2|6-8|Two Great Epics — The Ramayana and the Mahabharata are long story-poems that teach Hindus about duty through heroes facing hard choices.
rel|11|l21-3|6-8|Brahman and the Deities — Many Hindus worship many deities while understanding them as forms or faces of one ultimate reality, Brahman.
rel|11|l21-4|6-8|Dharma, Karma, Samsara, Moksha — Hindu teaching links duty, the results of actions, the cycle of rebirth and the hope of release into one picture of life.
rel|11|l21-5|6-8|Puja, Temples and Festivals — Hindu worship happens at home and in temples through offerings, prayers and festivals that bring families together.
rel|11|l21-6|6-8|Caste: History and Reform — Caste was a social ranking system in India; many reformers, Hindu and others, worked to end discrimination based on it.
rel|11|l21-7|6-8|Siddhartha Becomes the Buddha — Buddhist tradition tells how Prince Siddhartha left a life of comfort to seek an end to suffering and became the Buddha, the awakened one.
rel|11|l21-8|6-8|Four Truths and an Eightfold Path — Buddhists teach that suffering has a cause and an end, and that the Eightfold Path is the way to reach that end, called nirvana.
rel|11|l21-9|6-8|Ashoka and the Silk Road — Buddhism spread from India across Asia through rulers like Ashoka, monks, pilgrims and traders on the Silk Road.
rel|11|l21-10|6-8|Theravada, Mahayana and Chicago — Buddhism grew into major branches, including Theravada and Mahayana, and today Buddhists of many branches live in Chicago.
rel|11|l22-1|6-8|Guru Nanak and the Ten Gurus — Sikhism began with Guru Nanak in the Punjab, who taught devotion to one God and the equality of all people.
rel|11|l22-2|6-8|Scripture, Khalsa and Langar — Sikhs honor the Guru Granth Sahib as a living Guru, many join the Khalsa, and every gurdwara serves a free shared meal called langar.
rel|11|l22-3|6-8|Mahavira and Ahimsa — Jains follow the teaching of Mahavira and the earlier Tirthankaras, which centers on ahimsa, doing no harm to any living being.
rel|11|l22-4|6-8|The Jain Vows — Jains take vows of non-harm, truth, not stealing, self-control and non-attachment; monks and nuns keep them strictly and lay people in a milder form.
rel|11|l22-5|6-8|Confucius and Ren — Confucius taught that people become truly good through ren, humane care for others, practiced in everyday relationships.
rel|11|l22-6|6-8|Li and Xiao — Confucian teaching holds that proper conduct, li, and devotion to parents, xiao, build harmony in families and in society.
rel|11|l22-7|6-8|The Examinations — For about 1,300 years, China chose many officials through exams on the Confucian classics.
rel|11|l22-8|6-8|Laozi and the Dao — Daoists seek harmony with the Dao, the natural way of all things, through simplicity, balance and wu wei.
rel|11|l22-9|6-8|Shinto: Kami and Shrines — Shinto, the traditional religion of Japan, honors the kami, sacred powers found in nature, ancestors and special places.
rel|11|l22-10|6-8|Three Teachings and Two Ways — In China and Japan, many people blend several traditions, which makes simple religious labels hard to apply.
rel|12|l23-1|6-8|Scripture Without a Book — In many Indigenous traditions, sacred teachings are passed on by voice, song and ceremony instead of a written book.
rel|12|l23-2|6-8|Ancestors, Land and Seasons — Many Indigenous traditions tie the sacred to ancestors, to a specific homeland and to the turning seasons.
rel|12|l23-3|6-8|Olodumare and the Orisha — Yoruba tradition teaches of a supreme being, Olodumare, and many orisha who each guide part of life and nature.
rel|12|l23-4|6-8|Ifa: Reading for Guidance — In Ifa divination, a trained priest recites memorized verses to help a person seek guidance.
rel|12|l23-5|6-8|Traditions Formed Under Slavery — Enslaved Africans carried their traditions to the Americas, where they grew into Santeria, Candomble and Vodou.
rel|12|l23-6|6-8|Many Nations, Many Traditions — There is no single Native American religion; each nation has its own language, stories and ceremonies.
rel|12|l23-7|6-8|Nations of the Great Lakes — The Potawatomi, Ojibwe and Miami peoples lived in the Great Lakes region, including the land that is now Chicago.
rel|12|l23-8|6-8|Banned, Then Protected — U.S. policy once banned many Native ceremonies and sent children to boarding schools; the 1978 law declared a duty to protect Native religious freedom.
rel|12|l23-9|6-8|Tapu, Mana and the Sea — Many Polynesian traditions teach of mana, a sacred power, and tapu, rules that set sacred things apart.
rel|12|l23-10|6-8|Revival and Respect Today — Indigenous communities are reviving languages and ceremonies, and outsiders show respect by following each community's protocol.
rel|12|l24-1|6-8|Forty-Five Words — The First Amendment's two religion clauses keep government from setting up a religion and protect each person's right to practice.
rel|12|l24-2|6-8|Cases That Tested the Clauses — Supreme Court cases apply the First Amendment to real conflicts between faith and government.
rel|12|l24-3|6-8|Learning About Religion at School — Public schools may teach about religion objectively, but they may not teach students to practice or believe it.
rel|12|l24-4|6-8|A Right for Everyone — The Universal Declaration of Human Rights of 1948 names freedom of thought, conscience and religion as a right for all people.
rel|12|l24-5|6-8|When Freedom Is Denied — In many places people still face harm for their religion, and watchdog groups track these limits.
rel|12|l24-6|6-8|The 1893 Parliament — The World's Parliament of Religions in Chicago in 1893 was an early meeting of many world traditions in one place.
rel|12|l24-7|6-8|Chicago's Houses of Worship — The Chicago area is home to houses of worship from nearly every major world tradition.
rel|12|l24-8|6-8|Counting the World's Faiths — Researchers estimate how many people follow each religion, but the numbers differ because counting belief is hard.
rel|12|l24-9|6-8|People With No Religion — A large and growing share of people do not belong to any religion, and they hold a wide range of views.
rel|12|l24-10|6-8|Dividing and Uniting — Religion has been part of both terrible conflicts and great movements for justice.
rel|12|l24-11|6-8|Disagreeing With Respect — People can disagree deeply about religion and still treat one another with respect.
rel|13|l25-1|9-10|A Text a Community Treasures — A text becomes sacred through the way a community receives it, treats it and lives by it, not only through what is written in it.
rel|13|l25-2|9-10|What Sacred Texts Do — Communities use sacred texts in worship, as guides for law and ethics, and as the story of who they are.
rel|13|l25-3|9-10|Who Wrote It? — Traditions often hold beliefs about who gave or wrote their texts, and scholars also study how texts were composed; a careful reader keeps both in view.
rel|13|l25-4|9-10|For Whom, When and Why? — Every text was first addressed to an audience at a certain time for a purpose, and knowing these helps a reader understand what it says.
rel|13|l25-5|9-10|The Careful Reader's Questions — Asking who, to whom, when and why turns a hard passage into one you can explain, even when some answers are uncertain.
rel|13|l25-6|9-10|Oral Tradition Before Writing — Many sacred texts were memorized and recited for generations before anyone wrote them down, and recitation still matters today.
rel|13|l25-7|9-10|Scrolls, Codices and Palm Leaves — The form a text is written on, from scroll to codex to palm leaf to printed book, shapes how people read and use it.
rel|13|l25-8|9-10|Canon: Deciding What Is In — A canon is a community's list of authoritative books, and different communities have drawn the list in different ways.
rel|13|l25-9|9-10|Law, Story and Poetry — Sacred texts contain many genres, and each genre asks to be read in its own way.
rel|13|l25-10|9-10|Prophecy, Letter and Teaching — Prophecy speaks a message to a people, letters answer a community's questions and teachings instruct, and each shapes how a passage reads.
rel|13|l25-11|9-10|Studying Religion in a Public School — Public schools may teach about religion objectively, which is different from practicing or promoting a faith.
rel|13|l26-1|9-10|One Verse, Two English Versions — Because no two languages match word for word, every translator must choose, and each choice is a small act of interpretation.
rel|13|l26-2|9-10|A Word That Carries Weight — A single translated word can support different readings, as the history of Isaiah 7:14 shows for Jews and Christians.
rel|13|l26-3|9-10|Can Scripture Be Translated? — Traditions answer differently: Muslims treat the Arabic Qur'an as the Qur'an itself, while Christians have long spread the Bible in many languages.
rel|13|l26-4|9-10|The Plain Sense — Many readers begin with the plain or literal sense of a text: what the words say in their ordinary meaning.
rel|13|l26-5|9-10|Allegory and Symbol — Allegorical readings find a second, spiritual meaning under the surface of a text, as Jews and Christians have done with the Song of Songs.
rel|13|l26-6|9-10|Reading in Context — Contextual reading asks what a text meant in its own time and place, using history, language and comparison with other texts.
rel|13|l26-7|9-10|Rabbis and Church Councils — Judaism and Christianity developed different bodies to interpret scripture: rabbis in study and debate, and church councils and traditions.
rel|13|l26-8|9-10|Ulama and Gurus — In Islam, trained scholars called ulama interpret the Qur'an and law; in Sikh and Hindu traditions, gurus and teaching lineages guide interpretation.
rel|13|l26-9|9-10|Many Readings Within One Tradition — Members of the same tradition often read the same text differently, so no single reading speaks for everyone.
rel|13|l26-10|9-10|Describe, Attribute, Do Not Rank — Fair comparison describes what each tradition holds, attributes every belief to its holders and never ranks one tradition above another.
rel|13|l26-11|9-10|Chicago, 1893 — The 1893 World's Parliament of Religions in Chicago was a landmark in bringing world traditions into conversation, with real limits scholars still discuss.
rel|13|l26-12|9-10|A Toolkit for the Course — Seven questions, drawn from this unit, will help you read any sacred text in this course carefully and fairly.
rel|14|l27-1|9-10|Three Parts, One Library — The Tanakh is a library of three parts, Torah, Nevi'im and Ketuvim, and its name is built from their first letters.
rel|14|l27-2|9-10|Reading Torah in Community — Jews read the whole Torah aloud each year in weekly portions, so the text is studied together rather than only read alone.
rel|14|l27-3|9-10|In the Beginning — Genesis opens with two accounts of creation that together teach that the world is made, ordered and good, and that humans carry the image of God.
rel|14|l27-4|9-10|The Garden and the Choice — Genesis 3 tells of a command broken in Eden, and Jews and Christians draw different lessons from it about human nature.
rel|14|l27-5|9-10|Noah and the Rainbow — The flood story ends with a covenant between God and all living things, the first covenant in the Torah.
rel|14|l27-6|9-10|Abraham's Covenant — God's covenant with Abraham, marked by promise and by circumcision, begins the family whose story the rest of the Torah tells.
rel|14|l27-7|9-10|Moses at the Burning Bush — Exodus tells how a people enslaved in Egypt were called out through Moses, who met God in a bush that burned and was not consumed.
rel|14|l27-8|9-10|Plagues, Passover and the Sea — The ten plagues and the night of the first Passover are remembered each spring in the Seder, which retells the Exodus as a present event.
rel|14|l27-9|9-10|Sinai and the Ten Commandments — At Mount Sinai the covenant with Abraham's family becomes a covenant with a whole people, summarized in ten commandments that traditions count in different ways.
rel|14|l27-10|9-10|Holiness in Leviticus and Deuteronomy — Leviticus and Deuteronomy turn the covenant into daily life, with laws about worship, food, justice and love of neighbor and stranger.
rel|14|l27-11|9-10|Made, Broken, Called — Read as a whole, the Torah tells of a world made good, damaged by human choices, and called back through covenant and law.
rel|14|l27-12|9-10|Two Communities, One Text — Jews read the Tanakh and Christians read the Old Testament: the same books arranged, translated and interpreted in different ways.
rel|14|l28-1|9-10|Samuel and the Demand for a King — In 1 Samuel the people ask for a king, and the prophet Samuel warns them that a king will take as well as give.
rel|14|l28-2|9-10|David, Solomon and a Divided Kingdom — David made Jerusalem his capital, Solomon built the First Temple there, and after Solomon the kingdom split in two.
rel|14|l28-3|9-10|Elijah and the Still Small Voice — Elijah confronted King Ahab over the worship of Baal, and his story lives on in Jewish, Christian and Muslim tradition.
rel|14|l28-4|9-10|Amos: Let Justice Roll Down — Amos, a herdsman who preached in the northern kingdom, taught that worship without justice for the poor is empty.
rel|14|l28-5|9-10|Isaiah: Holiness, Peace and Comfort — The book of Isaiah moves from warnings of judgment to visions of peace and words of comfort, and both Jews and Christians read it closely.
rel|14|l28-6|9-10|Jeremiah: Warning and Hope — Jeremiah warned Jerusalem of its coming fall, then told the exiles to build lives in Babylon and promised a new covenant.
rel|14|l28-7|9-10|By the Rivers of Babylon — In 586 BCE Babylon destroyed Jerusalem and its Temple, and the exile forced the people to ask how to worship without a land or a Temple.
rel|14|l28-8|9-10|Return and Rebuilding — After Persia conquered Babylon, some exiles returned to rebuild the Temple and Jerusalem, while many others stayed in the diaspora.
rel|14|l28-9|9-10|The Psalms as Prayer and Poetry — The 150 Psalms are poems of praise, lament and thanks that Jews and Christians have prayed and sung for more than two thousand years.
rel|14|l28-10|9-10|Wisdom in Proverbs and Ecclesiastes — Proverbs teaches that wise living usually brings good results, while Ecclesiastes asks whether anything under the sun lasts.
rel|14|l28-11|9-10|Job and the Question of Suffering — The book of Job asks why a good person suffers, rejects easy answers, and ends with God speaking from a whirlwind.
rel|14|l28-12|9-10|Ruth, Esther and the Five Scrolls — Ruth and Esther are short stories of loyalty and courage, and each belongs to the Five Scrolls read on Jewish holidays through the year.
rel|15|l29-1|9-10|Judea Under Roman Rule — The Gospels are set in a Jewish land ruled by Rome, and that fact shapes almost every scene in them.
rel|15|l29-2|9-10|Temple, Synagogue and Rival Groups — First-century Judaism was varied, and the Gospels show Jesus in conversation and debate with several of its groups.
rel|15|l29-3|9-10|What a Gospel Is — A Gospel is an ancient account of Jesus written to proclaim good news, not a modern biography, and knowing that changes how you read it.
rel|15|l29-4|9-10|The Three Synoptic Gospels — Matthew, Mark and Luke share so much material that they can be read side by side, yet each has its own emphasis.
rel|15|l29-5|9-10|John's Different Voice — The Gospel of John tells the story in its own order and language, presenting Jesus as the eternal Word made flesh.
rel|15|l29-6|9-10|Teacher and Healer in Galilee — The Gospels present Jesus as a traveling teacher and healer in Galilee who gathered disciples and drew crowds.
rel|15|l29-7|9-10|The Sermon on the Mount — Matthew gathers Jesus' core moral teaching into one long speech that Christians have read as the heart of his ethics.
rel|15|l29-8|9-10|The Good Samaritan — A parable is a short story that makes the listener answer a question, and the Good Samaritan turns who is my neighbor into who acted as one.
rel|15|l29-9|9-10|The Prodigal Son and the Sower — Parables about a lost son and a scattering farmer show how these stories work on hearers and why they are read in more than one way.
rel|15|l29-10|9-10|The Week of the Passion — All four Gospels slow down to tell the last week of Jesus' life in detail, from his entry into Jerusalem to his death on a Roman cross.
rel|15|l29-11|9-10|The Resurrection as Christians Believe It — The claim that Jesus rose from the dead is the center of Christian faith, told with an empty tomb and appearances in four differing accounts.
rel|15|l29-12|9-10|The Kingdom of God — The Gospels sum up Jesus' message as the kingdom of God, God's rule arriving now and still to come, which Christians pray for and interpret in several ways.
rel|15|l30-1|9-10|Pentecost in Jerusalem — The book of Acts tells how the followers of Jesus became a public movement in Jerusalem, beginning at the festival of Pentecost.
rel|15|l30-2|9-10|Saul Becomes Paul — Paul, a Jewish Pharisee who once opposed the followers of Jesus, became their most influential missionary after an experience on the road to Damascus.
rel|15|l30-3|9-10|Journeys and the Jerusalem Council — Paul's journeys planted churches in Roman cities, and a meeting in Jerusalem decided that Gentile believers did not have to become Jews first.
rel|15|l30-4|9-10|How an Ancient Letter Works — Paul's letters are real ancient letters, written to particular churches about particular problems, and they are the oldest writings in the New Testament.
rel|15|l30-5|9-10|Faith and Grace in Romans and Galatians — In Romans and Galatians, Paul teaches that people are put right with God through faith and God's grace, a teaching Christians interpret in different ways.
rel|15|l30-6|9-10|Love and the Body in Corinth — First Corinthians answers a divided church by describing it as one body and naming love as the greatest gift.
rel|15|l30-7|9-10|Revelation and Apocalyptic Writing — Revelation is apocalyptic writing, full of symbols and visions, which Christians have read as a message about suffering, hope and the end of history.
rel|15|l30-8|9-10|How the Canon Formed — The New Testament's twenty-seven books were gathered over about three centuries as churches decided which writings to read as scripture.
rel|15|l30-9|9-10|The Creeds — Creeds are short statements of belief that early church councils wrote to settle disputes, and the Nicene Creed is still recited by most Christians.
rel|15|l30-10|9-10|Reading the Old Through the New — Christians read the Hebrew Bible as the Old Testament, interpreting it through Jesus, while Jews read the same books as a complete scripture on their own terms.
rel|15|l30-11|9-10|Catholic, Orthodox and Protestant — Christianity today has three large branches, Catholic, Orthodox and Protestant, shaped by the split of 1054 and the Reformation that began in 1517.
rel|16|l31-1|9-10|The Shema: Six Words — The Shema, a six-word line from Deuteronomy, states the core Jewish belief that God is one and is recited morning and night.
rel|16|l31-2|9-10|Covenant and Chosenness — Jews understand their relationship with God as a covenant, a binding two-way agreement made with Abraham and renewed at Sinai, that brings duties rather than privilege.
rel|16|l31-3|9-10|Mitzvot and Halakhah — Jewish tradition counts 613 commandments in the Torah, and halakhah is the body of law and custom that works out how to keep them.
rel|16|l31-4|9-10|Welcoming Shabbat at Home — The Sabbath begins at home on Friday before sunset with candles, a blessing over wine and bread, and a festive meal.
rel|16|l31-5|9-10|What Counts as Rest — The Torah forbids work on the Sabbath, and the rabbis defined work as thirty-nine categories of creative labor, which shapes what observant Jews do and avoid.
rel|16|l31-6|9-10|Synagogue, Torah and Havdalah — Sabbath worship centers on reading the Torah in the synagogue, and the day ends on Saturday night with the Havdalah ceremony.
rel|16|l31-7|9-10|A Calendar of Moon and Sun — The Jewish calendar follows the moon for months and the sun for years, adding a leap month seven times in nineteen years so holidays stay in their seasons.
rel|16|l31-8|9-10|The Days of Awe — Rosh Hashanah and Yom Kippur open the Jewish year with ten days of self-examination, repentance and a fast that Jewish tradition treats as the holiest day.
rel|16|l31-9|9-10|Sukkot, Hanukkah and Purim — Sukkot recalls the wilderness, Hanukkah the rededication of the Temple in 164 BCE, and Purim the rescue told in the Book of Esther.
rel|16|l31-10|9-10|Passover and Shavuot — Passover retells the exodus from Egypt at a home meal called the seder, and Shavuot, seven weeks later, celebrates the giving of the Torah at Sinai.
rel|16|l31-11|9-10|Keeping Kosher — Kashrut, the Jewish dietary law, comes from the Torah's rules about which animals may be eaten and how, and Jews differ in how strictly they keep it.
rel|16|l31-12|9-10|Birth and Coming of Age — A Jewish boy enters the covenant through circumcision on the eighth day, and at twelve or thirteen a Jewish child becomes a bat or bar mitzvah, responsible for the commandments.
rel|16|l31-13|9-10|Marriage and Mourning — A Jewish wedding takes place under a canopy with a written contract and a broken glass, and Jewish mourning moves through set stages of seven days, thirty days and a year.
rel|16|l32-1|9-10|The Second Temple Falls — In 70 CE Roman armies destroyed the Second Temple in Jerusalem, ending sacrifice and forcing Jewish life to find a new center.
rel|16|l32-2|9-10|From Altar to Prayer — After 70 CE, rabbis at Yavneh rebuilt Jewish life around prayer, study and acts of kindness in place of Temple sacrifice.
rel|16|l32-3|9-10|The Mishnah: Oral Torah in Writing — Around 200 CE, Rabbi Judah the Prince gathered the Oral Torah into the Mishnah, a Hebrew law code in six orders.
rel|16|l32-4|9-10|What the Talmud Is — The Talmud joins the Mishnah to centuries of rabbinic discussion called the Gemara, and it became the central text of Jewish study and law.
rel|16|l32-5|9-10|Hillel, Shammai and Holy Argument — The Talmud preserves disagreement on purpose, and the debates of Hillel and Shammai show that argument for the sake of heaven is itself honored.
rel|16|l32-6|9-10|Commentaries, Codes and Print — Later scholars such as Rashi and Maimonides explained the Talmud and organized its law, and printing fixed the page students still use.
rel|16|l32-7|9-10|Sepharad and Ashkenaz — Jews under Islamic rule and in Christian Europe formed two great cultural families, Sephardi and Ashkenazi, with different languages and customs.
rel|16|l32-8|9-10|Expulsions, Ghettos and the Shtetl — In medieval Christian Europe, Jews faced violence, false accusations and expulsions, and many moved east to Poland, where Jewish life grew and Hasidism began.
rel|16|l32-9|9-10|Citizenship and the Movements — As European Jews gained citizenship in the 1700s and 1800s, they answered modern life differently, forming the Reform, Orthodox, Conservative and Reconstructionist movements.
rel|16|l32-10|9-10|The Holocaust — Between 1933 and 1945 Nazi Germany and its collaborators carried out the Holocaust, the systematic murder of about six million Jews, which Jews also call the Shoah.
rel|16|l32-11|9-10|Israel and the Diaspora — Modern Zionism led to the founding of Israel in 1948; today nearly half of the world's Jews live there and the rest in the diaspora, with a wide range of views.
rel|16|l32-12|9-10|Jewish Chicago — Since the 1840s Jewish immigrants have built congregations, markets and institutions in Chicago, moving from the Near West Side to the North Side and suburbs.
rel|17|l33-1|9-10|Arabia Before Islam — Seventh-century Arabia was a land of trading towns and tribes, with many gods, a famous shrine at Mecca, and neighbors who were Jewish and Christian.
rel|17|l33-2|9-10|The First Revelation — Muslims believe that in about 610 the angel Gabriel brought Muhammad the first words of the Qur'an in a cave near Mecca, beginning twenty-three years of revelation.
rel|17|l33-3|9-10|Mecca, Medina and the Hijra — Rejected in Mecca, Muhammad and his followers moved to Medina in 622, the hijra that starts the Muslim calendar, and built the first Muslim community there.
rel|17|l33-4|9-10|How the Qur'an Is Arranged — The Qur'an has 114 suras arranged roughly from longest to shortest, not in the order revealed, and Muslims believe its Arabic text is God's own speech.
rel|17|l33-5|9-10|Recitation and Translation — Muslims experience the Qur'an first as sound recited in Arabic; translations are considered interpretations of its meaning, not the Qur'an itself.
rel|17|l33-6|9-10|Tawhid: The Oneness of God — Tawhid, the absolute oneness of God, is the center of Islamic belief, and it shapes what Muslims say about prophets, scripture, angels and judgment.
rel|17|l33-7|9-10|Shahada and Salat — The first two pillars are the declaration of faith, which makes a person a Muslim, and the five daily prayers that structure every day.
rel|17|l33-8|9-10|Zakat and the Ramadan Fast — Zakat, a yearly share of wealth given to those in need, and sawm, the dawn-to-sunset fast of Ramadan, turn belief into discipline and care for others.
rel|17|l33-9|9-10|The Hajj — The hajj, the pilgrimage to Mecca required once in a lifetime for those who are able, gathers millions of Muslims to retrace the steps of Abraham, Hagar and Muhammad.
rel|17|l33-10|9-10|The Two Eids — Islam has two major festivals: Eid al-Fitr, which ends Ramadan, and Eid al-Adha, which marks the sacrifice of the hajj; both center on prayer, giving and shared meals.
rel|17|l33-11|9-10|Inside a Mosque — A mosque is a place of prostration, arranged around the direction of Mecca, without images of God or people, and led in prayer by an imam.
rel|17|l33-12|9-10|Halal: What Muslims Eat — Halal means permitted; Islamic food rules forbid pork, blood, alcohol and improperly slaughtered meat, and they shape shopping, cooking and eating out.
rel|17|l34-1|9-10|Hadith and Sunnah — The Sunnah is the Prophet's example, known through hadith reports that scholars collected and tested, and it is the second source of Islamic guidance after the Qur'an.
rel|17|l34-2|9-10|Sharia and Fiqh — Sharia is the divine guidance Muslims believe God has given; fiqh is the human effort of scholars to understand it, and it covers worship and daily life far more than courts.
rel|17|l34-3|9-10|The Schools of Law — Sunni Muslims recognize four main schools of law and most Shia follow the Ja'fari school; each is a respected tradition of reasoning, not a separate religion.
rel|17|l34-4|9-10|The Question of Succession — When Muhammad died in 632, Muslims disagreed about who should lead; Sunnis accept the chosen caliphs, while Shia believe leadership belonged to Ali and the Prophet's family.
rel|17|l34-5|9-10|Karbala and Shia Devotion — The death of Ali's son Husayn at Karbala in 680 became the central story of Shia memory, relived each year on Ashura.
rel|17|l34-6|9-10|Sufism: The Inner Path — Sufism is the mystical side of Islam, in which Muslims seek direct experience of God's love through remembrance, poetry and a master's guidance.
rel|17|l34-7|9-10|Baghdad and the House of Wisdom — In Abbasid Baghdad, scholars of many faiths translated Greek, Persian and Indian works into Arabic, preserving them and building on them.
rel|17|l34-8|9-10|Algebra, Stars and Medicine — Scholars writing in Arabic advanced mathematics, astronomy, optics and medicine, and words like algebra and algorithm still carry their traces.
rel|17|l34-9|9-10|Córdoba and Timbuktu — Learning flourished far from Baghdad, in al-Andalus in Spain and in the West African city of Timbuktu, where manuscripts are still kept.
rel|17|l34-10|9-10|The Art of the Written Word — Because Muslims revere the Qur'an as God's word, calligraphy became the most honored Islamic art, joined by geometric pattern and the arabesque.
rel|17|l34-11|9-10|Muslims in America and Chicago — Islam has been part of American history since enslaved West Africans arrived, and Chicago today is home to one of the country's largest and most diverse Muslim communities.
rel|18|l35-1|9-10|One Name, Many Paths — Hinduism is not one organization with one founder; it is a family of related traditions that grew in South Asia over thousands of years.
rel|18|l35-2|9-10|The Vedas: Heard and Remembered — The Vedas are the oldest Hindu scriptures, hymns and rituals in Sanskrit that were memorized and passed down by mouth for centuries before they were written.
rel|18|l35-3|9-10|The Upanishads: Brahman and Atman — The Upanishads teach that behind the changing world is one ultimate reality, Brahman, and that the deepest self, atman, is one with it.
rel|18|l35-4|9-10|Samsara and Karma — Many Hindus believe the soul is reborn again and again in samsara, and that karma, the result of one's actions, shapes each new life.
rel|18|l35-5|9-10|Dharma: Duty and Right Order — Dharma is the right way of living, the duty and moral order that fit a person's role, stage of life and community.
rel|18|l35-6|9-10|Moksha: Release from the Wheel — Moksha is the final goal in much Hindu thought: freedom from the cycle of rebirth, understood in different ways by different schools.
rel|18|l35-7|9-10|Vishnu and His Avatars — Vishnu, the preserver, is worshiped by many Hindus as the supreme God who descends to earth in forms called avatars, including Rama and Krishna.
rel|18|l35-8|9-10|Shiva, Devi and Ganesha — Many Hindus worship Shiva or the Goddess, Devi, as supreme, and nearly all honor Ganesha as the remover of obstacles.
rel|18|l35-9|9-10|Four Paths to the Goal — Hindu teachers describe several paths, or yogas, toward moksha: knowledge, devotion, selfless action and meditation.
rel|18|l35-10|9-10|Puja at Home and in the Temple — Puja is Hindu worship through offerings of light, flowers, food and prayer, performed at home shrines and in temples.
rel|18|l35-11|9-10|Diwali and Holi — Diwali, the festival of lights, and Holi, the spring festival of colors, are among the most widely celebrated Hindu holidays, each with several stories behind it.
rel|18|l35-12|9-10|Caste: History and Reform — Caste is a system of inherited social groups in South Asia; Hindus and reformers have long debated it, and India's Constitution, in force from 1950, outlawed untouchability.
rel|18|l36-1|9-10|The Mahabharata in Outline — The Mahabharata is a vast Sanskrit epic about a family war; the Bhagavad Gita is one short part of it.
rel|18|l36-2|9-10|The Ramayana in Outline — The Ramayana tells of Prince Rama, his exile and his rescue of Sita, and it presents a model of dharma that millions of Hindus love.
rel|18|l36-3|9-10|Epics as Living Stories — Hindus meet the epics less often by reading them than through performance, festival, song, film and television.
rel|18|l36-4|9-10|The Frame of the Gita — The Gita is told as a report to a blind king, and it opens with Arjuna's collapse as he sees his own family on the other side.
rel|18|l36-5|9-10|The Self That Cannot Be Slain — Krishna first answers Arjuna's grief by teaching that the true self, the atman, is eternal and is not destroyed when the body dies.
rel|18|l36-6|9-10|Action Without Attachment — The Gita's most famous teaching is to act according to one's duty while letting go of attachment to the results.
rel|18|l36-7|9-10|Devotion and the Vision of God — In the Gita's later chapters Krishna reveals himself as the supreme God and teaches that loving devotion, bhakti, is the surest path.
rel|18|l36-8|9-10|The Person of Steady Wisdom — The Gita describes an ideal person whose mind stays calm and steady, free from craving, fear and anger, while still acting in the world.
rel|18|l36-9|9-10|Gandhi and the Gita — Mohandas Gandhi called the Gita his guide and read its battlefield as the inner struggle of every person, drawing from it a teaching of selfless, nonviolent action.
rel|18|l36-10|9-10|Many Readers, Many Readings — For more than a thousand years, teachers, translators and writers in India and the West have read the Gita in strikingly different ways.
rel|18|l36-11|9-10|Yoga: Roots and Modern Forms — Yoga began as a set of Indian spiritual disciplines aimed at liberation; modern yoga, focused on postures and health, grew from those roots in the last 150 years.
rel|19|l37-1|11-12|Siddhartha in History and Tradition — Buddhists trace their tradition to Siddhartha Gautama, a teacher in northern India around the fifth century BCE, whose life is known mostly through later Buddhist texts.
rel|19|l37-2|11-12|Renunciation and the Middle Way — After years of extreme self-denial nearly killed him, Siddhartha chose a Middle Way between luxury and self-torture, and Buddhists see that choice as the foundation of the path.
rel|19|l37-3|11-12|Awakening Under the Bodhi Tree — Buddhists believe Siddhartha reached full awakening while meditating at Bodh Gaya, and that he then taught for forty-five years before his death.
rel|19|l37-4|11-12|The First Truth: Dukkha — The Buddha's first truth is that ordinary life is marked by dukkha, a word that covers pain, stress and the unreliability of everything we lean on.
rel|19|l37-5|11-12|Craving and Its Ending — The second and third truths teach that suffering arises from craving and that when craving ceases, suffering ceases, a state Buddhists call nirvana.
rel|19|l37-6|11-12|The Eightfold Path — The fourth truth is the Eightfold Path, eight linked practices that Buddhists group under wisdom, ethical conduct and mental discipline.
rel|19|l37-7|11-12|Impermanence, Suffering, Non-Self — Buddhists teach that all conditioned things share three marks: they are impermanent, unsatisfactory and without a fixed self.
rel|19|l37-8|11-12|Buddha, Dharma, Sangha — Buddhists express commitment by taking refuge in the Three Jewels, the Buddha, the dharma and the sangha, and lay Buddhists undertake five precepts.
rel|19|l37-9|11-12|Meditation and Mindfulness — Buddhist meditation trains the mind through calm and insight, and its mindfulness practices have also spread into secular medicine and schools.
rel|19|l37-10|11-12|Reading the Dhammapada — The Dhammapada, a collection of 423 Pali verses, is among the most widely read Buddhist texts, and Müller's 1881 translation made it available in English.
rel|19|l38-1|11-12|Life in the Monastery — The sangha of monks and nuns lives by the monastic rules of the Vinaya and depends on laypeople, who give food and support in return for teaching.
rel|19|l38-2|11-12|Ashoka's Change of Heart — The Mauryan emperor Ashoka turned to Buddhist teaching after a bloody war and promoted the dharma across his empire and beyond it.
rel|19|l38-3|11-12|Routes Out of India — Buddhism traveled out of India by sea to Sri Lanka and Southeast Asia and by the Silk Road to China, and from there to Korea, Japan and Tibet.
rel|19|l38-4|11-12|The Pali Canon in Sri Lanka — Theravada Buddhists preserve the Pali Canon, first written down in Sri Lanka, and hold up the arhat who reaches nirvana as the goal of the path.
rel|19|l38-5|11-12|Theravada in Southeast Asia — In Thailand, Myanmar, Cambodia and Laos, Theravada shapes daily life through temples, temporary ordination and festivals such as Vesak.
rel|19|l38-6|11-12|The Bodhisattva Path — Mahayana Buddhists hold up the bodhisattva, who seeks awakening in order to free all beings, and teach that all things are empty of fixed existence.
rel|19|l38-7|11-12|Pure Land and Amida — Pure Land Buddhists trust in the vow of Amitabha Buddha and call on his name, hoping for rebirth in his Pure Land, where awakening is assured.
rel|19|l38-8|11-12|Zen in Japan — Zen, which grew from Chinese Chan, emphasizes seated meditation and direct insight, and it shaped Japanese arts from gardens to tea.
rel|19|l38-9|11-12|Vajrayana and the Dalai Lama — Vajrayana Buddhism, rooted in Tibet, adds mantras, mandalas and teacher lineages to Mahayana, and its best-known figure is the Dalai Lama.
rel|19|l38-10|11-12|Buddhism Meets Confucius and Laozi — In China, Buddhism met Confucian family values and Daoist ideas, and over centuries the three were described as three teachings that could live side by side.
rel|19|l38-11|11-12|Buddhism in America and Chicago — Buddhism came to America through Asian immigrants and Western converts, was introduced at Chicago's 1893 Parliament, and today lives in temples across the Chicago area.
rel|2|l3-1|k-2|A Story Told Again — Some stories are told again and again for a very long time.
rel|2|l3-2|k-2|Saying Whose Story It Is — We say which group tells a story.
rel|2|l3-3|k-2|Noah and the Big Boat — Jews, Christians and Muslims all tell a story about Noah.
rel|2|l3-4|k-2|The Prince Who Looked Outside — Buddhists tell of a prince who saw that people grow old and get sick.
rel|2|l3-5|k-2|Lamps for Rama — Hindus tell how lamps welcomed Rama home.
rel|2|l3-6|k-2|Guru Nanak Shares Food — Sikhs tell how Guru Nanak shared food with people who were hungry.
rel|2|l4-1|k-2|Four Special Books — Many religions have a book that they keep with great care.
rel|2|l4-2|k-2|A Scroll and a Book — A Torah is a long scroll; the others are books with pages.
rel|2|l4-3|k-2|Clean Hands and Covers — People wash, cover and hold their holy books with care.
rel|2|l4-4|k-2|Kept High and Safe — People keep holy books up high, never on the floor.
rel|2|l4-5|k-2|Words Out Loud — People read and sing their holy books out loud.
rel|2|l4-6|k-2|Special to Some, Open to All — A book can be special to one family and still interesting to everyone.
rel|20|l39-1|11-12|The Zhou and the Mandate of Heaven — Confucius looked back to the early Zhou kings, whose right to rule rested on the idea that Heaven gives its mandate to the virtuous and takes it from the cruel.
rel|20|l39-2|11-12|A World Coming Apart — Confucius taught in the Spring and Autumn period, when Zhou authority had collapsed and rival thinkers competed to explain how order could be restored.
rel|20|l39-3|11-12|Kong Qiu of Lu — Confucius (551–479 BCE) was a teacher from the state of Lu whose ideas outlived his failure to find a ruler who would put them into practice.
rel|20|l39-4|11-12|A Book of Sayings — The Analects is a collection of short sayings and dialogues compiled by Confucius's followers over generations, so it must be read as a record of conversations, not a treatise.
rel|20|l39-5|11-12|Ren: The Humane Heart — Ren, translated humaneness or benevolence, is the highest virtue in the Analects: care for other people as the root from which all other goodness grows.
rel|20|l39-6|11-12|Li: Ritual and Propriety — Li, meaning ritual, ceremony and the courtesies of daily life, is how ren takes visible form in the Confucian view, and how a society holds together without force.
rel|20|l39-7|11-12|Xiao and the Five Relationships — Filial piety, xiao, is the first school of virtue in Confucian thought, and it opens into five relationships that order all of society.
rel|20|l39-8|11-12|Becoming a Junzi — The junzi, once a word for a nobleman by birth, became in the Analects the person of cultivated character that anyone might become through learning.
rel|20|l39-9|11-12|Mencius, Xunzi and Human Nature — After Confucius, Mencius argued that human nature is good and Xunzi that it must be shaped by effort, and the tradition carried both voices forward.
rel|20|l39-10|11-12|Scholars, Examinations and the State — From the Han dynasty until 1905, Confucian texts were the curriculum of government, tested by civil-service examinations that shaped Chinese society.
rel|20|l39-11|11-12|Ancestors and the Qingming Festival — Ancestor veneration, caring for the dead as continuing members of the family, is the practice where Confucian ethics most clearly looks like religion.
rel|20|l39-12|11-12|Philosophy, Ethics or Religion? — Whether Confucianism is a religion depends on what the word religion is taken to mean, and people in East Asia have answered in more than one way.
rel|20|l40-1|11-12|Laozi and the Gatekeeper — Tradition says Laozi wrote the Daodejing, but scholars see it as a collection that took shape over time and was circulating by about 300 BCE.
rel|20|l40-2|11-12|Reading a Book of Paradox — The Daodejing is a short book of 81 poetic chapters that teaches through paradox, so every translation of it, including Legge's, is an interpretation.
rel|20|l40-3|11-12|The Dao Beyond Names — In the Daodejing the Dao is the nameless source and pattern of all things, and de is the power a thing has when it follows its own Way.
rel|20|l40-4|11-12|Wu Wei: Acting Without Forcing — Wu wei, action without forcing, is the Daodejing's ideal for the sage and the ruler: to act in step with the natural course of things rather than against it.
rel|20|l40-5|11-12|The Lesson of Water — The Daodejing uses water as its favorite image of the Dao: soft, low and yielding, yet able to wear away the hardest things.
rel|20|l40-6|11-12|Yin and Yang — Yin and yang are paired, complementary forces whose balance and constant change Chinese thought uses to describe everything that happens.
rel|20|l40-7|11-12|The Butterfly and the Cook — The Zhuangzi teaches Daoist ideas through humorous and strange stories that question our certainties and show skill flowing from the Way.
rel|20|l40-8|11-12|Death, Usefulness and Freedom — The Zhuangzi treats death as part of nature's transformations and praises the freedom of those who stop chasing usefulness, status and fixed opinions.
rel|20|l40-9|11-12|Celestial Masters and Daoist Priests — From the second century CE, Daoism also became an organized religion with revealed scriptures, ordained priests, temples, monasteries and a vast canon.
rel|20|l40-10|11-12|Immortals, Qi and Inner Alchemy — Many Daoists have sought long life and transcendence, first through elixirs and later through inner alchemy, the refining of the body's own energies.
rel|20|l40-11|11-12|The Three Teachings and Popular Religion — In Chinese life Daoism, Confucianism and Buddhism have long mixed with local gods and ancestor rites, so most people drew on all three rather than joining one.
rel|20|l40-12|11-12|The Way in Brush, Body and Medicine — Daoist ideas of qi, yin and yang and harmony with nature shaped Chinese landscape painting, traditional medicine and martial arts such as tai chi.
rel|21|l41-1|11-12|Nanak's Journey and Message — Sikh tradition begins with Guru Nanak, who taught devotion to one God and rejected divisions between people.
rel|21|l41-2|11-12|Ten Gurus, One Light — Sikhs believe the same divine light passed through ten human Gurus, from Nanak in 1469 to Gobind Singh, who died in 1708.
rel|21|l41-3|11-12|The Scripture That Is the Guru — Sikhs treat the Guru Granth Sahib as their living Guru, a book of hymns that is honored, sung and consulted rather than simply read.
rel|21|l41-4|11-12|Ik Onkar and the Three Duties — Sikh teaching centers on remembering the one God while working honestly and sharing with others.
rel|21|l41-5|11-12|Seva and the Langar — Sikhs express the equality of all people through seva, selfless service, most visibly in the free langar meal.
rel|21|l41-6|11-12|The Khalsa and the Five Ks — In 1699 Guru Gobind Singh founded the Khalsa, whose initiated members wear five visible signs of their commitment.
rel|21|l41-7|11-12|Inside a Gurdwara — A gurdwara is any place where the Guru Granth Sahib is installed, and its layout teaches openness and equality.
rel|21|l41-8|11-12|Sikhs in America and Chicago — Sikhs have lived in the United States for over a century, and the Chicago area has a large and active Sikh community.
rel|21|l41-9|11-12|Mahavira, the Conqueror — Jains follow the teaching of Mahavira, the last of twenty-four Tirthankaras, who taught a path of self-conquest and non-harm.
rel|21|l41-10|11-12|Ahimsa and the Five Vows — Jain life is organized around ahimsa, non-harm toward every living being, expressed in five vows kept strictly by monastics and more moderately by laypeople.
rel|21|l41-11|11-12|Karma as Matter, and Many Viewpoints — Jains understand karma as fine matter that clings to the soul, and they teach that no single viewpoint captures the whole truth.
rel|21|l41-12|11-12|Jains Today and Gandhi's Debt — Jains are a small community with wide influence, including on Gandhi's practice of non-violence and on a Chicago suburb's temple.
rel|21|l42-1|11-12|Story, Song and Ceremony as Scripture — In many Indigenous traditions, sacred knowledge is carried by story, song, ceremony and land rather than by one written book.
rel|21|l42-2|11-12|Respect, Protocol and Restricted Knowledge — Some sacred knowledge belongs to particular people, seasons or ceremonies, and outsiders who study it are expected to follow the community's protocol.
rel|21|l42-3|11-12|Olodumare and the Orisha — Yoruba tradition teaches of a supreme God, Olodumare, and of many orisha, divine powers who are closely involved in human life.
rel|21|l42-4|11-12|Ancestors in the Community — Many African traditions, including the Yoruba, honor ancestors as members of the community who continue to guide and protect the living.
rel|21|l42-5|11-12|Ifa: Divination and Memory — In Ifa divination, a trained priest reads patterns and recites memorized verses that guide a person facing a decision.
rel|21|l42-6|11-12|Faith Carried Across the Atlantic — Enslaved Africans carried their religious traditions to the Americas, where they survived by adapting under harsh conditions.
rel|21|l42-7|11-12|Santeria in Cuba and the United States — Santeria, also called Regla de Ocha or Lucumi, is a Cuban tradition of orisha devotion that won an important religious freedom case in 1993.
rel|21|l42-8|11-12|Candomble in Brazil — Candomble is an Afro-Brazilian tradition centered in Bahia, where priestesses lead houses devoted to the orixas.
rel|21|l42-9|11-12|Many Nations, Many Creation Stories — Native American nations each have their own creation stories, and many connect the sacred to specific lands and seasons.
rel|21|l42-10|11-12|The Anishinaabe of the Great Lakes — The Ojibwe, Odawa and Potawatomi, the Anishinaabe peoples of the Great Lakes, lived in the Chicago area and remain part of the region today.
rel|21|l42-11|11-12|Ceremony and Gathering — Native ceremonies such as the sweat lodge and the Sun Dance are sacred and often closed, while powwows are public gatherings that welcome visitors.
rel|21|l42-12|11-12|Suppression, Survival and Revival — U.S. policy suppressed Native religions for decades until laws such as the American Indian Religious Freedom Act of 1978 began to protect them.
rel|22|l43-1|11-12|Hammurabi's Stone and Sinai's Law — Ancient Near Eastern law codes and the Torah's laws share forms and topics, but the Torah presents its law as the terms of a covenant with God.
rel|22|l43-2|11-12|Sharia and the Schools of Law — Sharia is what Muslims understand as God's guidance for life; fiqh is the human scholarship that works it out, and that scholarship has several schools.
rel|22|l43-3|11-12|Canon Law, Halakha and the First Amendment — Religious communities keep their own internal law, such as canon law and halakha, while the U.S. Constitution keeps government from establishing or restricting religion.
rel|22|l43-4|11-12|Buildings That Teach — Sacred architecture puts a tradition's beliefs into stone: which way you face, where the light falls and who may enter all carry meaning.
rel|22|l43-5|11-12|Images, Icons and Empty Space — Traditions disagree about whether the sacred may be pictured, and their art, from icons to calligraphy to empty niches, shows the answer each one gives.
rel|22|l43-6|11-12|Chant, Recitation and Song — In most traditions the sacred text is heard as much as read, and sound carries it: chant, recitation, kirtan and gospel song.
rel|22|l43-7|11-12|Observatories and Scriptoria — For centuries religious needs and religious institutions drove much of the world's science, from Islamic astronomy and algebra to monastic and university scholarship in Europe.
rel|22|l43-8|11-12|Galileo and the Church — The Galileo affair of 1616 to 1633 was a conflict over who may interpret Scripture and nature, not a simple war between religion and science.
rel|22|l43-9|11-12|Darwin and the Range of Responses — Religious responses to Darwin's theory of evolution have ranged from rejection to full acceptance, and that range exists inside every major tradition.
rel|22|l43-10|11-12|Zakat and Tzedakah — In Islam and Judaism, giving to the poor is an obligation of justice rather than an optional kindness: zakat and tzedakah.
rel|22|l43-11|11-12|Charity, Seva and Dana — Christianity, Sikhism, Hinduism, Buddhism and Confucianism each teach giving and service in their own terms: charity, seva, dana and ren.
rel|22|l43-12|11-12|Hospitals, Schools and Relief — Many of the world's hospitals, schools and relief agencies began as religious institutions, and many still are, serving people of every faith and none.
rel|22|l44-1|11-12|The Crusades — The Crusades of 1095 to 1291 were wars that Latin Christians fought as religious acts of penance, and they are remembered very differently by Christians, Muslims and Jews.
rel|22|l44-2|11-12|Wars of Religion in Europe — After the Reformation, Catholic and Protestant rulers fought for more than a century, and the exhausting Thirty Years' War helped push Europe toward toleration.
rel|22|l44-3|11-12|The Partition of India — In 1947 British India was divided into India and Pakistan along religious lines, and the partition brought mass migration and killing among Hindus, Muslims and Sikhs.
rel|22|l44-4|11-12|Northern Ireland and the Question of Cause — In Northern Ireland religion marked two communities, but scholars describe the Troubles mainly as a conflict over national identity, rights and power.
rel|22|l44-5|11-12|Gandhi and Truth-Force — Gandhi built a method of nonviolent resistance, satyagraha, from Hindu and Jain ideas of ahimsa, the Bhagavad Gita and the Sermon on the Mount.
rel|22|l44-6|11-12|King and the Beloved Community — Martin Luther King Jr. joined the Bible's prophets and the Sermon on the Mount with Gandhi's method to lead a nonviolent movement for civil rights.
rel|22|l44-7|11-12|Truth, Reconciliation and Interfaith Peacemakers — Desmond Tutu in South Africa and interfaith movements such as Liberia's women's peace campaign drew on religious teaching to end violence and heal societies.
rel|22|l44-8|11-12|Two Clauses, One Freedom — The First Amendment protects religious freedom in two ways: the Establishment Clause keeps government from sponsoring religion, and the Free Exercise Clause keeps it from restricting religion.
rel|22|l44-9|11-12|Free Exercise in the Courts — Supreme Court cases brought by Jehovah's Witnesses, the Amish, Native Americans and others have shaped how far government must make room for religious practice.
rel|22|l44-10|11-12|Teaching About Religion in Public Schools — Public schools may not lead or sponsor worship, but they may teach about religion, and students keep the right to practice their own faith.
rel|22|l44-11|11-12|Persecution in Our Time — Religious persecution continues today against many communities, and international law names freedom of religion as a human right.
rel|22|l44-12|11-12|Chicago's Parliament and Interfaith City — The 1893 World's Parliament of Religions in Chicago began the modern interfaith movement, and the city remains a center of interfaith life.
rel|23|l45-1|11-12|Two Questions, Not One — A good life asks what a person should become; justice asks what people owe one another, and every tradition answers both.
rel|23|l45-2|11-12|Comparing Without Ranking — Fair comparison describes each tradition in its own terms, attributes every claim, shows variety within the tradition, and refuses to declare a winner.
rel|23|l45-3|11-12|The Torah's Neighbor — In Jewish tradition the good life is a life of mitzvot, and justice begins with the command to love the neighbor and protect the stranger, the widow and the poor.
rel|23|l45-4|11-12|The Prophets Demand Justice — Amos, Micah and Isaiah taught that worship without justice for the poor is worthless, giving Judaism and Christianity a shared language for social ethics.
rel|23|l45-5|11-12|The Sermon on the Mount — In Matthew 5–7 Jesus blesses the poor and the merciful, commands love of enemies, and gives the Golden Rule, which Christians read as the shape of life in the Kingdom of God.
rel|23|l45-6|11-12|Justice and Mercy in the Qur'an — The Qur'an commands justice even against oneself and one's kin, pairs it with God's mercy, and makes care for the poor a pillar of the good life.
rel|23|l45-7|11-12|Dharma and the Gita's Answer — Hindu traditions describe the good life as living one's dharma, and the Gita teaches that right action is done as duty and offering, without grasping at its reward.
rel|23|l45-8|11-12|The Eightfold Path and Compassion — Buddhists describe the good life as walking the Eightfold Path, whose ethical steps are right speech, right action and right livelihood, and whose fruit is compassion for all beings.
rel|23|l45-9|11-12|Ren and the Dao — Confucius taught that a good life is humaneness practiced through ritual and family; the Daodejing taught that it is living simply in harmony with the Way, like water.
rel|23|l45-10|11-12|Seva and the Shared Meal — Sikh teaching answers both questions at once: a good life is honest work, remembrance of God and sharing, and justice is practiced daily in selfless service and the langar.
rel|23|l45-11|11-12|Ethics Without Scripture — Secular ethics answers the same questions by reason alone, through virtue, duty or consequences, and belongs in the comparison beside the religious answers.
rel|23|l45-12|11-12|Where the Answers Meet and Part — The traditions converge on care for the vulnerable and a version of the Golden Rule, but they differ on the source of the rule, the nature of the self, and how justice relates to God.
rel|23|l46-1|11-12|Judaism: This World and the World to Come — The Hebrew Bible focuses on life in this world, while later Jewish tradition taught resurrection and the world to come, and Jewish movements still differ on how to understand them.
rel|23|l46-2|11-12|Christianity: The Empty Tomb — Christians believe that Jesus rose from the dead and that his resurrection promises the resurrection of all, followed by judgment and eternal life, though churches differ on the details.
rel|23|l46-3|11-12|Islam: The Day of Judgment — Muslims believe that after death each soul waits in the barzakh until the Day of Judgment, when all are raised, their deeds weighed, and they enter paradise or hell.
rel|23|l46-4|11-12|Hinduism: The Soul Changes Clothes — Many Hindus believe the atman passes through many lives in samsara, shaped by karma, until it reaches moksha, release from rebirth.
rel|23|l46-5|11-12|Buddhism: Rebirth Without a Soul — Buddhists teach rebirth driven by karma but deny a permanent self that passes from life to life, and the goal is nirvana, the ending of the cycle.
rel|23|l46-6|11-12|Jainism and Sikhism: Two Paths to Release — Jains teach that the soul frees itself from karmic matter through strict nonviolence, while Sikhs teach that liberation comes through God's grace and remembering God's name.
rel|23|l46-7|11-12|The Ancestors Remain — In many African and Chinese traditions the dead become ancestors who stay connected to the living, who honor them and ask for their help.
rel|23|l46-8|11-12|Job and the Whirlwind — The Book of Job rejects the idea that suffering is always punishment, and God's answer from the whirlwind points to the limits of human understanding.
rel|23|l46-9|11-12|The Cross and the Test — Christians believe God entered suffering in Jesus on the cross, while the Qur'an teaches that hardship is a test met with patience and trust in God.
rel|23|l46-10|11-12|The First Truth and Karma — Buddhism begins by naming suffering as a fact of life, and karma explains much suffering in Indian traditions, though teachers warn against using it to blame the suffering.
rel|23|l46-11|11-12|Mourning Practices Compared — Each tradition gives mourners a structure of time, words and actions, and those structures reflect what it believes about death.
rel|23|l46-12|11-12|Secular and Humanist Answers — Secular thinkers and humanists generally hold that death is the end of the person and that meaning is found in this life, in love, memory and the good one does.
rel|24|l47-1|11-12|What Makes a Question Researchable — A good capstone question is open, comparable across traditions, and answerable from primary texts.
rel|24|l47-2|11-12|Narrowing a Topic to a Question — Shrink a topic step by step until the question names one issue that three traditions have addressed in texts.
rel|24|l47-3|11-12|Choosing Three Traditions — Pick three traditions that have each spoken to your question in their texts, and choose for range rather than for convenience.
rel|24|l47-4|11-12|Primary, Secondary and Tertiary Sources — A primary source is the tradition's own text; a secondary source explains it; a tertiary source summarizes the explanations.
rel|24|l47-5|11-12|Public-Domain Translations and Their Limits — Public-domain translations can be quoted freely, but each carries the language and assumptions of its own century.
rel|24|l47-6|11-12|Where Reliable Texts Live — Find primary texts through libraries, universities, museums and the traditions' own publishers, and judge every website by who made it and why.
rel|24|l47-7|11-12|Author, Audience and Purpose Again — Before a verse can count as evidence, ask who is speaking in it, to whom, and for what purpose.
rel|24|l47-8|11-12|Genre Changes the Meaning — Law, story, poetry, prophecy, letter and teaching each ask to be read differently, and a paper should say which kind of text it is quoting.
rel|24|l47-9|11-12|How Adherents Read It — A text means what its community has understood it to mean, so report how adherents read a passage, not only what the words say.
rel|24|l47-10|11-12|How to Cite a Sacred Text — Each scripture has its own citation system, and a paper must use the right one so a reader can find the passage.
rel|24|l47-11|11-12|A Note System That Works — Take notes in three columns, the text, its context, and how adherents read it, tagged by tradition and tied to your question.
rel|24|l47-12|11-12|Avoiding Stereotype and Overgeneralization — Write about traditions with precise qualifiers, name the branch or school you mean, and never let one text or one adherent stand for everyone.
rel|24|l48-1|11-12|What a Thesis Claims — A capstone thesis states what each tradition would answer, names the reason each gives, and makes a comparison a reader could test against the texts.
rel|24|l48-2|11-12|Evidence in Three Layers — Strong evidence has three layers, the quoted text, its context and how adherents have read it, joined to the claim by a sentence of reasoning.
rel|24|l48-3|11-12|Diversity Within a Tradition — Every tradition speaks with many voices, so a strong paper names the branch or school it means and reports where members disagree.
rel|24|l48-4|11-12|Comparing Without Ranking — Comparison sets traditions side by side to show where they agree and differ; ranking judges which is best, and the capstone does only the first.
rel|24|l48-5|11-12|Respectful Language — Use each tradition's own names and terms, attribute every belief, and replace loaded words with accurate, neutral ones.
rel|24|l48-6|11-12|Handling Difficult Texts — When a text speaks of war, punishment or unequal roles, quote it accurately, give its context, and report how adherents have read it, without attacking or excusing.
rel|24|l48-7|11-12|Building the Written Essay — The essay moves from the question to the thesis, gives each tradition its own section of layered evidence, then compares them and names the limits of the study.
rel|24|l48-8|11-12|The Oral Presentation — A strong presentation states the thesis early, reads one key text per tradition accurately with its citation, and treats sacred words and objects with care.
rel|24|l48-9|11-12|Answering Questions — Answer audience questions by returning to the texts, naming diversity within the tradition, and saying plainly when you do not know.
rel|24|l48-10|11-12|Reading the Rubric — The rubric weights evidence most heavily, and it rewards fairness, attribution and diversity within traditions as much as it rewards organization.
rel|24|l48-11|11-12|Peer Review Against the Rubric — A peer reviewer checks a partner's draft line by line against the rubric and the texts, and gives specific, kind, usable comments.
rel|24|l48-12|11-12|What the Study of Religion Is For — The academic study of religion helps people in a diverse society read carefully, understand their neighbors and disagree fairly, without asking anyone to believe or disbelieve.
rel|3|l5-1|k-2|The Golden Rule — The Golden Rule says: treat others the way you want to be treated.
rel|3|l5-2|k-2|Jesus and Hillel — Jesus and Hillel were Jewish teachers who taught the Golden Rule long ago.
rel|3|l5-3|k-2|The Prophet Muhammad — Muslims remember that the Prophet Muhammad taught to want good for others.
rel|3|l5-4|k-2|Confucius and the Buddha — Confucius and the Buddha also taught people not to hurt others.
rel|3|l5-5|k-2|Giving to People in Need — Many religions teach giving to people who need help.
rel|3|l5-6|k-2|Langar: A Meal for All — At a Sikh langar, everyone sits and eats together for free.
rel|3|l6-1|k-2|Thanks Before a Meal — Many families say thank you before or after they eat.
rel|3|l6-2|k-2|Candles on Friday Night — Many Jewish families light candles and say a blessing to start Shabbat.
rel|3|l6-3|k-2|Five Prayers a Day — Many Muslims pray five times each day, facing toward Mecca.
rel|3|l6-4|k-2|A Song of Praise — Many people sing to give thanks and to pray.
rel|3|l6-5|k-2|Sitting and Breathing — Many Buddhists sit still and pay attention to their breath.
rel|3|l6-6|k-2|Being Kind When Others Are Quiet — We stay calm and kind when someone else is praying or being quiet.
rel|4|l7-1|3-5|What People Believe — Most religions teach beliefs, ideas their followers hold to be true about life and what matters most.
rel|4|l7-2|3-5|What People Do — Religions are also practices, things followers do, like praying, eating special foods and keeping holidays.
rel|4|l7-3|3-5|Who They Do It With — Religions are communities, groups of people who gather and help each other, often at a house of worship.
rel|4|l7-4|3-5|The Cross and the Star — A symbol is a picture that stands for an idea; the cross stands for Christianity and the six-pointed star for Judaism.
rel|4|l7-5|3-5|The Crescent and the Khanda — The crescent moon and star is often linked to Islam, and the khanda is the symbol of the Sikhs.
rel|4|l7-6|3-5|Om and the Wheel — Om is a sacred sound for Hindus, and the eight-spoked wheel stands for the Buddha's teaching.
rel|4|l7-7|3-5|Tradition and Sacred — A tradition is what is handed down, and sacred means very special and set apart for holy use.
rel|4|l7-8|3-5|People of No Religion — Many people follow no religion, and they are part of every fair study of how people live.
rel|4|l7-9|3-5|Describe, Do Not Judge — At school we learn about religions by describing them fairly, which is different from being part of one.
rel|4|l8-1|3-5|Two Clocks in the Sky — Some calendars count years by the sun and some count months by the moon, and a few use both.
rel|4|l8-2|3-5|Why Some Holidays Move — Holidays set by the moon move on the school calendar, while Christmas stays on one date.
rel|4|l8-3|3-5|Lunar New Year — Lunar New Year starts on a new moon in late January or February and is kept by many families in East Asia and beyond.
rel|4|l8-4|3-5|A New Year in the Fall — Rosh Hashanah is the Jewish New Year, and ten days later comes Yom Kippur, the Day of Atonement.
rel|4|l8-5|3-5|Passover — Passover remembers the story Jews tell of leaving slavery in Egypt, retold at a meal called the seder.
rel|4|l8-6|3-5|Christmas and Easter — Christians celebrate Jesus's birth at Christmas and, they believe, his rising from death at Easter.
rel|4|l8-7|3-5|Ramadan and Two Eids — Muslims fast in daylight during Ramadan, celebrate Eid al-Fitr at its end, and keep Eid al-Adha at the time of the pilgrimage.
rel|4|l8-8|3-5|Diwali and Holi — Diwali is the Hindu festival of lights in the fall, and Holi is the spring festival of colors.
rel|4|l8-9|3-5|Vesak and Vaisakhi — Vesak honors the Buddha on a spring full moon, and Vaisakhi is a Sikh spring holiday remembering the Khalsa.
rel|4|l8-10|3-5|A Year on One Wall — Putting holidays on one calendar shows what each one remembers and how different calendars work together.
rel|5|l9-1|3-5|Abraham and One God — Jews, Christians and Muslims all tell stories about Abraham, who Jews see as the first of their ancestors.
rel|5|l9-2|3-5|Moses and Mount Sinai — Jews tell how Moses led the Israelites out of Egypt and how God gave the Torah at Mount Sinai.
rel|5|l9-3|3-5|The Torah — The Torah, the first five books of the Hebrew Bible, is the most important text in Judaism.
rel|5|l9-4|3-5|The Synagogue and Shabbat — Many Jews gather at a synagogue and rest on Shabbat, from Friday sunset to Saturday night.
rel|5|l9-5|3-5|Kosher Food and the Jewish Year — Kosher rules guide what many Jews eat, and holidays like Rosh Hashanah and Yom Kippur mark the Jewish year.
rel|5|l9-6|3-5|Jerusalem, a Shared City — Jerusalem is a holy city for Jews, Christians and Muslims.
rel|5|l9-7|3-5|Jesus the Teacher — Christians follow Jesus, a Jewish teacher who, the Gospels say, taught about love for God and others.
rel|5|l9-8|3-5|Christmas and Easter — Christmas remembers the birth of Jesus, and Easter celebrates what Christians believe is his rising from death.
rel|5|l9-9|3-5|The Bible's Two Parts — The Christian Bible has two parts, the Old Testament and the New Testament.
rel|5|l9-10|3-5|Church, Baptism and Three Families — Christians gather in churches, share baptism and communion, and belong to three big families: Catholic, Orthodox and Protestant.
rel|5|l10-1|3-5|Muhammad in Mecca — Muslims believe Muhammad was the last prophet, and that God first spoke to him through an angel in a cave near Mecca.
rel|5|l10-2|3-5|The Qur'an — Muslims believe the Qur'an is God's own words, given to Muhammad in Arabic.
rel|5|l10-3|3-5|Allah Means God — Allah is the Arabic word for God, used by Muslims and by Arabic-speaking Christians.
rel|5|l10-4|3-5|Saying the Faith and Praying — The first two pillars are saying the faith and praying five times a day.
rel|5|l10-5|3-5|Giving and Fasting — The third and fourth pillars are giving to people in need and fasting during Ramadan.
rel|5|l10-6|3-5|The Pilgrimage to Mecca — The fifth pillar is the hajj, a journey to Mecca that Muslims make once in their life if they can.
rel|5|l10-7|3-5|The Mosque and Friday Prayer — Muslims gather at a mosque, and many attend a special group prayer at midday on Friday.
rel|5|l10-8|3-5|Eid: Two Big Celebrations — Muslims celebrate two Eids: Eid al-Fitr after Ramadan and Eid al-Adha at the time of the hajj.
rel|5|l10-9|3-5|Sunni and Shia, Near and Far — Muslims live all over the world, and most are Sunni or Shia, two big families within Islam.
rel|6|l11-1|3-5|One Tradition, Many Ways — Hinduism is a very old tradition from India with many ways to worship and many sacred texts.
rel|6|l11-2|3-5|Many Gods and One Spirit — Many Hindus describe many gods and goddesses as faces of one great spirit, called Brahman.
rel|6|l11-3|3-5|Worship at Home and Temple — Puja is Hindu worship with offerings, light and prayer, done at home and in temples.
rel|6|l11-4|3-5|Diwali and Holi — Diwali celebrates light over darkness, and Holi welcomes spring with colors.
rel|6|l11-5|3-5|Karma: Actions Matter — Hindus teach karma, the idea that every action brings results.
rel|6|l11-6|3-5|The Circle of Lives — Many Hindus believe the soul is reborn many times until it reaches moksha, freedom.
rel|6|l11-7|3-5|Prince Siddhartha — Buddhists tell how Prince Siddhartha left his palace to find the answer to suffering.
rel|6|l11-8|3-5|The Four Noble Truths — The Buddha taught that craving causes suffering and that a path can end it.
rel|6|l11-9|3-5|The Eightfold Path — Buddhists follow the Eightfold Path of wise seeing, kind acting and careful minds.
rel|6|l11-10|3-5|Monks, Temples and Meditation — Buddhists meditate, visit temples and support monks and nuns who study the Buddha's teaching.
rel|6|l11-11|3-5|Two Big Branches — Theravada and Mahayana are two big branches of Buddhism found in different lands.
rel|6|l11-12|3-5|Vesak and Comparing Paths — Vesak honors the Buddha, and Hindus and Buddhists share some words but explain them differently.
rel|6|l12-1|3-5|Guru Nanak's Message — Sikhs follow the teachings of Guru Nanak, who taught one God and the equality of all people.
rel|6|l12-2|3-5|The Gurdwara and the Langar — In the gurdwara, Sikhs sing from their holy book and share a free meal called langar.
rel|6|l12-3|3-5|The Turban and the Five Ks — Many Sikhs keep five articles of faith, called the five Ks, and wear a turban.
rel|6|l12-4|3-5|Mahavira and Ahimsa — Jains follow Mahavira's teaching of ahimsa, doing no harm to any living thing.
rel|6|l12-5|3-5|Living Gently — Jains practice ahimsa in daily life through careful eating, speaking and working.
rel|6|l12-6|3-5|Compare: Ahimsa and Karma — Hindus, Buddhists and Jains all speak of karma and non-harm, but in their own ways.
rel|6|l12-7|3-5|Confucius and Respect — Confucius taught respect for parents and teachers and kindness as the base of a good society.
rel|6|l12-8|3-5|The Dao: The Way of Nature — Daoists try to live in harmony with the Dao, the natural way of all things.
rel|6|l12-9|3-5|Lunar New Year and Blended Ways — Lunar New Year and ancestor honoring show how many people in East Asia blend several traditions.
rel|7|l13-1|3-5|Many Buildings, Many Faiths — Chicago has churches, synagogues, mosques, temples and gurdwaras, where people of many faiths gather.
rel|7|l13-2|3-5|Inside a Church, Synagogue and Mosque — Each house of worship has special things inside that show what matters to the people who gather there.
rel|7|l13-3|3-5|A Temple Open to All — The Bahá'í House of Worship in Wilmette has nine sides and nine doors and welcomes people of every faith.
rel|7|l13-4|3-5|The Parliament of 1893 — In 1893 Chicago hosted the World's Parliament of Religions, where leaders of many faiths spoke to one another.
rel|7|l13-5|3-5|The Parliament Returns — Starting in 1993, the Parliament of the World's Religions began meeting again, including more times in Chicago.
rel|7|l13-6|3-5|Faiths Work Together — Groups of different faiths often join hands on helping projects, like food drives and building homes.
rel|7|l13-7|3-5|The First Amendment — The First Amendment says the government may not pick a religion, and people may follow any faith or none.
rel|7|l13-8|3-5|Religion at a Public School — Public schools teach about religions without telling students what to believe.
rel|7|l13-9|3-5|Being a Good Neighbor — Good neighbors ask kind questions and never tease others about food, clothes or holidays.
rel|7|l14-1|3-5|What Is an Oral Tradition? — An oral tradition is knowledge passed down by speaking, singing and remembering, not only by writing.
rel|7|l14-2|3-5|Memory and Respect — In oral traditions, people remember stories carefully and tell them with respect.
rel|7|l14-3|3-5|Stories With Permission — Some stories are shared with everyone, and some are only for members of a community or for certain times.
rel|7|l14-4|3-5|People of the Great Lakes — The Potawatomi, Ojibwe and Odawa have lived around the Great Lakes for a very long time and still live here today.

rel|7|l14-6|3-5|The Yoruba and the Orishas — The Yoruba of West Africa pass on teachings about God, the orishas and the ancestors mostly by telling and ceremony.
rel|7|l14-7|3-5|Across the Ocean — When people move, they carry their faith with them, and it can grow in new places.
rel|7|l14-8|3-5|The Great Migration — In the Great Migration, millions of Black Americans moved north, and their churches helped build Chicago.
rel|7|l14-9|3-5|New Neighbors, New Temples — Immigrants from around the world brought churches, synagogues, temples, mosques and gurdwaras to the Chicago area.
rel|8|l15-1|6-8|Primary and Secondary Sources — A primary source comes from the time or people being studied; a secondary source explains or studies it later.
rel|8|l15-2|6-8|Who, When, Why and for Whom — Every source has an author, a time, a purpose and an audience, and asking about them helps a reader understand it.
rel|8|l15-3|6-8|Law and Story — Sacred books often hold different kinds of writing, called genres, such as laws that guide life and stories that teach through events.
rel|8|l15-4|6-8|Poetry and Prophecy — Poetry expresses feeling through images, and prophecy, as traditions describe it, is a message spoken on behalf of God.
rel|8|l15-5|6-8|Letters and Teachings — Some sacred writings are letters to real communities, and others are collected sayings of a teacher.
rel|8|l15-6|6-8|BCE and CE — BCE and CE count years before and after year 1 in the common calendar, and historians use them so every tradition can share one timeline.
rel|8|l15-7|6-8|When Dates Are Uncertain — The mark c. means about, and it tells a reader that a date is a careful estimate from the evidence.
rel|8|l15-8|6-8|When Traditions Began — The major world religions began at different times across more than three thousand years, and many grew from older roots.
rel|8|l15-9|6-8|Where Religions Began and Live Now — Religions often begin in one small region and later spread worldwide, so a map of beginnings looks very different from a map of today.
rel|8|l15-10|6-8|What a Historian Can and Cannot Say — A historian can describe what people believed and did, but cannot use evidence to decide whether a belief is true.
rel|8|l16-1|6-8|What Makes a Ritual — A ritual is an action done the same way on purpose, and adherents say it connects them to something they hold sacred.
rel|8|l16-2|6-8|Birth and Coming of Age — Many traditions mark birth and growing up with rites of passage that welcome a person into the community.
rel|8|l16-3|6-8|Marriage and Death — Traditions mark marriage and death with rituals that express their teachings about love, family and what comes after life.
rel|8|l16-4|6-8|Mecca, Jerusalem and Rome — A pilgrimage is a journey to a sacred place, and three cities draw pilgrims from Islam, Judaism and Christianity.
rel|8|l16-5|6-8|Varanasi, Bodh Gaya and Amritsar — South Asia holds pilgrimage places sacred to Hindus, Buddhists and Sikhs, each tied to a river, a teacher or a Guru.
