rel|8|l16-6|6-8|Church and Mosque — A church and a mosque are shaped by how their communities worship, from a cross-shaped floor to a wall that points to Mecca.
rel|8|l16-7|6-8|Synagogue and Gurdwara — A synagogue centers on the Torah scroll, and a gurdwara centers on the Guru Granth Sahib and a shared kitchen.
rel|8|l16-8|6-8|Mandir and Stupa — A Hindu mandir leads worshippers toward a small inner shrine, and a Buddhist stupa is a dome that pilgrims walk around.
rel|8|l16-9|6-8|A Holy Day Each Week — Judaism, Christianity and Islam each set apart one day of the week, but they choose different days and keep them differently.
rel|8|l16-10|6-8|Fasts and Feasts — Many traditions follow a time of fasting with a time of feasting, and both teach through the body what a tradition values.
rel|9|l17-1|6-8|Abraham and the Covenant — Jews, Christians and Muslims all look back to Abraham, and in the Torah God makes a covenant with him and his family.
rel|9|l17-2|6-8|Moses, the Exodus and Sinai — The Torah tells how Moses led the Israelites out of slavery in Egypt and how they received God's teaching at Mount Sinai.
rel|9|l17-3|6-8|Reading the Story as Historians — Historians compare the Bible with archaeology and other records; they describe what people believed without deciding if it is true.
rel|9|l17-4|6-8|Exile in Babylon — In 586 BCE Babylon destroyed the first Temple and sent many Jews into exile, where they learned to keep their faith far from home.
rel|9|l17-5|6-8|The Second Temple Rises and Falls — Jews rebuilt the Temple after the exile, but Rome destroyed this second Temple in 70 CE.
rel|9|l17-6|6-8|The Rabbis and Their Books — After 70 CE, rabbis made study, prayer and good deeds the center of Jewish life, and wrote the Mishnah and the Talmud.
rel|9|l17-7|6-8|Shabbat and the Synagogue — Shabbat is a weekly day of rest, and the synagogue is where Jews gather to pray, read Torah and study.
rel|9|l17-8|6-8|Holidays and the Life Cycle — Jewish holidays follow a lunar calendar, and rites mark birth, coming of age, marriage and death.
rel|9|l17-9|6-8|Communities and Movements — Jews live in many lands with different customs, and today most belong to Orthodox, Conservative, Reform or other movements, or to none.
rel|9|l17-10|6-8|Antisemitism and the Holocaust — Jews have faced hatred called antisemitism for centuries; in the Holocaust, Nazi Germany murdered about six million Jews.
rel|9|l18-1|6-8|Judea Under Rome — Jesus lived in Judea and Galilee while the Roman Empire ruled, a time when many Jews hoped for a Messiah.
rel|9|l18-2|6-8|Jesus in the Gospels — Four Gospels tell Jesus' story; Christians believe he died on a cross and rose from the dead.
rel|9|l18-3|6-8|Paul and the Early Church — Paul's travels and letters helped spread the faith to non-Jews across the Roman world.
rel|9|l18-4|6-8|Martyrs, Constantine and the Creed — Christians were at times persecuted until Constantine allowed their faith in 313 CE; church councils then wrote creeds.
rel|9|l18-5|6-8|East, West and the Middle Ages — In 1054 the church split into Catholic and Orthodox, while monasteries, cathedrals and universities shaped medieval Europe.
rel|9|l18-6|6-8|Luther and the Printing Press — In 1517 Martin Luther challenged church practices, and the printing press spread his ideas fast, starting the Protestant Reformation.
rel|9|l18-7|6-8|Many Churches, Three Families — After Luther came Calvin, other Protestant churches and a Catholic reform, leaving three large Christian families today.
rel|9|l18-8|6-8|The Americas and the Black Church — Christianity came to the Americas with European colonizers, and enslaved Africans and their descendants built churches of their own.
rel|9|l18-9|6-8|Civil Rights and Chicago's Churches — Black churches helped lead the civil rights movement, and Chicago today holds churches of every Christian family.
bib|1|l1-1|k-2|One Cover, Many Books — The Bible is one big book made of many smaller books.
bib|1|l1-2|k-2|The First Story — Genesis is the first book, and it starts with a story about the beginning.
bib|1|l1-3|k-2|Light, Sky, Land and Sea — The story says God made light, sky, land and sea in the first three days.
bib|1|l1-4|k-2|Sun, Fish, Animals and Rest — The story says God made sun, moon, fish, birds, animals and people, then rested.
bib|1|l1-5|k-2|A Garden Called Eden — The story says God put the first people in a garden called Eden with one rule.
bib|1|l1-6|k-2|The Snake and the Fruit — The story says a snake talked, the rule was broken, and the people left the garden.
bib|1|l2-1|k-2|Noah Builds a Boat — The story says God told Noah to build a big boat because a flood was coming.
bib|1|l2-2|k-2|Two of Each Animal — The story says animals came two by two, and then it rained for forty days.
bib|1|l2-3|k-2|The Raven and the Dove — The story says Noah sent out birds to find out if the water was gone.
bib|1|l2-4|k-2|The Rainbow Promise — The story says the rainbow is a sign of a promise never to flood the whole earth again.
bib|1|l2-5|k-2|The Tall Tower — The story of Babel says people built a tall tower and then began to speak many languages.
bib|1|l2-6|k-2|Noah in Other Books — Jews, Christians and Muslims all tell a story of Noah, each in their own book.
bib|10|l19-1|6-8|Seven Days in Genesis 1 — Genesis 1 tells creation as an ordered week, with a pattern of speech, making, naming and rest.
bib|10|l19-2|6-8|A Garden in Genesis 2 — Genesis 2 tells of creation again, in a different order and style, centered on a garden and a first human pair.
bib|10|l19-3|6-8|Two Accounts, Many Readers — Readers have handled the two creation accounts in different ways: as one story told twice, as two sources joined, or as a general view followed by a close-up.
bib|10|l19-4|6-8|Cain and Abel — Genesis 4 tells of the first brothers, the first murder, and a question the text leaves the reader to answer.
bib|10|l19-5|6-8|Noah and the Flood — Genesis 6 to 9 tells of a flood, an ark, and a covenant marked by a rainbow.
bib|10|l19-6|6-8|The Tablet and the Ark — The Mesopotamian flood story in the Epic of Gilgamesh is like Genesis in many details and different in its meaning.
bib|10|l19-7|6-8|The Tower of Babel — Genesis 11 explains many languages through a short story about a tower, a name, and a scattering.
bib|10|l19-8|6-8|Abraham, Sarah and Hagar — Genesis 12 to 25 tells of a promise to Abraham and Sarah, and of Hagar and Ishmael, whose story the text also honors.
bib|10|l19-9|6-8|Isaac, Rebekah and Jacob — Genesis 24 to 36 follows the promise through Rebekah's cleverness, Jacob's trickery, and a night wrestling that gives Israel its name.
bib|10|l19-10|6-8|Joseph in Egypt — The Joseph story, Genesis 37 to 50, is a long, shaped tale that carries the family into Egypt and sets up the book of Exodus.
bib|10|l19-11|6-8|What the Spade Can Say — Archaeology can describe the world the ancestor stories are set in, but it cannot confirm or deny the people themselves.
bib|10|l20-1|6-8|Slavery and a Basket — Exodus opens with Jacob's family grown into a people, enslaved in Egypt, and a baby saved by women who defy Pharaoh.
bib|10|l20-2|6-8|The Burning Bush — At a bush that burns without burning up, the text says God gives Moses a task and a name.
bib|10|l20-3|6-8|Plagues and Passover — Exodus 7 to 12 tells of ten plagues and of a night whose meal, Passover, is still kept every spring.
bib|10|l20-4|6-8|The Sea and the Song — Exodus 14 and 15 tell the crossing of the sea twice, first as a story and then as a song.
bib|10|l20-5|6-8|Sinai and the Covenant — At Sinai the text turns from story to law, and the Ten Commandments open with a reminder of the escape from Egypt.
bib|10|l20-6|6-8|Counting the Ten — Jews, Catholics and Lutherans, and other Christians number the same verses of Exodus 20 in three different ways.
bib|10|l20-7|6-8|The Covenant Code and Hammurabi — Exodus 21 to 23 is a set of case laws that resemble, and differ from, the laws of the Babylonian king Hammurabi.
bib|10|l20-8|6-8|The Tabernacle — The last third of Exodus describes a portable tent-shrine in great detail, and ends with God's presence filling it.
bib|10|l20-9|6-8|Leviticus and Holiness — Leviticus, the book of priests and offerings, also contains the command to love your neighbor as yourself.
bib|10|l20-10|6-8|Deuteronomy and the Shema — Deuteronomy is Moses's farewell speeches, and its call to hear, "Shema," became the central prayer of Judaism.
bib|10|l20-11|6-8|Egypt, Canaan and the Historians — Egypt ruled Canaan in the Late Bronze Age, Israel is first named around 1208 BCE, and historians disagree about what lies behind the Exodus story.
bib|11|l21-1|6-8|Joshua and the Walls of Jericho — The book of Joshua tells how Israel entered Canaan, took Jericho, and divided the land among twelve tribes.
bib|11|l21-2|6-8|What the Ground Says — Archaeology gives a picture of early Israel that partly matches Joshua and partly does not, and scholars explain the gaps in different ways.
bib|11|l21-3|6-8|Judges in a Crisis — Judges tells a repeating story of trouble and rescue, with leaders like Deborah, Gideon and Samson, and ends by saying there was no king.
bib|11|l21-4|6-8|The People Ask for a King — In 1 Samuel the elders ask Samuel for a king, and the text gives both a warning against kings and the start of kingship.
bib|11|l21-5|6-8|Saul, David and One Kingdom — The books of Samuel tell how kingship passed from Saul to David, who made Jerusalem his capital and received a promise about his house.
bib|11|l21-6|6-8|Solomon and the Temple — First Kings presents Solomon as a wise builder who raised the Temple in Jerusalem, and also as a king whose burdens and shrines led to trouble.
bib|11|l21-7|6-8|The Kingdom Splits — After Solomon, the text says ten northern tribes broke away as Israel, leaving Judah under David's line, and Kings judges every ruler by one test.
bib|11|l21-8|6-8|Assyria Takes Samaria — The Assyrian empire pressed Israel for tribute for a century and then, in 722 BCE, captured Samaria and deported its people.
bib|11|l21-9|6-8|Hezekiah, Josiah and the Fall of Jerusalem — Judah survived Assyria under Hezekiah, reformed under Josiah, and then fell to Babylon, which burned the Temple in 586 BCE.
bib|11|l21-10|6-8|The House of David Stone — The Tel Dan stele, found in 1993, is an enemy king's record that names the House of David, the first mention of David outside the Bible.
bib|11|l21-11|6-8|King Mesha's Side of the Story — The Mesha stele lets us read a war between Israel and Moab from the Moabite king's point of view and set it beside 2 Kings 3.
bib|11|l21-12|6-8|Sennacherib's Prism in Chicago — Sennacherib's clay prism gives Assyria's account of the 701 BCE attack on Judah, and a copy of it sits in a museum in Chicago.
bib|11|l22-1|6-8|Carried Off to Babylon — Babylon deported Judah's leaders in 597 and 586 BCE, and the exiles settled in villages where they could keep together.
bib|11|l22-2|6-8|What the Exile Changed — Without land, king or Temple, the exiles held on through text, prayer and practices that marked them as a people.
bib|11|l22-3|6-8|Cyrus and the Cylinder — In 539 BCE Persia's King Cyrus took Babylon, and both his clay cylinder and the book of Ezra describe him letting captive peoples go home.
bib|11|l22-4|6-8|A Smaller Temple — The returned exiles rebuilt the Temple by 515 BCE, a modest building that stood for the next five centuries.
bib|11|l22-5|6-8|Ezra Reads, Nehemiah Builds — Ezra brought the Torah to the people as a public reading, and Nehemiah rebuilt Jerusalem's wall, both under Persian authority.
bib|11|l22-6|6-8|Chronicles Tells It Again — First and Second Chronicles retell the story of Samuel and Kings for readers after the exile, with the Temple and David's line at the center.
bib|11|l22-7|6-8|Esther at the Persian Court — The book of Esther tells how a Jewish queen in Persia risked her life to save her people, and gives Purim its story.
bib|11|l22-8|6-8|Daniel in Babylon's Court — Daniel tells stories of Jewish young men staying faithful in a foreign court, and then visions of empires rising and falling.
bib|11|l22-9|6-8|Alexander and the Greek World — Alexander's conquest in 332 BCE brought Greek language and culture to Judea, and the Torah was translated into Greek.
bib|11|l22-10|6-8|The Maccabees and Hanukkah — A revolt against the Greek king Antiochus IV won back the Temple in 164 BCE, a story told in the books of Maccabees and remembered at Hanukkah.
bib|11|l22-11|6-8|The World Jesus Was Born Into — By the first century Judea was under Rome and Herod, with a rebuilt Temple, synagogues, and several Jewish groups reading the same scriptures.
bib|12|l23-1|6-8|One Hundred Fifty Poems — The Book of Psalms is a collection of 150 poems, arranged in five smaller books.
bib|12|l23-2|6-8|Names at the Top — Many psalms carry a heading naming David or another figure, and readers disagree about what those headings mean.
bib|12|l23-3|6-8|Kinds of Psalms — Scholars sort the psalms into types, such as praise, lament, thanksgiving, royal, wisdom and pilgrimage, by their shape and purpose.
bib|12|l23-4|6-8|Psalm 23: The Shepherd — Psalm 23 builds its comfort out of two pictures, a shepherd and a host, and the parallel lines that carry them.
bib|12|l23-5|6-8|Psalm 1: Two Roads — Psalm 1 opens the whole book by contrasting two ways of life, and its first word is translated differently in Jewish and Christian Bibles.
bib|12|l23-6|6-8|Psalm 137: Songs in Exile — Psalm 137 is a lament from the Babylonian exile that ends with a cry for revenge, and readers have long wrestled with that ending.
bib|12|l23-7|6-8|Psalm 150: Every Instrument — Psalm 150 closes the book with a burst of praise that names instrument after instrument.
bib|12|l23-8|6-8|In the Synagogue — Jewish worship has used the psalms daily, weekly and at festivals for well over two thousand years.
bib|12|l23-9|6-8|In the Church — Christians took the psalms into their worship from the beginning, chanting, translating and setting them to new music.
bib|12|l23-10|6-8|Gospel Music in Chicago — Chicago's Black churches turned psalm-shaped praise into gospel music in the 1930s.
bib|12|l23-11|6-8|How the Lines Work — Parallelism comes in several patterns, and spotting them is the first step to writing a psalm-shaped poem.
bib|12|l23-12|6-8|Your Own Psalm — You can write a psalm-shaped poem by choosing a type, using parallel lines and imagery, and building a turn.
bib|12|l24-1|6-8|What Wisdom Writing Is — Wisdom literature is a genre found across the ancient Near East that asks how to live well, based on experience rather than law or prophecy.
bib|12|l24-2|6-8|Egypt's Amenemope — An Egyptian instruction book, the Instruction of Amenemope, closely resembles a section of Proverbs, and scholars debate how the two are related.
bib|12|l24-3|6-8|The Two-Line Saying — A proverb packs a lesson into two parallel lines, often by contrast, and is meant to be memorized and tested against life.
bib|12|l24-4|6-8|The Fear of the LORD — Proverbs makes "the fear of the LORD" the starting point of wisdom, and readers debate what kind of fear it means.
bib|12|l24-5|6-8|The Woman of Valor — Proverbs ends with an acrostic poem praising a capable woman, which Jewish families sing on Friday night.
bib|12|l24-6|6-8|Who Wrote Proverbs? — Proverbs names several sources in its headings, and tradition and scholarship read those names in different ways.
bib|12|l24-7|6-8|The Wager in Heaven — Job's prose opening sets up a test that Job never learns about, and it raises the question the whole book argues.
bib|12|l24-8|6-8|The Friends' Theology — Job's friends defend the belief that suffering is always deserved, and the book shows that belief failing against Job's case.
bib|12|l24-9|6-8|The Voice from the Storm — God answers Job out of a whirlwind with questions about creation, not with an explanation, and readers disagree about what that answer means.
bib|12|l24-10|6-8|Vanity of Vanities — Ecclesiastes questions whether anything lasts, and its key word hevel has been translated many ways.
bib|12|l24-11|6-8|The Song of Songs — The Song of Songs is love poetry between two voices, and Jewish and Christian traditions have long read it as an allegory as well.
bib|12|l24-12|6-8|Three Books That Disagree — Proverbs, Job and Ecclesiastes give different answers to the same question, and the Bible keeps all three.
bib|13|l25-1|6-8|Rome, Herod and Pilate — The Gospels are set in a land ruled by Rome through local kings and Roman governors.
bib|13|l25-2|6-8|The Temple and the Groups — Jewish life in the Gospels centered on the Temple, and several groups disagreed about how to keep the Torah under Rome.
bib|13|l25-3|6-8|The Dead Sea Scrolls — Scrolls found near the Dead Sea in 1947 give a rare window into Jewish life and scripture in the time of the Gospels.
bib|13|l25-4|6-8|What a Gospel Is — A gospel is an ancient kind of life-story written to persuade, and the New Testament has four of them.
bib|13|l25-5|6-8|When the Gospels Were Written — Most scholars date the Gospels to c. 65–100 CE, with Mark first and John last.
bib|13|l25-6|6-8|The Synoptics and John — Matthew, Mark and Luke share so much that they are called the Synoptics; John tells the story its own way.
bib|13|l25-7|6-8|Parables Close Up — A parable is a short story that makes the listener think, and the Gospels record dozens of them.
bib|13|l25-8|6-8|The Sermon on the Mount — Matthew 5–7 gathers Jesus's teaching into one long sermon that opens with blessings and closes with a rule for living.
bib|13|l25-9|6-8|Miracle Stories — The Gospels tell of healings, feedings and stilled storms, and readers interpret these stories in different ways.
bib|13|l25-10|6-8|The Passion Narrative — All four Gospels end with a long account of Jesus's last week, arrest, trial and death, called the Passion.
bib|13|l25-11|6-8|The Resurrection Accounts — Each Gospel ends with an empty tomb and, as Christians believe, a risen Jesus, and each tells it differently.
bib|13|l26-1|6-8|A Sequel to Luke — Acts continues the Gospel of Luke and tells how Jesus's followers spread from Jerusalem toward Rome.
bib|13|l26-2|6-8|Pentecost, Peter and Stephen — The early chapters of Acts tell of a crowd at Pentecost, Peter's leadership and Stephen's death.
bib|13|l26-3|6-8|Saul Becomes Paul — Acts tells three times how Saul, an opponent of the movement, became Paul, its most traveled messenger.
bib|13|l26-4|6-8|The Roads and the Sea — Acts traces Paul's travels across the eastern Mediterranean, using the roads and shipping lanes of the Roman Empire.
bib|13|l26-5|6-8|Five Cities — Philippi, Corinth, Ephesus and Rome each shaped a letter, and each still shows the traces of the ancient city.
bib|13|l26-6|6-8|How an Ancient Letter Works — Paul's letters follow the shape of ordinary Greek letters, with a greeting, a thanksgiving, a body and a farewell.
bib|13|l26-7|6-8|Paul's Letters in Order — Thirteen letters carry Paul's name; the Bible orders them by length, and scholars debate which he wrote himself.
bib|13|l26-8|6-8|Philemon and the Catholic Letters — Philemon shows a letter at work in one household, and seven "catholic" letters speak to the church at large.
bib|13|l26-9|6-8|Revelation: Visions Under Rome — Revelation is an apocalypse, a book of visions and symbols written to encourage churches under Roman pressure.
bib|13|l26-10|6-8|How 27 Books Became One — The New Testament canon formed over three centuries, and Athanasius's list of 367 CE is the first to match today's 27 books.
bib|14|l27-1|9-10|The Shape of Genesis 1 — Genesis 1:1–2:3 is built on repetition: a seven-day frame, a set formula for each day, and a refrain that God saw it was good.
bib|14|l27-2|9-10|The Garden Account — Genesis 2:4–3:24 tells of creation again, in a different order and a different style, centered on a garden, a man, a woman and a command.
bib|14|l27-3|9-10|Two Names, Two Voices — Genesis 1 calls God Elohim; Genesis 2–3 calls God YHWH Elohim, printed LORD God; readers explain that shift in very different ways.
bib|14|l27-4|9-10|The Documentary Hypothesis — Many scholars since the 1800s have explained the Torah as a weave of four written sources, labeled J, E, D and P, combined by later editors.
bib|14|l27-5|9-10|Tradition and the Critics — Jewish and Christian tradition names Moses as the Torah's author, and both religious readers and academic scholars have challenged the four-source model.
bib|14|l27-6|9-10|Genesis Beside Babylon — Enuma Elish, a Babylonian creation poem, shares images with Genesis 1 but tells a very different kind of story, and readers weigh the likeness in different ways.
bib|14|l27-7|9-10|Eden Read as Story — Read as narrative, Genesis 3 is full of deliberate gaps, and noticing what the text does not say is a close-reading skill.
bib|14|l27-8|9-10|Eden as Theology and Allegory — Jewish, Christian and Muslim readers draw different lessons from Eden, and some in every tradition have read it as allegory rather than as literal history.
bib|14|l27-9|9-10|Cain and Abel — Genesis 4 tells the first murder in a few spare verses, and its unexplained choices have made it a model text for reading gaps.
bib|14|l27-10|9-10|The Flood and Gilgamesh — The flood story in Genesis 6–9 has close parallels in Mesopotamian texts, and its own doubled details have fueled the source debate.
bib|14|l27-11|9-10|Babel and the Toolkit — The tower of Babel story uses wordplay and mirrored structure, and it models the close-reading toolkit: repetition, keyword, structure and gap.
bib|14|l28-1|9-10|Treaties of the Hittite Kings — Hittite treaties of the 1300s and 1200s BCE follow a six-part form, and scholars have used that form to read the covenant texts of the Torah.
bib|14|l28-2|9-10|Sinai in Treaty Form — Exodus 19–24 and Deuteronomy present the covenant with the elements of an ancient treaty, turned to a bond between God and a people.
bib|14|l28-3|9-10|Reading the Decalogue — The Ten Commandments appear twice in the Torah, in Exodus 20 and Deuteronomy 5, and the Hebrew text calls them the ten words.
bib|14|l28-4|9-10|Three Ways to Count — Jews, Catholics and Lutherans, and most other Christians number the Ten Commandments differently, because the text itself does not number them.
bib|14|l28-5|9-10|Sabbath, Two Reasons — The sabbath command is grounded in creation in Exodus 20 and in the exodus from Egypt in Deuteronomy 5, and readers draw on both.
bib|14|l28-6|9-10|The Covenant Code — Exodus 21–23, the Covenant Code, is a collection of case laws on daily life, and its most famous line, eye for eye, has been read as a limit rather than a demand.
bib|14|l28-7|9-10|The Goring Ox and Hammurabi — The Torah's law about an ox that gores closely matches laws in the Code of Hammurabi, and the differences show what each collection cared about.
bib|14|l28-8|9-10|Holiness as Ethics — Leviticus 19 ties holiness to how people treat one another, from leaving crops for the poor to loving the neighbor and the stranger.
bib|14|l28-9|9-10|The Speeches and the Shema — Deuteronomy is framed as Moses' farewell speeches, and its call to hear, the Shema, became the central daily prayer of Judaism.
bib|14|l28-10|9-10|Blessings and Curses — Deuteronomy ends its law with blessings for keeping the covenant and curses for breaking it, and asks the people to choose.
bib|14|l28-11|9-10|The Golden Calf and the Thirteen Attributes — After the golden calf, Exodus 34:6–7 has God describe God's own character, a passage Jewish liturgy calls the Thirteen Attributes and readers of every tradition return to.
bib|14|l28-12|9-10|How Readers Read the Law — Jews, Christians and academic scholars each read the law of the Torah in a different frame, and knowing the frame explains the reading.
bib|15|l29-1|9-10|What a Prophet Is — In the Hebrew Bible a prophet is a messenger who delivers God's word to a people and its rulers, often as a challenge.
bib|15|l29-2|9-10|Amos and the Poor — Amos says worship is worthless while the poor are cheated, and calls for justice to flow like a river.
bib|15|l29-3|9-10|Hosea's Marriage — Hosea turns his own painful marriage into a picture of the bond between God and Israel.
bib|15|l29-4|9-10|Micah's Short Answer — Micah 6:8 sums up what the prophets ask: do justice, love kindness, walk humbly.
bib|15|l29-5|9-10|Isaiah and the Assyrians — Isaiah of Jerusalem spoke during the Assyrian crisis and told kings to trust God rather than alliances.
bib|15|l29-6|9-10|Comfort from Babylon — Isaiah 40-55 speaks to exiles in Babylon, announcing comfort, return, and a God who rules all nations.
bib|15|l29-7|9-10|The Servant Songs — Four poems in Second Isaiah describe a suffering servant, whom Jews and Christians have identified differently.
bib|15|l29-8|9-10|Jeremiah's Call — Jeremiah was called young, protested, and spent forty years delivering a message his people did not want.
bib|15|l29-9|9-10|The Temple Sermon — Jeremiah told worshippers the temple would not save them, and was nearly executed for saying it.
bib|15|l29-10|9-10|A New Covenant — Jeremiah promises a covenant written on the heart, a passage Jews and Christians have read in different ways.
bib|15|l29-11|9-10|Ezekiel's Visions — Ezekiel, a priest among the exiles, saw visions of a moving throne, dry bones, and a rebuilt temple.
bib|15|l29-12|9-10|How Prophets Speak — Prophetic books use recognizable forms: the oracle, the woe, the lawsuit and the vision report.
bib|15|l29-13|9-10|The Prophets in Birmingham — Civil rights leaders read Amos, Micah and Isaiah as a language for justice, and their words shaped the movement.
bib|15|l30-1|9-10|The Shape of the Book — Job is a prose folktale wrapped around a long poem, and its meaning depends on how the two parts fit.
bib|15|l30-2|9-10|The Friends and Their Theology — Job's friends defend a rule that the good prosper and the wicked suffer, and the book shows that rule breaking down.
bib|15|l30-3|9-10|The Voice from the Storm — God's speeches answer Job with the wildness of creation, not with reasons, and readers disagree about what that means.
bib|15|l30-4|9-10|Hevel, Breath and Vapor — Ecclesiastes calls everything hevel, a word for breath or vapor, and tests every path to meaning under the sun.
bib|15|l30-5|9-10|A Time for Everything — Ecclesiastes 3 lists the seasons of life as a cycle no one controls, and readers have set it to music and mourning.
bib|15|l30-6|9-10|Joy in the Ordinary — Alongside its doubt, Ecclesiastes tells the reader to eat, drink, work and love, because these are what God gives.
bib|15|l30-7|9-10|The Editor's Last Word — Ecclesiastes ends with a second voice that sums up Qohelet and adds a call to fear God and keep the commandments.
bib|15|l30-8|9-10|Proverbs Beside Job — Proverbs teaches that wisdom generally leads to a good life; Job and Ecclesiastes test that teaching against hard cases.
bib|15|l30-9|9-10|The Problem of Evil — Why a good and powerful God allows suffering is a question many traditions ask, and each answers in its own way.
bib|15|l30-10|9-10|Job on the Stage — Archibald MacLeish's J.B. (1958) put Job in a modern circus tent and asked the old question after two world wars.
bib|15|l30-11|9-10|Job After Auschwitz — Elie Wiesel's The Trial of God (1979) puts God on trial in Job's spirit, arguing and still refusing to let go.
bib|16|l31-1|9-10|A Mountain and Nine Blessings — Matthew 5–7 gathers the teaching of Jesus into one long sermon, and it opens with the Beatitudes, nine blessings that reverse everyday ideas of who is fortunate.
bib|16|l31-2|9-10|You Have Heard, But I Say — In six sayings called the antitheses, Matthew's Jesus quotes a command from the Torah and then pushes it inward, from the act to the intention behind it.
bib|16|l31-3|9-10|The Prayer in Two Versions — The Lord's Prayer appears in Matthew 6 and, in a shorter form, in Luke 11; comparing them shows how the gospels shaped the same words and how manuscripts changed over time.
bib|16|l31-4|9-10|The Golden Rule and Two Houses — The sermon closes with a one-line summary of the law, the golden rule, and a parable of two builders that asks the hearer to act on what was heard.
bib|16|l31-5|9-10|Mountain and Plain Compared — Luke 6:17–49 contains a shorter sermon on level ground with much of the same material as Matthew 5–7, and the differences show each gospel's choices.
bib|16|l31-6|9-10|Blessed Are the Poor, Woe to the Rich — Luke's beatitudes bless the poor and hungry without qualification and add four woes, which gives his sermon a sharper edge than Matthew's.
bib|16|l31-7|9-10|Seed on Four Kinds of Ground — The parable of the sower appears in Mark, Matthew and Luke with an explanation attached, and it introduces both the form of the parable and a puzzle about why Jesus used it.
bib|16|l31-8|9-10|Who Is My Neighbor? — The Good Samaritan turns the question "who is my neighbor?" into "who acted as a neighbor?" and makes a member of a despised group the hero.
bib|16|l31-9|9-10|A Father and Two Sons — The parable of the prodigal son ends without telling whether the elder brother comes in, leaving the listener to finish the story.
bib|16|l31-10|9-10|The Last Shall Be First — The laborers in the vineyard, found only in Matthew, pays every worker the same and asks the hearer whether generosity can feel unfair.
bib|16|l31-11|9-10|What the Kingdom Means — "Kingdom of God" is the central phrase of Jesus's teaching in the first three gospels, and readers have long debated whether it means a present reality, a coming one, or both.
bib|16|l31-12|9-10|Hillel and Jesus — Hillel, a Jewish teacher a generation before Jesus, left sayings that sound close to the gospels, and setting the two side by side shows Jesus's teaching within first-century Judaism.
bib|16|l32-1|9-10|Reading in Four Columns — The Passion narratives are the longest continuous stories in the gospels, and reading them side by side is the standard method for seeing what each writer chose.
bib|16|l32-2|9-10|The Night in the Garden — The Gethsemane scene shows Jesus in distress in Mark and calm in John, and the difference is a window on how each gospel presents him.
bib|16|l32-3|9-10|Two Trials and a Question — The gospels place Jesus before Jewish leaders and then before Pilate, and the way each gospel divides responsibility has had serious consequences for Jews in later centuries.
bib|16|l32-4|9-10|The Cross and Its Sign — Crucifixion was a Roman public punishment, and the four accounts of it share a frame while differing in details such as the wording of the sign and the hour.
bib|16|l32-5|9-10|Seven Sayings in Three Sets — No gospel records all seven last words from the cross; Mark and Matthew give one, Luke three and John three, and each set fits its gospel's portrait of Jesus.
bib|16|l32-6|9-10|The Women at the Tomb — All four gospels say women found the tomb empty on the first day of the week, and Mark's oldest ending stops there, with the women afraid and silent.
bib|16|l32-7|9-10|The Appearances and Their Settings — The gospels and Paul describe appearances of the risen Jesus in different places and to different people, and Christians hold them as the foundation of their faith.
bib|16|l32-8|9-10|Witnesses Outside the Gospels — Two non-Christian writers, Josephus and Tacitus, mention Jesus within about ninety years of his death, and both confirm the outline of a Jewish teacher executed under Pilate.
bib|16|l32-9|9-10|How Historians Weigh the Gospels — Historians use stated criteria to sort gospel traditions, and although they disagree on much, a short list of facts about Jesus commands wide agreement.
bib|16|l32-10|9-10|Jesus in Jewish Tradition — Judaism does not regard Jesus as messiah or divine, and modern Jewish scholars have studied him as a Jewish teacher of the Second Temple period.
bib|16|l32-11|9-10|Jesus in the Qur'an — Islam honors Jesus, called Isa, as a prophet born of the virgin Maryam who performed miracles by God's permission, but the Qur'an denies that he was God's son and, in most Muslim readings, that he was crucified.
bib|16|l32-12|9-10|Believing a Text and Studying It — A person can read the gospels as scripture, as history, or as literature, and a public school teaches the second and third while respecting the first.
bib|17|l33-1|11-12|Who Paul Was — Paul, a Jewish Pharisee from Tarsus, became the most influential early Christian writer; historians reconstruct his life from his own letters and from the book of Acts.
bib|17|l33-2|11-12|The Letters in Order — Thirteen New Testament letters carry Paul's name; scholars agree that seven are certainly his and debate the rest.
bib|17|l33-3|11-12|Reading a Letter in Its Occasion — A letter answers a situation; to read Paul fairly you ask who wrote to whom, why, and what the whole letter is doing.
bib|17|l33-4|11-12|The Crisis in Galatia — Galatians answers a dispute over whether Gentile believers had to be circumcised and keep the Torah in order to belong.
bib|17|l33-5|11-12|Abraham, Faith and Law — Paul argues from Genesis that Abraham was counted righteous by faith before circumcision, so Gentiles belong by faith too.
bib|17|l33-6|11-12|Freedom and Its Fruit — Galatians 5–6 says freedom from the law is not license: love fulfills the law, and the Spirit produces a visible character.
bib|17|l33-7|11-12|All Have Sinned — Romans 1–3 argues that Gentile and Jew alike fall short, so that no one can claim standing before God on their own.
bib|17|l33-8|11-12|Justified by Faith — Romans 3–5 states that God sets people right by grace through faith, using Abraham as the model, and readers have argued for centuries over exactly what that means.
bib|17|l33-9|11-12|Paul and Israel — Romans 9–11 wrestles with the question of Israel's place in God's purpose, and Paul ends with the claim that God has not cast off his people.
bib|17|l33-10|11-12|Living Together: Romans 12–15 — The last chapters of Romans turn from argument to practice: humility, love, respect for government, and patience between the strong and the weak.
bib|17|l33-11|11-12|Paul on Women, Slavery and the State — Readers on opposite sides of great moral questions have quoted Paul, and the argument often turns on which passages count and how they are read.
bib|17|l33-12|11-12|Luther, Sanders and the New Perspective — Luther read Romans as an answer to the guilty conscience; since 1977 the New Perspective has read it as an argument about who belongs to God's people.
bib|17|l34-1|11-12|The Hebrew Canon — The Jewish canon, the Tanakh, has three parts, and its contents settled over centuries rather than at a single meeting.
bib|17|l34-2|11-12|The Septuagint and the Apocrypha — The Greek Septuagint included books not in the Hebrew canon, and Jewish, Catholic, Orthodox and Protestant Bibles differ over them to this day.
bib|17|l34-3|11-12|Marcion, the Muratorian Fragment and Athanasius — The New Testament canon took shape between about 140 and 367 CE, through a rejected proposal, early lists and a bishop's letter.
bib|17|l34-4|11-12|The Dead Sea Scrolls — Scrolls found near the Dead Sea from 1947 pushed the oldest known Hebrew Bible manuscripts back by about a thousand years.
bib|17|l34-5|11-12|Codices and Papyri — The New Testament survives in thousands of Greek manuscripts, from tiny second-century papyrus scraps to complete fourth-century codices.
bib|17|l34-6|11-12|Textual Criticism and the Ending of Mark — Textual critics compare manuscripts to recover the earliest wording; the last twelve verses of Mark are the best-known case where they differ.
bib|17|l34-7|11-12|Jerome and the Vulgate — Jerome's Latin Bible, made c. 382–405 from Hebrew and Greek, became the Bible of Western Europe for a thousand years.
bib|17|l34-8|11-12|Wycliffe and Tyndale — Putting the Bible into English was dangerous: Wycliffe's followers were persecuted, and Tyndale, whose words fill the King James Bible, was executed in 1536.
bib|17|l34-9|11-12|The King James Bible of 1611 — Commissioned in 1604 and published in 1611, the King James Bible was a committee revision that became the most influential book in English.
bib|17|l34-10|11-12|One Verse, Five Translations — Since 1881 new translations have used older manuscripts and modern English; comparing one verse across versions shows every choice a translator makes.
bib|17|l34-11|11-12|Who Decides What a Word Means — Some of the sharpest disputes between traditions rest on single words, and a fair reader can explain each choice without declaring a winner.
bib|18|l35-1|11-12|Milton Retells Genesis — Paradise Lost (1667) turns the few verses of Genesis 2–3 into a twelve-book epic, and the choices Milton made still shape how many English readers picture Eden, Satan and the fall.
bib|18|l35-2|11-12|Bunyan's Road and Melville's Whale — The Pilgrim's Progress (1678) and Moby-Dick (1851) show two ways literature uses the Bible: as a map for an allegory and as a store of names, sermons and questions.
bib|18|l35-3|11-12|Toni Morrison and the Bible Reread — Toni Morrison's novels take biblical names and verses and reread them through African American history, showing that reception can question a text as well as honor it.
bib|18|l35-4|11-12|Michelangelo and Rembrandt — Painters had to decide what a verse looks like; Michelangelo's Sistine ceiling (1508–1512) and Rembrandt's biblical scenes are readings of the text made in paint.
bib|18|l35-5|11-12|Handel's Messiah and the Spirituals — Handel's Messiah (1741) set King James verses to music for concert halls, while enslaved African Americans made the Exodus and the prophets into songs of their own; both are readings of the text in sound.
bib|18|l35-6|11-12|Puritans and a City on a Hill — The Puritans of New England read the Bible as their own story, and the phrase 'city upon a hill' from Matthew 5:14 has traveled from a 1630 sermon into American political speech.
bib|18|l35-7|11-12|Scripture on Both Sides of Slavery — In the decades before the Civil War, defenders and opponents of slavery both argued from the Bible, and the debate split American churches before it split the nation.
bib|18|l35-8|11-12|Douglass, Lincoln and King — Frederick Douglass, Abraham Lincoln and Martin Luther King Jr. each used the Bible in public argument, and each read it against the way it was being used around them.
bib|18|l35-9|11-12|Abington v. Schempp and the Public School — In 1963 the Supreme Court ruled that public schools may not hold devotional Bible readings, and in the same opinion said that studying the Bible for its literary and historical value is permitted.
bib|18|l35-10|11-12|Translation and the Spread of Reading — Bible translation has driven literacy and printing for five centuries, from Luther and Tyndale to the Cherokee syllabary, and the Bible remains the most translated book in the world.
bib|18|l35-11|11-12|Tracing a Verse Through a Culture — A reception study follows one verse or story from its text through later art, speech and argument, asking at each step what was kept, what changed and why.
bib|18|l36-1|11-12|A Passage and a Question — A capstone begins with a passage short enough to read closely and a question that the text can answer and that its readers have answered differently.
bib|18|l36-2|11-12|Two Translations Side by Side — Reading a passage in two translations exposes the choices every translator makes and points you toward the words that carry the argument.
bib|18|l36-3|11-12|Genre and Shape — Naming a passage's genre and mapping its structure tells you what kind of claims it can make and where its weight falls.
bib|18|l36-4|11-12|The World Behind the Text — Historical context, stated carefully and with 'c.' where dates are uncertain, explains what a passage's first hearers would have taken for granted.
bib|18|l36-5|11-12|Key Words in Hebrew and Greek — Tracing one key word through your passage and the rest of the Bible often supplies the strongest evidence in a capstone.
bib|18|l36-6|11-12|Jewish, Christian and Academic Readers — Every capstone consults at least three kinds of readers, Jewish, Christian and academic, and reports each reading in its own terms with attribution.
bib|18|l36-7|11-12|A Thesis About What the Text Does — A capstone thesis makes a claim about what the passage does and means that is specific, arguable and answerable from the text, not a claim about whether it is true.
bib|18|l36-8|11-12|Evidence and Citation — Evidence in a capstone means quoted or paraphrased text, features of its shape and language, and attributed readings, each cited by book, chapter and verse or by author, title and year.
bib|18|l36-9|11-12|Other Readings, Fairly Stated — A strong capstone states the best case for readings it does not adopt, in their readers' own terms, and explains why its thesis still stands.
bib|18|l36-10|11-12|Essay, Presentation and Rubric — The capstone is delivered twice, as a written essay and as a short presentation with the text open, and both are scored on the same rubric.
bib|18|l36-11|11-12|Reading a Sacred Text in a Public School — Studying the Bible in a public school means reading a text that is sacred to many classmates as literature and history, respecting their beliefs without adopting or dismissing them.
bib|2|l3-1|k-2|Abraham and Sarah Set Out — The story says Abraham and Sarah left home because God told them to go.
bib|2|l3-2|k-2|A Promise Under the Stars — The story says God promised Abraham a family as many as the stars.
bib|2|l3-3|k-2|Isaac Is Born — The story says Sarah had a baby, Isaac, when she and Abraham were very old.
bib|2|l3-4|k-2|Rebekah at the Well — The story says a kind girl named Rebekah became Isaac's wife.
bib|2|l3-5|k-2|Jacob and Esau — The story says twins Jacob and Esau fought, and then made up.
bib|2|l3-6|k-2|A Ladder to the Sky — The story says Jacob dreamed of a ladder with angels going up and down.
bib|2|l3-7|k-2|A Family Tree — The story says Jacob had twelve sons, and you can draw the whole family as a tree.
bib|2|l4-1|k-2|Joseph's Special Coat — The story says Jacob loved Joseph most and gave him a special coat.
bib|2|l4-2|k-2|Dreams That Bow Down — The story says Joseph dreamed his family would bow to him, and it made his brothers angry.
bib|2|l4-3|k-2|Sold by His Brothers — The story says the brothers sold Joseph, and traders took him to Egypt.
bib|2|l4-4|k-2|Pharaoh's Strange Dreams — The story says Joseph explained Pharaoh's dreams: seven fat years, then seven thin years.
bib|2|l4-5|k-2|Saving Up Food — The story says Pharaoh put Joseph in charge, and Joseph stored food for the hungry years.
bib|2|l4-6|k-2|The Brothers Come to Buy Food — The story says the brothers came to Egypt for food and did not know Joseph.
bib|2|l4-7|k-2|Joseph Forgives — The story says Joseph forgave his brothers, and the whole family moved to Egypt.
bib|3|l5-1|k-2|Many, Many People — The story says Jacob's family grew into a big people in Egypt.
bib|3|l5-2|k-2|A New King Says Work — The story says a new king made the Israelites work as slaves.
bib|3|l5-3|k-2|A Mother's Plan — The story says a mother hid her baby in a basket on the river to keep him safe.
bib|3|l5-4|k-2|A Princess Finds a Baby — The story says Pharaoh's daughter found the baby and named him Moses.
bib|3|l5-5|k-2|Moses Runs Away — The story says Moses grew up, got in trouble, and ran away to a far land.
bib|3|l5-6|k-2|The Bush That Kept Burning — The story says Moses saw a bush on fire that did not burn up, and heard God speak.
bib|3|l5-7|k-2|Let My People Go — The story says Moses went back to Egypt to tell Pharaoh to let the people go.
bib|3|l6-1|k-2|Ten Hard Things — The story says Pharaoh said no, so ten hard things called plagues came to Egypt.
bib|3|l6-2|k-2|The Night of Passover — The story says the people ate a special meal in a hurry on the night they left Egypt.
bib|3|l6-3|k-2|The Sea Opens — The story says the sea opened and the people crossed on dry ground.
bib|3|l6-4|k-2|A Song and Some Bread — The story says the people sang for joy, then found strange bread in the desert.
bib|3|l6-5|k-2|Ten Rules on Stone — The story says the people came to Mount Sinai and were given ten rules.
bib|3|l6-6|k-2|One Day to Rest — One of the ten rules says to rest one day each week, and the story links it to creation.
bib|3|l6-7|k-2|Remembering Every Year — Jews remember the Exodus every year at Passover, and Christians read the story too.
bib|4|l7-1|k-2|A Boy Who Watched Sheep — The story says David was the youngest son, and he watched sheep before he was a king.
bib|4|l7-2|k-2|David and the Giant — The story says David faced the giant Goliath with only a sling and a stone.
bib|4|l7-3|k-2|David Becomes King — The story says David grew up and became the king of Israel, with Jerusalem as his city.
bib|4|l7-4|k-2|Songs Called Psalms — The Bible has a book of songs called Psalms, and many of them have David's name at the top.
bib|4|l7-5|k-2|Psalm 23 in Simple Words — Psalm 23 says God is like a shepherd who takes care of the singer.
bib|4|l7-6|k-2|Daniel and the Lions — The story says Daniel kept praying, was thrown to the lions, and came out safe.
bib|4|l7-7|k-2|Jonah and the Big Fish — The story says Jonah ran the wrong way, a big fish swallowed him, and then a whole city said sorry.
bib|4|l7-8|k-2|What a Prophet Is — In the Bible, a prophet is a person who speaks a message, and speaking it takes courage.
bib|4|l8-1|k-2|The Christian Bible and Jesus — The New Testament is the part of the Bible that Christians read for the story of Jesus.
bib|4|l8-2|k-2|Shepherds in the Night — Luke's Gospel says Jesus was born in Bethlehem and shepherds came to see him.
bib|4|l8-3|k-2|A Star and Some Gifts — Matthew's Gospel tells about wise men who followed a star to bring gifts to Jesus.
bib|4|l8-4|k-2|What a Parable Is — A parable is a short story with a lesson inside, and the Gospels say Jesus told many of them.
bib|4|l8-5|k-2|The Good Samaritan — In the Good Samaritan story, a neighbor is anyone who stops to help.
bib|4|l8-6|k-2|The Mustard Seed — The mustard seed parable says something very small can grow very big.
bib|4|l8-7|k-2|Jesus Welcomes Children — The Gospels say that when helpers tried to send children away, Jesus called them to him.
bib|4|l8-8|k-2|Christmas and Easter — Christmas and Easter are days when Christians remember the birth of Jesus and, as they believe, his rising.
bib|5|l9-1|3-5|A Library on One Shelf — The Bible is a collection of many smaller books, written over a long time by many people.
bib|5|l9-2|3-5|Two Testaments, One Tanakh — Christian Bibles have an Old Testament and a New Testament; the Jewish Bible is called the Tanakh.
bib|5|l9-3|3-5|Counting the Same Words — Protestant, Catholic and Jewish Bibles count their books differently, so 66, 73 and 24 can describe much of the same text.
bib|5|l9-4|3-5|Chapters and Verses — Chapter and verse numbers were added long after the books were written, to help readers find the same place.
bib|5|l9-5|3-5|How to Find John 3:16 — To find a verse, use the book name, then the chapter number, then the verse number.
bib|5|l9-6|3-5|Kinds of Books Inside — The Bible's books come in kinds, such as law, story, poetry, prophecy and letters, and each kind is read a different way.
bib|5|l9-7|3-5|Hebrew, Aramaic and Greek — The Bible was first written in three languages: Hebrew, a little Aramaic, and Greek.
bib|5|l9-8|3-5|The King James Bible of 1611 — The King James Version, finished in 1611, became the most famous English Bible and shaped the English language.
bib|5|l9-9|3-5|Newer Bibles, Same Books — Newer translations use modern words, and Jewish and Christian scholars each make their own.
bib|5|l9-10|3-5|Synagogue, Church, Home, School — People read the Bible in different places and for different reasons: worship, family, and study.
bib|5|l9-11|3-5|Bibles in Chicago — Illinois has many kinds of readers, and Chicago once hosted a world meeting where people of many faiths spoke about their scriptures.
bib|5|l10-1|3-5|Genesis to Deuteronomy — The Torah is the first five books of the Bible: Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers and Deuteronomy, in that order.
bib|5|l10-2|3-5|What Torah Means — Torah means teaching, and Jews read it from a scroll while Christians read it as the start of the Old Testament.
bib|5|l10-3|3-5|Creation and a Garden — Genesis opens with the story of God making the world in six days and resting on the seventh, then a garden where a rule is broken.
bib|5|l10-4|3-5|The Flood and the Tower — Genesis tells of a great flood, a rainbow promise, and a tower where languages were mixed.
bib|5|l10-5|3-5|Abraham, Isaac and Jacob — Genesis tells of one family, Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, who received a promise of land and many descendants.
bib|5|l10-6|3-5|Joseph in Egypt — Genesis ends with Joseph, sold by his brothers, rising in Egypt and bringing his whole family there.
bib|5|l10-7|3-5|Moses and the Plagues — Exodus says the family became a people, was enslaved, and Moses was sent to tell Pharaoh to let them go.
bib|5|l10-8|3-5|Passover and the Sea — Exodus tells of the night of Passover and the crossing of the sea, which Jews remember every year.
bib|5|l10-9|3-5|Sinai and the Ten Commandments — At Mount Sinai, Exodus says, God gave the people the Ten Commandments and made a covenant with them.
bib|5|l10-10|3-5|Years in the Wilderness — Leviticus gives laws for worship and daily life, and Numbers tells of forty years of wandering in the desert.
bib|5|l10-11|3-5|Moses Looks at the Land — Deuteronomy is Moses's farewell; he repeats the law, and the Torah ends with him seeing Canaan but not entering.
bib|6|l11-1|3-5|A New Leader Named Joshua — The book of Joshua begins where Deuteronomy ends, with a new leader told to be strong and to keep the Torah.
bib|6|l11-2|3-5|The Walls of Jericho — Joshua 6 tells how the people marched around Jericho for seven days, and readers of every kind have asked what the story means.
bib|6|l11-3|3-5|Twelve Tribes, One Land — Joshua says the land was divided among twelve tribes, each named for a son or grandson of Jacob.
bib|6|l11-4|3-5|Choose This Day — Joshua 24 ends the book with the people gathered at Shechem, renewing their covenant.
bib|6|l11-5|3-5|What a Judge Was — In the book of Judges, a judge is not a person in a courtroom but a leader who rises up in a crisis.
bib|6|l11-6|3-5|Deborah Under the Palm Tree — Deborah is a judge and a prophet who sits under a palm tree, settles disputes and sends a general to war.
bib|6|l11-7|3-5|Gideon's Small Army — The story of Gideon shrinks an army from thousands to three hundred to make a point about who wins the battle.
bib|6|l11-8|3-5|Samson's Strength — Samson is the strongest judge and one of the weakest at keeping promises, and his story ends the book's list of leaders.
bib|6|l11-9|3-5|Ruth and Naomi — Ruth is a foreigner from Moab who chooses to stay with Naomi, and her words became one of the Bible's best-loved promises.
bib|6|l11-10|3-5|A Field, a Wedding and a King — Ruth gathers grain in the field of Boaz, marries him, and the book ends by naming her as the great-grandmother of David.
bib|6|l12-1|3-5|Hannah's Prayer and Samuel — The book of Samuel begins with a mother's prayer, and her son Samuel becomes the last of the judges and the maker of kings.
bib|6|l12-2|3-5|Give Us a King — In 1 Samuel 8, the people ask for a king like other nations, and Samuel warns them what a king will cost.
bib|6|l12-3|3-5|Saul, the First King — Saul is tall, brave and chosen, but the story says he loses his place as king by not following instructions.
bib|6|l12-4|3-5|A Shepherd Is Chosen — Samuel goes to Bethlehem and anoints the youngest son of Jesse, a shepherd boy named David.
bib|6|l12-5|3-5|David and Goliath — The story of David and Goliath is about a small, unarmed boy who trusts, facing a giant who mocks.
bib|6|l12-6|3-5|David and Jonathan — Jonathan, the king's son, becomes David's closest friend and protects him from his own father's jealousy.
bib|6|l12-7|3-5|King in Jerusalem — David becomes king of all Israel, makes Jerusalem his capital and brings the ark there, and the text says his family line will last.
bib|6|l12-8|3-5|Nathan's Story of the Lamb — The text does not hide David's worst deed, and a prophet's short story makes the king judge himself.
bib|6|l12-9|3-5|The Wise King — Solomon asks for wisdom instead of riches, and the story of the two mothers shows what that wisdom looked like.
bib|6|l12-10|3-5|The Temple in Jerusalem — Solomon builds the first Temple in Jerusalem, a stone and cedar house for the ark that took seven years to complete.
bib|6|l12-11|3-5|One Kingdom Becomes Two — After Solomon dies, ten tribes break away, and the books of Kings measure every ruler by the rules for a king.
bib|7|l13-1|3-5|A Message, Not a Fortune — In the Bible, a prophet is a person who speaks a message, most often to kings and crowds.
bib|7|l13-2|3-5|Elijah and the Fire on Carmel — The book of 1 Kings tells how Elijah faced King Ahab and the prophets of Baal on Mount Carmel.
bib|7|l13-3|3-5|The Still Small Voice — After the loud fire, 1 Kings 19 says Elijah heard God not in wind or earthquake but in a quiet voice.
bib|7|l13-4|3-5|Amos the Shepherd — Amos, a shepherd, told the rich people of Israel that God cared how they treated the poor.
bib|7|l13-5|3-5|Isaiah in Jerusalem — Isaiah spoke in Jerusalem and said that caring for the orphan and the widow mattered more than offerings.
bib|7|l13-6|3-5|How Prophets Spoke — Prophets used poems, pictures and even actions to make their message hard to forget.
bib|7|l13-7|3-5|Assyria Takes Israel — In 722 BCE the empire of Assyria captured Samaria, and the northern kingdom of Israel came to an end.
bib|7|l13-8|3-5|Jeremiah Warns Jerusalem — Jeremiah told Jerusalem that Babylon would come, and the city's leaders tried hard to make him stop.
bib|7|l13-9|3-5|The Temple Burns — In 586 BCE Babylon captured Jerusalem, burned the Temple and took many people into exile.
bib|7|l13-10|3-5|By the Rivers of Babylon — Psalm 137 is a song from the exile about missing Jerusalem and not being able to sing.
bib|7|l13-11|3-5|Ezekiel and the Dry Bones — In Babylon, Ezekiel saw a valley of dry bones come to life, a picture of hope for a people who felt finished.
bib|7|l14-1|3-5|Psalm 23: The Shepherd — Psalm 23 pictures God as a shepherd who leads, feeds and protects, and it is loved by Jews and Christians alike.
bib|7|l14-2|3-5|Psalm 100 and Psalm 150: Praise — Psalms 100 and 150 are songs of praise that call for joy, music and every living thing to join in.
bib|7|l14-3|3-5|Five Books of Songs — The 150 psalms are grouped into five smaller books, each ending with a line of blessing.
bib|7|l14-4|3-5|Proverbs: Short and Wise — The book of Proverbs is a collection of short sayings about how to live wisely.
bib|7|l14-5|3-5|Job's Hard Question — The book of Job asks why good people suffer, and it does not give an easy answer.
bib|7|l14-6|3-5|Daniel and His Friends — The book of Daniel tells of young exiles in Babylon who kept their ways and were kept safe in a furnace and a lions' den.
bib|7|l14-7|3-5|Esther Speaks Up — The book of Esther tells how a Jewish queen in Persia risked her life to save her people, remembered each year at Purim.
bib|7|l14-8|3-5|Cyrus Opens the Door — In 538 BCE, Cyrus of Persia let the exiles return to Jerusalem and rebuild the Temple.
bib|7|l14-9|3-5|Ezra Reads the Torah — Nehemiah 8 tells of Ezra reading the Torah aloud to the whole people, who listened from morning to midday.
bib|7|l14-10|3-5|Nehemiah Builds the Wall — Nehemiah left a palace job in Persia to rebuild Jerusalem's broken wall, and the book says it took 52 days.
bib|8|l15-1|3-5|What a Gospel Is — The New Testament begins with four books called Gospels, and each one tells the story of Jesus in its own way.
bib|8|l15-2|3-5|Judea Under Rome — The Gospels take place in a land ruled by Rome, where Jewish people prayed at the Temple and hoped for freedom.
bib|8|l15-3|3-5|Two Birth Stories — Luke and Matthew each tell the birth of Jesus in Bethlehem, but they tell it with different visitors and different details.
bib|8|l15-4|3-5|John at the River — All four Gospels say a preacher named John the Baptist came before Jesus and baptized people in the Jordan River.
bib|8|l15-5|3-5|Twelve Followers — The Gospels say Jesus chose twelve disciples, ordinary workers like fishermen, to travel and learn with him.
bib|8|l15-6|3-5|Stories With a Point — Jesus taught with short stories called parables, and the parable of the sower shows how they work.
bib|8|l15-7|3-5|A Neighbor and a Lost Son — Two parables found only in Luke, the Good Samaritan and the prodigal son, are about kindness to someone you did not expect to love.
bib|8|l15-8|3-5|Teaching on a Hill — Matthew gathers many of Jesus's sayings into one long talk on a hill, the Sermon on the Mount, in chapters 5 to 7.
bib|8|l15-9|3-5|Stories of Healing — The Gospels tell many stories in which Jesus heals sick people, and Christians read them as signs of who he was.
bib|8|l15-10|3-5|Palms and a Supper — The Gospels tell of Jesus riding into Jerusalem to cheering crowds and sharing a last Passover meal with his disciples.
bib|8|l15-11|3-5|The Cross and the Empty Tomb — The Gospels say Jesus was put to death on a cross by Rome and, as Christians believe, rose from the dead on the third day.
bib|8|l16-1|3-5|Luke's Second Book — The Acts of the Apostles picks up where the Gospel of Luke ends and tells how the followers of Jesus spread out from Jerusalem.
bib|8|l16-2|3-5|Wind and Fire at Pentecost — Acts 2 says that on the holiday of Pentecost the followers of Jesus spoke in many languages, and thousands joined them that day.
bib|8|l16-3|3-5|The First Followers Share — Acts says the first followers in Jerusalem shared their food and money, while Peter healed and preached at the Temple.
bib|8|l16-4|3-5|A Light on the Road — Acts 9 tells how Saul, who was chasing the followers of Jesus, became Paul, their most traveled leader.
bib|8|l16-5|3-5|Paul's Journeys — Acts follows Paul on long trips by road and sea to cities around the Mediterranean, where he started groups of followers.
bib|8|l16-6|3-5|A Meeting in Jerusalem — Acts 15 tells of a meeting where the leaders decided that people who were not Jewish could join without keeping every law of the Torah.
bib|8|l16-7|3-5|What a Letter Can Do — Twenty-one books of the New Testament are letters, and a letter let a leader teach a church he could not visit.
bib|8|l16-8|3-5|The Love Chapter — In 1 Corinthians 13, Paul tells a fighting church that without love, nothing else they do is worth anything.
bib|8|l16-9|3-5|Letters From Other Leaders — Besides Paul's letters, the New Testament has letters linked to James, Peter, John and Jude, and one called Hebrews.
bib|8|l16-10|3-5|A Book of Pictures — Revelation, the last book of the Bible, uses strange pictures to give hope to churches in a hard time.
bib|8|l16-11|3-5|Twenty-Seven Books, Every Land — The New Testament has 27 books in four kinds, and the faith it describes spread over time to every part of the world.
bib|9|l17-1|6-8|Kinds of Writing — Genre is the kind of writing a passage is, and it tells a reader which questions to ask.
bib|9|l17-2|6-8|A Library, Not One Book — The Bible is a library of books in different genres, so a careful reader checks the shelf before reading.
bib|9|l17-3|6-8|Studying a Sacred Text at School — In a public school the Bible is studied as literature and history, and every belief about it is described, not taught.
bib|9|l17-4|6-8|Plot in a Few Words — Biblical narrative moves fast, gives few details and leaves gaps the reader must notice.
bib|9|l17-5|6-8|The Narrator Who Stays Quiet — The biblical narrator rarely judges or explains, so readers must weigh the characters for themselves.
bib|9|l17-6|6-8|Repetition and Pattern — Biblical stories repeat words and patterns on purpose, and a change in the pattern is a signal to the reader.
bib|9|l17-7|6-8|Commands and Cases — Biblical law comes in two shapes: direct commands and 'if… then' cases, and each shape does a different job.
bib|9|l17-8|6-8|Ten Words, Three Numberings — The Ten Commandments appear twice in the Torah, and Jews, Catholics and Protestants number them differently.
bib|9|l17-9|6-8|The Covenant Code and Hammurabi — Biblical case law shares a world with older Mesopotamian codes, and the differences show what each valued.
bib|9|l17-10|6-8|Lists of Names as Maps — A genealogy is a map of a people drawn in names, and it tells readers who belongs to whom.
bib|9|l17-11|6-8|Who, for Whom, When and Why — Careful readers ask who wrote a passage, for whom, when and why, and hold the answers as tradition or as scholars' views.
bib|9|l18-1|6-8|Say It, Then Say It Again — Hebrew poetry is built on parallelism: a line says something, and the next line echoes, sharpens or turns it.
bib|9|l18-2|6-8|Pictures in Words — Biblical poets teach through images drawn from daily life, such as shepherds, trees and water.
bib|9|l18-3|6-8|Poems with a Shape — Some biblical poems follow the Hebrew alphabet letter by letter, and their shape is part of their meaning.
bib|9|l18-4|6-8|The Messenger's Words — Biblical prophecy is a message delivered to people in the present, not a prediction of the far future.
bib|9|l18-5|6-8|Acted-Out Messages — Prophets used forms like the oracle, the woe and the sign-act, and each form shaped how a message landed.
bib|9|l18-6|6-8|Short Wise Sayings — Wisdom sayings pack a lesson about daily life into two balanced lines that are easy to remember.
bib|9|l18-7|6-8|An Ancient Life with a Purpose — A gospel is an ancient kind of life story, written to tell readers who Jesus was and to move them to a response.
bib|9|l18-8|6-8|Four Tellings of One Life — Matthew, Mark and Luke share much material, John stands apart, and the differences are clues to each writer's aim.
bib|9|l18-9|6-8|How a Letter Is Built — New Testament letters follow the shape of ancient letters: greeting, thanks, body and farewell.
bib|9|l18-10|6-8|Reading a Letter Whole — A letter should be read from start to finish, in its situation, before any single verse is lifted out.
bib|9|l18-11|6-8|Visions in Hard Times — An apocalypse is a vision report written in a time of persecution to tell readers that history has a hidden end.
bib|9|l18-12|6-8|Symbols and Numbers — Apocalyptic writing uses symbols and numbers as a shared code, and readers decode them by learning what they meant then.
heb|1|l1-1|k-2|A Scroll with Five Books — The Torah is a long scroll with five books inside it.
heb|1|l1-2|k-2|In the Beginning — The first book of the Torah is called Bereshit, which means "In the beginning."
heb|1|l1-3|k-2|Light, Sky, Land and Sea — The Torah says the first three days brought light, sky, land and sea.
heb|1|l1-4|k-2|Sun, Moon and Stars — The Torah says the sun, moon and stars were made on day four.
heb|1|l1-5|k-2|Fish, Birds, Animals and People — The Torah says living things came on days five and six, and people came last.
heb|1|l1-6|k-2|A Day of Rest — The Torah says God rested on the seventh day, and Jewish families call that day Shabbat.
heb|1|l1-7|k-2|Candles, Bread and Songs — Jewish families welcome Shabbat with candles, bread and songs.
heb|1|l1-8|k-2|A Garden Called Eden — The Torah tells of a garden called Eden with trees, rivers and the first people.
heb|1|l2-1|k-2|Noah Builds an Ark — The Torah says God told Noah to build a very big boat called an ark.
heb|1|l2-2|k-2|Two by Two — The Torah says the animals came into the ark two by two.
heb|1|l2-3|k-2|Forty Days of Rain — The Torah says it rained for forty days and forty nights.
heb|1|l2-4|k-2|The Dove and the Leaf — The Torah says a dove brought back an olive leaf, a sign that the water was going down.
heb|1|l2-5|k-2|The Rainbow Promise — The Torah says the rainbow is a sign of God's promise never to flood the whole world again.
heb|1|l2-6|k-2|The Tower of Babel — The Torah says people built a tall tower, and then they began to speak many languages.
heb|1|l2-7|k-2|One Story, Many Families — Jews, Christians and Muslims all tell a story of Noah, and each reads it in its own way.
heb|10|l19-1|6-8|The Book That Begins With a Call — Leviticus, called Vayikra in Hebrew, is the Torah's book of priests, offerings and holiness, set at the Tent of Meeting.
heb|10|l19-2|6-8|The Day of Atonement — Leviticus 16 describes a yearly day when the high priest made atonement for the whole people; Jewish tradition keeps it as Yom Kippur.
heb|10|l19-3|6-8|Be Holy: Leviticus 19 — Leviticus 19 ties holiness to everyday fairness, and includes the command to love your neighbor as yourself.
heb|10|l19-4|6-8|Counting the Camp — Numbers, called Bamidbar, opens with a census of the tribes and a plan for the camp around the Tent of Meeting.
heb|10|l19-5|6-8|Twelve Spies and Forty Years — Numbers 13–14 tells how the people refused to enter the land after the spies' report, and the text says a generation wandered forty years as a result.
heb|10|l19-6|6-8|Balaam and the Talking Donkey — In Numbers 22–24 a foreign seer hired to curse Israel blesses it instead, and his donkey sees what he cannot.
heb|10|l19-7|6-8|The Daughters of Zelophehad — Numbers 27 records five sisters asking for their father's land and the law changing in response; the rabbis praised them as wise.
heb|10|l19-8|6-8|Moses Speaks and the Shema — Deuteronomy is framed as Moses's speeches before his death, and its sixth chapter holds the Shema, the central line of Jewish prayer.
heb|10|l19-9|6-8|The Stranger, the Poor and the Field — Deuteronomy commands care for the stranger, the widow, the orphan and the poor, grounding it in the memory of Egypt; Jewish tradition calls such giving tzedakah.
heb|10|l19-10|6-8|Blessings, Curses and a Mountain Grave — Deuteronomy ends with a choice between blessing and curse, a song, and the death of Moses within sight of the land he does not enter.
heb|10|l19-11|6-8|Counting the Mitzvot — Rabbinic tradition counts 613 commandments in the Torah, and Jewish thinkers have organized and explained them in many ways.
heb|10|l19-12|6-8|A Way of Life, Read Every Year — Jewish tradition reads the Torah's laws as halakhah, a way of walking through life, and reads the whole Torah aloud in a yearly cycle.
heb|10|l20-1|6-8|Four Books, One Story — Joshua, Judges, Samuel and Kings read as one connected history, and many scholars think they were shaped by editors who measured every king against Deuteronomy.
heb|10|l20-2|6-8|Crossing In: Text and Spade — The book of Joshua tells of a swift conquest, while archaeology points to a slower settlement of small villages; readers hold the two accounts side by side.
heb|10|l20-3|6-8|The Judges and Their Cycle — The book of Judges tells a repeating cycle of turning away, oppression, crying out and rescue, and ends by saying there was no king.
heb|10|l20-4|6-8|Samuel, Saul and the Wish for a King — First Samuel tells how the people asked the prophet Samuel for a king, and how Saul, the first king, rose and fell.
heb|10|l20-5|6-8|David: Shepherd, King, Sinner — Second Samuel presents David as the king who united the tribes and made Jerusalem his capital, and as a man whose wrongs the text does not hide.
heb|10|l20-6|6-8|Solomon and the Temple — First Kings presents Solomon as a wise king who built the Temple in Jerusalem, and as a ruler whose heavy taxes and foreign altars the text criticizes.
heb|10|l20-7|6-8|The Kingdom Splits — After Solomon, the text says ten northern tribes broke away under Jeroboam around 930 BCE, leaving two kingdoms, Israel and Judah.
heb|10|l20-8|6-8|Elijah and Elisha — Elijah and Elisha, prophets in the northern kingdom, confront kings and the worship of Baal, and Elijah lives on in Jewish practice as the guest who will return.
heb|10|l20-9|6-8|Assyria: One Kingdom Falls, One Survives — The Assyrian empire destroyed the northern kingdom in 722 BCE and besieged Jerusalem in 701 BCE, and the book of Kings and Assyrian records both tell of it.
heb|10|l20-10|6-8|Josiah and the Found Scroll — Second Kings 22–23 tells how a scroll found in the Temple led King Josiah to reform worship, and scholars connect that scroll with Deuteronomy.
heb|10|l20-11|6-8|Babylon Burns Jerusalem — Second Kings ends with Babylon capturing Jerusalem, burning the Temple in 586 BCE and deporting Judah, and Jewish tradition mourns it each year on the ninth of Av.
heb|10|l20-12|6-8|Stones That Speak: Outside Evidence — Inscriptions such as the Tel Dan stele and Sennacherib's prism name people and events from the book of Kings, and historians use them to test and date the story.
heb|11|l21-1|6-8|The Prophet as Messenger — In the Tanakh a prophet (navi) is a messenger who delivers God's words to people, usually people who do not want to hear them.
heb|11|l21-2|6-8|Call Stories, Oracles and Sign-Acts — Prophetic books share three building blocks: a call story, oracles in poetry, and sign-acts performed with the prophet's own body.
heb|11|l21-3|6-8|Prophets in the Synagogue Year — Every Shabbat and holiday, a passage from the Prophets called the haftarah is read after the Torah portion, so the prophets are heard all year.
heb|11|l21-4|6-8|Isaiah and the Assyrian Threat — Isaiah of Jerusalem spoke during the Assyrian crisis of the late 700s BCE, telling kings to trust God rather than foreign alliances.
heb|11|l21-5|6-8|Swords into Plowshares — Isaiah 2:4 pictures a future when nations stop learning war, a vision Jewish readers, and many others, have made a symbol of peace.
heb|11|l21-6|6-8|Comfort Ye: Isaiah 40 and After — Isaiah 40–66 speaks comfort to exiles, and readers have long discussed who wrote it and who the "servant" of Isaiah 53 is.
heb|11|l21-7|6-8|Jeremiah at the Temple Gate — Jeremiah warned Jerusalem that the Temple would not protect a city that did wrong, and he was nearly killed for saying so.
heb|11|l21-8|6-8|A New Covenant and a City in Mourning — Jeremiah promised a covenant written on the heart, and the book of Lamentations, which tradition links to him, mourns the city he saw fall.
heb|11|l21-9|6-8|Ezekiel: The Chariot and the Dry Bones — Ezekiel, a priest among the exiles in Babylonia, reported visions of a divine chariot and a valley of dry bones that came to life.
heb|11|l21-10|6-8|Amos and Hosea — Amos demanded justice for the poor and Hosea pictured God's bond with Israel as a troubled marriage; both spoke to the northern kingdom.
heb|11|l21-11|6-8|Jonah and Micah — Jonah is a story about a prophet who runs from mercy, and Micah 6:8 sums up what the prophets ask of a person in one sentence.
heb|11|l21-12|6-8|Malachi and the Close of Prophecy — Malachi, the last of the Twelve, spoke after the Temple was rebuilt, and Jewish tradition sees the age of prophecy ending with him.
heb|11|l22-1|6-8|How a Psalm Is Built — The book of Psalms is 150 poems in five books, and its poetry works by parallelism, saying a thing twice in two matching ways.
heb|11|l22-2|6-8|Kinds of Psalms — Psalms fall into types such as praise, lament, thanksgiving and royal psalms, and the type tells a reader what the poem is doing.
heb|11|l22-3|6-8|Psalms in the Siddur — The Jewish prayer book, the siddur, is full of psalms, so Jews have prayed these poems daily for two thousand years.
heb|11|l22-4|6-8|Proverbs: The World Makes Sense — Proverbs collects short sayings that teach a young person how to live well, on the view that wise choices usually bring good results.
heb|11|l22-5|6-8|Job: When the Good Suffer — The book of Job argues, in long poetry, about why a good person suffers, and ends with God's questions instead of an answer.
heb|11|l22-6|6-8|Ecclesiastes: Everything Is Breath — Ecclesiastes questions whether anything lasts, then advises enjoying simple things and keeping God's commands, and it is read at Sukkot.
heb|11|l22-7|6-8|Song of Songs and Ruth — Song of Songs, read at Passover, is love poetry that tradition reads as God and Israel; Ruth, read at Shavuot, tells how a stranger became David's ancestor.
heb|11|l22-8|6-8|Lamentations at Midsummer, Ecclesiastes in the Fall — Lamentations on Tisha B'Av and Ecclesiastes at Sukkot pair a book with a season, so that the calendar teaches the book's mood.
heb|11|l22-9|6-8|Esther and Purim — The book of Esther, which never names God, tells how a Jewish queen in Persia saved her people, and it is read with noise and costumes at Purim.
heb|11|l22-10|6-8|Daniel: Court Tales and Visions — Daniel joins stories of a Jew serving foreign kings with visions of the future, and part of it is written in Aramaic.
heb|11|l22-11|6-8|Ezra and Nehemiah: The Return — Ezra–Nehemiah tells how exiles returned under Persian rule, rebuilt the Temple and the walls, and heard the Torah read aloud in public.
heb|11|l22-12|6-8|Chronicles and the Last Word — Chronicles retells Israel's story from Adam to Cyrus with the Temple at its center, and the Tanakh ends with the words "let him go up."
heb|12|l23-1|9-10|A Pattern Built From Refrains — Genesis 1:1–2:3 is built on a strict pattern of repeated phrases, and the pattern itself is part of the meaning.
heb|12|l23-2|9-10|Three Commentators on Verse One — Rashi, Ibn Ezra and Ramban each read the first verse differently, and their disagreement is part of how Jewish tradition studies Torah.
heb|12|l23-3|9-10|Enuma Elish Beside Genesis 1 — Reading Genesis 1 next to the Babylonian creation epic shows what the two texts share and where Genesis makes different choices.
heb|12|l23-4|9-10|A Second Way to Begin — Genesis 2:4–3:24 tells creation again, with a different order, a different name for God and a different style.
heb|12|l23-5|9-10|The Serpent and the Question — Genesis 3 turns on wordplay, dialogue and a question from God, and each of these has been read closely by Jewish readers.
heb|12|l23-6|9-10|Eden in the Midrash and Beyond — Jewish and Christian traditions read the garden story differently, and midrash fills its gaps in its own way.
heb|12|l23-7|9-10|The Documentary Hypothesis in Brief — Modern academic scholars explain the doubled stories of the Torah as woven from several sources, and Jewish readers have responded in several ways.
heb|12|l23-8|9-10|Reading the Journey Word by Word — Genesis 22 is told in spare, exact words, and its repetitions carry the weight of the story.
heb|12|l23-9|9-10|The Gaps and the Silence — Genesis 22 leaves out what its characters feel, and this silence shapes how the story has been read for centuries.
heb|12|l23-10|9-10|The Ram, the Age and the Test — The end of the Akedah and the age of Isaac are read differently by midrash, Ibn Ezra and Ramban, showing how commentators weigh a text.
heb|12|l23-11|9-10|The Akedah in the Year and in Modern Thought — Genesis 22 is read aloud every Rosh Hashanah and has been argued over by modern thinkers of several traditions.
heb|12|l24-1|9-10|Economy and Silence — Biblical narrative tells its stories with very few words about looks, feelings and motives, and that restraint is a technique, not a lack.
heb|12|l24-2|9-10|The Keyword — A repeated word, the keyword, can tie scenes together and carry a judgment the narrator never states.
heb|12|l24-3|9-10|The Meeting at the Well — Biblical narrative uses type-scenes, set patterns the audience knew, and tells character through how each telling changes the pattern.
heb|12|l24-4|9-10|Two Sons and One Blessing — Genesis 25–27 tells how Jacob gained Esau's birthright and blessing, and readers from Rashi to today have argued about what it means.
heb|12|l24-5|9-10|Leah and Rachel — Genesis 29–30 gives the sisters voices through the names they give their sons, and their story shaped later Jewish memory.
heb|12|l24-6|9-10|The Night at the Jabbok — Genesis 32 tells of a night wrestling match that ends in a wound, a blessing and the name Israel, and readers have never agreed on who the stranger was.
heb|12|l24-7|9-10|Dinah's Chapter — Genesis 34 tells a hard story in which Dinah never speaks, and readers across the centuries have argued about her brothers' violence.
heb|12|l24-8|9-10|Dreams, the Coat and the Pit — Genesis 37 sets the Joseph story in motion with dreams told twice, a coat, a pit and a sale, and its seams have interested both rabbis and scholars.
heb|12|l24-9|9-10|Prison and Palace — Genesis 39–41 moves Joseph from a prison to Pharaoh's court through dreams, and shows repetition with variation at work.
heb|12|l24-10|9-10|Judah's Speech — Judah's speech in Genesis 44:18–34 is the longest in Genesis and the turning point of the story, and Jewish tradition reads it as a model of teshuvah.
heb|12|l24-11|9-10|The Recognition Scene — Joseph's revealing of himself in Genesis 45, and his words in Genesis 50, give the story its own reading of why things happened.
heb|12|l24-12|9-10|As Literature and as Torah — The same chapters can be read as literature and as Torah, and knowing both ways of reading is the goal of this unit.
heb|13|l25-1|9-10|Arriving at the Mountain — Exodus 19 opens the Sinai scene with an offer: if Israel keeps the covenant, it will be a treasured people and a kingdom of priests.
heb|13|l25-2|9-10|The Shape of an Ancient Treaty — Many scholars see in Exodus 19–24 the pattern of ancient Near Eastern treaties between a great king and a lesser one.
heb|13|l25-3|9-10|Thunder, Boundaries and Fear — The Torah frames the giving of the sayings with thunder, fire and a boundary, and the people ask Moses to speak for them.
heb|13|l25-4|9-10|Aseret HaDibrot: Ten Sayings — The Hebrew Bible calls them the ten words or sayings, and gives them twice, in Exodus 20 and Deuteronomy 5, with small differences.
heb|13|l25-5|9-10|Three Ways to Count to Ten — Jews, Catholics and Lutherans, and Protestants and Orthodox Christians divide the same verses into ten in three different ways.
heb|13|l25-6|9-10|Parents, Neighbors and Rest — The second group of sayings deals with people: parents, life, marriage, property, truth and desire, and Shabbat rest extends to servants and animals.
heb|13|l25-7|9-10|A King's Stone — Hammurabi's laws, carved around 1750 BCE, are the best-known of several Mesopotamian law collections that let readers compare the Torah's laws with their neighbors.
heb|13|l25-8|9-10|The Goring Ox, Twice — Exodus and Hammurabi treat the goring ox almost identically until the outcome, where the Torah stones the animal and treats the death as more than a debt.
heb|13|l25-9|9-10|Eye for Eye — Exodus 21:24 states "eye for eye," Hammurabi applies it literally between equals, and rabbinic tradition reads the Torah's words as monetary compensation.
heb|13|l25-10|9-10|The Stranger, the Widow and the Orphan — The Covenant Code protects the stranger, widow and orphan and gives a reason from Israel's own story: you were strangers in Egypt.
heb|13|l25-11|9-10|Sealing the Covenant — In Exodus 24 the covenant is ratified with a reading, a spoken consent, blood and a shared meal, and the people say "we will do, and obey."
heb|13|l25-12|9-10|Shavuot and the Giving of the Torah — The Torah names Shavuot as a harvest festival seven weeks after Passover; rabbinic tradition adds that it is the anniversary of the giving of the Torah.
heb|13|l26-1|9-10|A Herdsman from Tekoa — Amos, a southerner who denied being a professional prophet, preached in the northern kingdom around 760 BCE and was told to go home.
heb|13|l26-2|9-10|Let Justice Well Up — In Amos 5:21–24 God rejects Israel's festivals and songs and demands justice and righteousness instead.
heb|13|l26-3|9-10|A God of All Peoples — Amos judges Israel's neighbors by the same standard as Israel and says God brought other nations out of their lands too.
heb|13|l26-4|9-10|A Marriage as a Parable — Hosea's marriage to an unfaithful wife is presented as a living parable of God's bond with an unfaithful Israel, and of a love that outlasts betrayal.
