heb|13|l26-5|9-10|Mercy, Not Sacrifice — Hosea 6:6 sets chesed, loyal love, above sacrifice, and after the Temple fell the rabbis used the verse to say that acts of kindness atone.
heb|13|l26-6|9-10|What Does the LORD Require? — Micah 6:6–8 answers the question of what to bring to God with three verbs: do justly, love mercy, walk humbly.
heb|13|l26-7|9-10|Worship Without Justice — Isaiah 1 says God is weary of sacrifices and prayers from hands full of blood, and calls the people to seek justice and defend the orphan and widow.
heb|13|l26-8|9-10|The Fast God Chooses — Isaiah 58, read on Yom Kippur morning, says the fast God wants is to free the oppressed, feed the hungry and shelter the poor.
heb|13|l26-9|9-10|Comfort Ye, Comfort Ye — Isaiah 40 opens a new voice of consolation for the exiles, and Jewish tradition reads it on the Shabbat after Tisha B'Av as the first of seven weeks of comfort.
heb|13|l26-10|9-10|Teshuvah: The Prophetic Demand — The prophets' constant call is to return, teshuvah, and rabbinic tradition built the Days of Awe around that idea.
heb|13|l26-11|9-10|Jonah on Yom Kippur Afternoon — The book of Jonah, read on Yom Kippur afternoon, shows a foreign city that repents and a prophet who must be taught mercy, and ends with a question.
heb|13|l26-12|9-10|The Prophets Marching — The prophets live on in the daily prayer book and in modern Jewish social action, and Heschel and King carried their words into the civil rights movement.
heb|14|l27-1|9-10|Lines That Answer Each Other — Hebrew poetry is built from parallelism: a second line that restates, sharpens or completes the first.
heb|14|l27-2|9-10|Three Kinds of Pairing — Readers sort parallel lines into synonymous, antithetic and synthetic pairs, and each kind does a different job.
heb|14|l27-3|9-10|Images, Sounds and the Alphabet — Hebrew poets also work with images, wordplay and acrostics, including alphabet poems like Psalm 119 and Lamentations.
heb|14|l27-4|9-10|Psalm 23 and Psalm 150 — Psalm 23 builds trust from a single image, and Psalm 150 closes the whole book with a summons to praise.
heb|14|l27-5|9-10|Psalm 90 and Psalm 137 — Psalm 90 weighs a short human life against God's eternity, and Psalm 137 is the voice of exiles in Babylon.
heb|14|l27-6|9-10|The Shape of a Lament — Psalms of lament follow a pattern, from complaint to plea to trust, and the turn in the middle is where the poem changes.
heb|14|l27-7|9-10|A Book of Love Poems — The Song of Songs is eight chapters of love poetry between a woman and a man, set among vineyards, gardens and the streets of Jerusalem.
heb|14|l27-8|9-10|Akiva's Reading and the Allegory — Jewish tradition reads the Song as the love between God and Israel, and that allegorical reading is how it entered the synagogue.
heb|14|l27-9|9-10|Psalms in the Siddur — The Jewish prayer book is built largely from psalms, sung at set times: Kabbalat Shabbat, Hallel and the daily services.
heb|14|l27-10|9-10|Writing in the Psalm Form — A student can write in the psalm form by using parallel lines, a governing image and, for a lament, a turn.
heb|14|l28-1|9-10|A Story in Prose, a Poem in the Middle — Job is a poem of argument set inside a short prose story, and the two parts pull against each other.
heb|14|l28-2|9-10|The Friends and the Whirlwind — The friends argue that suffering is deserved; the voice from the whirlwind answers with the size of creation, not with a reason.
heb|14|l28-3|9-10|Jewish Readers of Job — Jewish tradition has read Job as history, as parable and as philosophy, and the Talmud records the argument.
heb|14|l28-4|9-10|Kohelet and the Word Hevel — Ecclesiastes is the speech of Kohelet, and its key word hevel, breath or vapor, appears about 38 times.
heb|14|l28-5|9-10|A Time for Every Purpose — The poem of the seasons in Ecclesiastes 3 lists fourteen pairs of opposites and asks what a person gains from any of them.
heb|14|l28-6|9-10|The Editor's Last Word — Ecclesiastes ends with an epilogue in another voice, and the rabbis debated whether the book belonged in Scripture at all.
heb|14|l28-7|9-10|A Story of Reversals — Esther is a court tale set in Persia in which every threat is turned upside down, and the Hebrew text never names God.
heb|14|l28-8|9-10|Purim: Laughter With a Commandment Inside — Purim turns the book of Esther into a holiday of reading, gifts, food and costumes, each practice rooted in the scroll's own words.
heb|14|l28-9|9-10|The Dark Chapter — Esther 9 describes the Jews killing their enemies, and readers across the centuries have handled the chapter in different ways.
heb|14|l28-10|9-10|Three Books That Argue — Job, Ecclesiastes and Esther each leave the reader to argue, and Jewish tradition kept them for that reason as much as any.
heb|15|l29-1|11-12|The Find at Qumran — The Dead Sea Scrolls, found from 1947 on in caves near Qumran, are the oldest known manuscripts of books of the Tanakh.
heb|15|l29-2|11-12|The Great Isaiah Scroll — The Great Isaiah Scroll from Cave 1, copied around 125 BCE, is the oldest complete copy of any book of the Tanakh.
heb|15|l29-3|11-12|What a Thousand Years Changed — Comparing the scrolls with the medieval text shows a very stable tradition with a small number of real variants worth studying.
heb|15|l29-4|11-12|The Septuagint — The Septuagint is the Greek translation of the Tanakh made in Egypt from about the third century BCE, the first translation of the Bible into any language.
heb|15|l29-5|11-12|Where the Greek Differs — The Septuagint differs from the Hebrew in the length and order of some books, in numbering, in which books it includes and in particular readings.
heb|15|l29-6|11-12|Targums and the Samaritan Torah — The Aramaic Targums and the Samaritan Pentateuch are two more ancient forms of the text, each shaped by the community that used it.
heb|15|l29-7|11-12|Vowels and Accents — The Masoretes of Tiberias, between about the seventh and tenth centuries CE, added vowel points and accent marks to a text that had been written in consonants alone.
heb|15|l29-8|11-12|Counting Every Letter — The masorah is a system of notes that counts letters, words and verses so a copyist can check a manuscript against the tradition.
heb|15|l29-9|11-12|Aleppo and Leningrad — The Aleppo Codex (c. 930 CE) and the Leningrad Codex (1008 CE) are the two most important surviving Masoretic Bibles, and the Leningrad is the basis of modern printed editions.
heb|15|l29-10|11-12|Reading a Famous Variant — Textual criticism compares witnesses to decide which reading is likely earliest, and Deuteronomy 32:8 is a classic case.
heb|15|l29-11|11-12|Bomberg's Bible and the Text Today — The printed Rabbinic Bible of 1525 fixed the Hebrew text for four centuries, and modern editions return to the great codices while keeping the Masoretic tradition.
heb|15|l30-1|11-12|What a Translation Does — A translation is a chain of decisions about words, grammar and sound, so no two translations of a verse say quite the same thing.
heb|15|l30-2|11-12|Isaiah 7:14 in Two Bibles — Isaiah 7:14 shows how one Hebrew word, almah, became a dividing line between Jewish and Christian translations.
heb|15|l30-3|11-12|One Verse in Five Versions — Setting several translations of one verse side by side shows what each translator valued: tradition, sense, grammar or sound.
heb|15|l30-4|11-12|The 1917 Committee — The JPS 1917 translation was the first English Bible produced by a committee of Jewish scholars for American Jews, built on the Revised Version and the Hebrew.
heb|15|l30-5|11-12|The New JPS of 1985 — The NJPS translation, completed in 1985, replaced the old English with modern prose and translated by sense rather than word for word.
heb|15|l30-6|11-12|Buber, Rosenzweig and Fox — Martin Buber and Franz Rosenzweig translated the Bible into German to be heard aloud, and Everett Fox carried their method into English.
heb|15|l30-7|11-12|Chesed and Tzedek — Chesed and tzedek each hold a cluster of English meanings, so translators must choose one and readers must remember the rest.
heb|15|l30-8|11-12|Nefesh, Ruach and Shalom — Nefesh, ruach and shalom are body-and-life words that English tends to make spiritual or narrow, and translators must resist both.
heb|15|l30-9|11-12|Torah: Law or Teaching? — Torah means teaching or instruction, and translating it as law, following the Greek nomos, has shaped how outsiders picture Judaism.
heb|15|l30-10|11-12|The Name of God — Jews do not pronounce the four-letter name of God, and translations from the Septuagint to JPS 1917 have rendered it the LORD rather than transliterating it.
heb|15|l30-11|11-12|Who Translates, for Whom — Every translation is made by particular people for particular readers, and knowing who and for whom is part of reading it well.
heb|16|l31-1|11-12|Two Kinds of Midrash — The rabbis of the early centuries CE read the Torah in two modes, midrash halakhah for law and midrash aggadah for story, and both begin by asking a question of the text.
heb|16|l31-2|11-12|Genesis Rabbah Opens the Torah — Genesis Rabbah reads the first word of the Torah through a verse in Proverbs and pictures the Torah as the blueprint God consulted to build the world.
heb|16|l31-3|11-12|The Talmud Cites a Verse — The Talmud uses set rules of reasoning, such as gezerah shavah and kal va-chomer, to draw law from verses, and it insists that even a heavenly voice cannot overrule a verse's interpretation by the majority.
heb|16|l31-4|11-12|Rashi of Troyes — Rashi (1040–1105) wrote a running commentary that explains the plain sense and selects midrash that fits the words, and his work became the first companion to the text for Jewish readers.
heb|16|l31-5|11-12|Ibn Ezra the Grammarian — Abraham ibn Ezra (c. 1089–c. 1164) read the Torah through Hebrew grammar and plain sense, and left careful hints about verses that puzzled him.
heb|16|l31-6|11-12|Ramban Argues with Both — Ramban (1194–1270) wrote a commentary that openly debates Rashi and Ibn Ezra, reads the ancestors' deeds as signs for their descendants, and adds mystical hints "by way of truth."
heb|16|l31-7|11-12|Radak and the Book of Roots — David Kimhi, Radak (c. 1160–1235), made Hebrew grammar and a dictionary of roots the tools of commentary, and read the Prophets and Psalms with an eye on Christian readings.
heb|16|l31-8|11-12|Maimonides and the Torah's Words for God — In the Guide of the Perplexed, Maimonides argues that the Torah's physical language about God, its hands, eyes and image, must be read as figures of speech, because the Torah speaks in human language.
heb|16|l31-9|11-12|The Zohar and the Four Levels — The Kabbalists of thirteenth-century Spain read the Torah as a garment over hidden meaning and named four levels of reading, peshat, remez, derash and sod, remembered by the acronym PaRDeS.
heb|16|l31-10|11-12|The Mikraot Gedolot Page — The Rabbinic Bible, first printed in Venice in 1517 and reshaped in 1525, put the Masoretic text, the Aramaic Targum and the commentators on one page, and that layout has framed Jewish reading ever since.
heb|16|l31-11|11-12|A Thousand-Year Argument — Jewish interpretation works as a conversation across centuries in which each reader answers earlier ones, and disagreement, when it is careful, is a form of respect for the text.
heb|16|l32-1|11-12|Tanakh and Old Testament — The Jewish Tanakh and the Christian Old Testament contain the same core books but count, order and sometimes translate them differently, and the difference shapes what each reader sees.
heb|16|l32-2|11-12|Order Matters — The Tanakh ends with Chronicles and a call to go up to Jerusalem, while the Christian Old Testament ends with Malachi and the promise of Elijah, and each ending prepares a different next chapter.
heb|16|l32-3|11-12|Isaiah 7:14 in Two Translations — Isaiah 7:14 shows how one Hebrew word, almah, became a dividing line, with Jewish translators reading "young woman," the Greek Septuagint and Christian tradition reading "virgin," and historians asking about King Ahaz.
heb|16|l32-4|11-12|Isaiah 53: Who Is the Servant? — Jewish commentators mostly read the servant of Isaiah 53 as Israel, Christian tradition reads him as Jesus, and historians ask what the exiles heard; each reading answers a different question.
heb|16|l32-5|11-12|Psalm 22: A Cry in Two Traditions — Psalm 22 is read in Jewish tradition as David's prayer and, in the midrash, as Esther's, and in Christian tradition as the words of Jesus on the cross; one verse's translation divides the readings.
heb|16|l32-6|11-12|Genesis 1:26: Let Us Make — The plural "let us make" in Genesis 1:26 is read by the rabbis as God consulting the angels, by many church fathers as a hint of the Trinity, and by historians as a divine council or a plural of majesty.
heb|16|l32-7|11-12|Paris 1240 and Barcelona 1263 — Medieval disputations were staged contests in which Jewish scholars had to defend their texts before Christian judges; Ramban's account of Barcelona shows a reader arguing about what counts as binding.
heb|16|l32-8|11-12|Dialogue Today — From the 1893 World's Parliament of Religions in Chicago to Nostra Aetate (1965) and Dabru Emet (2000), Jews and Christians have moved from staged contests toward voluntary dialogue.
heb|16|l32-9|11-12|Spinoza's Method — Spinoza's Theological-Political Treatise (1670) argued that the Bible should be studied like nature, from its text and history alone, and used Ibn Ezra's hints to ask whether Moses wrote all of the Torah.
heb|16|l32-10|11-12|Wellhausen and the Four Sources — The documentary hypothesis, given its classic form by Wellhausen in 1878, proposed that the Torah was woven from four sources, J, E, D and P; Jewish scholars responded in different ways, and the theory has been revised.
heb|16|l32-11|11-12|Archaeology from Albright to Finkelstein — Archaeology has moved from William F. Albright's confidence that digging would confirm the biblical story to Israel Finkelstein's revised chronology, and the Tel Dan stele shows what an inscription can and cannot settle.
heb|16|l32-12|11-12|Literary and Feminist Readers — Since the 1980s, literary critics such as Robert Alter and feminist readers such as Phyllis Trible have read the Tanakh for its artistry and for its women, asking what the text does rather than only who wrote it.
heb|17|l33-1|11-12|The Siddur and the Shema — The Jewish prayer book, the siddur, is built largely out of verses from the Tanakh, and its center is the Shema from Deuteronomy.
heb|17|l33-2|11-12|Psalms Every Morning — Whole psalms form the backbone of daily and Shabbat prayer, so the book of Psalms is the part of the Tanakh Jews have recited most often.
heb|17|l33-3|11-12|The Song of the Sea Each Day — Exodus 15, the song sung after the crossing of the sea, is recited every morning, so a moment in the story becomes a daily act of memory.
heb|17|l33-4|11-12|Fifty-Four Portions a Year — The Torah is read aloud from a scroll in weekly portions that finish the whole five books every year, so the calendar itself is a reading plan.
heb|17|l33-5|11-12|The Haggadah and a Wandering Aramean — The Passover Haggadah builds its telling of the exodus on Deuteronomy 26:5-8, expounding one short passage line by line.
heb|17|l33-6|11-12|Five Scrolls, Five Seasons — Five short books of the Ketuvim, the Megillot, are each read on a holiday, so the calendar matches a text to a mood.
heb|17|l33-7|11-12|Mezuzah and Tefillin — Two objects, the mezuzah on the doorpost and tefillin on the arm and head, carry Torah passages that Jewish tradition reads as instructions to do exactly that.
heb|17|l33-8|11-12|Blessings Over Bread and Light — Everyday Jewish blessings are short formulas that often quote or answer a verse, so a meal or a Friday evening becomes a small reading of Scripture.
heb|17|l33-9|11-12|Hebrew Revived, Yiddish Retold — Modern Hebrew literature and Zionism drew constantly on the Tanakh, while Yiddish writers retold and joked with its verses for everyday readers.
heb|17|l33-10|11-12|Go Down, Moses: The Text in American Voices — Black spirituals and American civic speech drew on the Tanakh's stories of exodus, justice and liberty, reading them as their own.
heb|17|l33-11|11-12|The Tanakh in Chicago — Chicago's synagogues, schools and the 1893 Parliament of Religions show the Tanakh read, taught and debated in one American city.
heb|17|l34-1|11-12|A Passage and a Question — A good capstone starts with a short passage you can quote in full and one question the passage itself raises.
heb|17|l34-2|11-12|Two Translations Side by Side — Reading your passage in JPS 1917 and one other translation shows where the Hebrew is hard, because that is where the translations differ.
heb|17|l34-3|11-12|Peshat: Structure and Key Words — Before any commentary, map the passage's structure and its repeated words, because that is where the text shows what it is doing.
heb|17|l34-4|11-12|Finding the Gaps — The Tanakh's narrative leaves out motives, ages and feelings, and every reader's interpretation begins in what the text does not say.
heb|17|l34-5|11-12|Rashi and the Midrash — Rashi reads for the plain sense but brings midrash wherever the text's gaps or oddities call for it, and he names which he is doing.
heb|17|l34-6|11-12|Ibn Ezra and Ramban Answer Back — Ibn Ezra and Ramban read the same verses with different tools, grammar and purpose, and often disagree with Rashi and each other.
heb|17|l34-7|11-12|A Modern Reader at the Table — A twentieth- or twenty-first-century reader, literary, historical or feminist, adds a question the medieval commentators did not ask.
heb|17|l34-8|11-12|A Thesis About What the Text Does — A capstone thesis claims something about what the passage does with its words and how readers have responded, and it can be shown from the text.
heb|17|l34-9|11-12|Citing Book, Verse and Commentator — Every claim in a capstone points to a place: book, chapter and verse for the text, the verse commented on for a commentator, and the translation and year for a quotation.
heb|17|l34-10|11-12|Other Readings, Honestly Named — A strong argument states the best other readings fairly and explains what they see, rather than hiding them.
heb|17|l34-11|11-12|Chavruta: Arguing in Pairs — Presenting the capstone in chavruta, a study pair, follows the traditional Jewish way of learning by argument with a partner.
heb|17|l34-12|11-12|The Rubric and the Reflection — The capstone is scored on reading, attribution and argument, and it closes with a reflection on what it means to study a sacred text in a public school.
heb|2|l3-1|k-2|Go to a New Land — The Torah says God told Abraham and Sarah to leave home and go to a new land, and they went.
heb|2|l3-2|k-2|A Promise of a Big Family — The Torah says God promised Abraham and Sarah a very big family.
heb|2|l3-3|k-2|Three Visitors — The Torah says Abraham ran to feed three strangers who came to his tent.
heb|2|l3-4|k-2|Kindness to Strangers — Jewish tradition says Abraham's tent was open on all sides so every guest could come in.
heb|2|l3-5|k-2|Isaac Is Born — The Torah says Sarah had a son named Isaac, and his name means "he laughs."
heb|2|l3-6|k-2|Rebekah at the Well — The Torah says Rebekah gave water to a stranger and his camels, and she became Isaac's wife.
heb|2|l3-7|k-2|The First of the Family — Jewish tradition calls Abraham and Sarah the first mother and father of the Jewish people.
heb|2|l4-1|k-2|Jacob and Esau — The Torah says Isaac and Rebekah had twin sons, Jacob and Esau, who did not get along.
heb|2|l4-2|k-2|The Ladder Dream — The Torah says Jacob dreamed of a ladder to the sky with angels going up and down.
heb|2|l4-3|k-2|Rachel and Leah — The Torah says Jacob worked many years for Laban and married both Leah and Rachel.
heb|2|l4-4|k-2|A New Name: Israel — The Torah says Jacob wrestled all night with a stranger and got a new name, Israel.
heb|2|l4-5|k-2|Twelve Sons and Dinah — The Torah says Jacob had twelve sons and a daughter, and the twelve tribes are named for his sons.
heb|2|l4-6|k-2|The Coat and the Dreams — The Torah says Jacob gave Joseph a special coat, and Joseph's dreams made his brothers jealous.
heb|2|l4-7|k-2|Joseph in Egypt — The Torah says Joseph's brothers sold him, but in Egypt he became a great helper to the king.
heb|2|l4-8|k-2|Brothers Together Again — The Torah says Joseph forgave his brothers, and the whole family moved to Egypt.
heb|3|l5-1|k-2|Slaves in Egypt — The Torah says Jacob's family grew into a people, and a Pharaoh made them slaves.
heb|3|l5-2|k-2|A Baby in a Basket — The Torah says a mother hid baby Moses in a basket, and Pharaoh's daughter found him.
heb|3|l5-3|k-2|A Bush That Did Not Burn Up — The Torah says God spoke to Moses from a burning bush and sent him back to Egypt.
heb|3|l5-4|k-2|Pharaoh Says No — The Torah says Moses told Pharaoh to let the people go, and ten plagues came when he said no.
heb|3|l5-5|k-2|Bread With No Time to Rise — The Torah says the people left Egypt so fast their bread had no time to rise; Jewish families eat matzah to remember.
heb|3|l5-6|k-2|The Sea Opens — The Torah says the sea opened, the people crossed, and Miriam led the singing.
heb|3|l5-7|k-2|Telling It Every Year — Jewish families retell the Exodus story every year at a Passover meal called the Seder.
heb|3|l6-1|k-2|A Camp in the Wilderness — The Torah says the people walked to a mountain called Sinai and camped there.
heb|3|l6-2|k-2|Thunder and a Cloud — The Torah says a thick cloud covered the mountain, and the people heard God speak.
heb|3|l6-3|k-2|One God and a Day of Rest — The first sayings are about one God and about resting on Shabbat.
heb|3|l6-4|k-2|Family, Truth and What Is Yours — The other sayings are about how people treat each other.
heb|3|l6-5|k-2|What a Mitzvah Is — A mitzvah is a commandment in the Torah; Jewish tradition counts many of them.
heb|3|l6-6|k-2|Shavuot: Remembering the Mountain — Jewish families celebrate Shavuot to remember the day the Torah says the ten sayings were given.
heb|3|l6-7|k-2|The Torah Scroll in the Synagogue — In a synagogue, the Torah is a big scroll kept in a special cabinet and read aloud.
heb|4|l7-1|3-5|What the Name Tanakh Means — Tanakh is a name built from the first letters of its three parts: Torah, Nevi'im and Ketuvim.
heb|4|l7-2|3-5|Twenty-Four Books — Jewish tradition counts 24 books in the Tanakh: five in the Torah, eight in Nevi'im and eleven in Ketuvim.
heb|4|l7-3|3-5|What Each Part Holds — The Torah tells the story from creation to Moses, Nevi'im tells of the land and the prophets, and Ketuvim holds songs, sayings and scrolls.
heb|4|l7-4|3-5|The Language of the Tanakh — The Tanakh was written mostly in Hebrew, with a few parts in a related language called Aramaic.
heb|4|l7-5|3-5|Reading From Right to Left — Hebrew is read from right to left, and a Torah scroll is written without vowel marks.
heb|4|l7-6|3-5|A Scribe and a Quill — A Torah scroll is written by hand on parchment by a trained scribe, and it must have no mistakes.
heb|4|l7-7|3-5|The Pointer and the Ark — In a synagogue the Torah scroll rests in a cabinet called the ark, and the reader follows the words with a pointer called a yad.
heb|4|l7-8|3-5|The Weekly Portion — The Torah is divided into weekly portions, called parashot, so the whole scroll is read aloud over one year.
heb|4|l7-9|3-5|Simchat Torah: Ending and Starting — On the holiday of Simchat Torah, Jewish communities finish the last words of the Torah and immediately begin again at the first.
heb|4|l7-10|3-5|Same Books, Different Order — Christians call these books the Old Testament and arrange them in a different order; this course follows the Tanakh's order and the JPS translation of 1917.
heb|4|l8-1|3-5|The Hebrew Names of the Books — Each of the five books of the Torah has a Hebrew name taken from its first words: Bereshit, Shemot, Vayikra, Bamidbar and Devarim.
heb|4|l8-2|3-5|Bereshit: Beginnings and a Family — Genesis tells of creation, the flood, and one family, from Abraham and Sarah to Joseph in Egypt.
heb|4|l8-3|3-5|Shemot: Slaves and a Baby in a Basket — Exodus begins with the family grown into a people, made slaves in Egypt, and a baby named Moses saved from the river.
heb|4|l8-4|3-5|The Night of Passover and the Sea — The Torah says ten plagues struck Egypt, the people left in a hurry on the night of Passover, and the sea opened before them.
heb|4|l8-5|3-5|Sinai and the Mishkan — At Mount Sinai the Torah says God gave the people the Ten Sayings and the teaching, and then they built a tent called the Mishkan to carry with them.
heb|4|l8-6|3-5|Vayikra: Holy Living — Leviticus is mostly laws about offerings, priests and holy living, including the rule to love your neighbor as yourself.
heb|4|l8-7|3-5|Bamidbar: Forty Years of Walking — Numbers tells of counting the people, sending spies, and forty years in the wilderness before a new generation reaches the edge of the land.
heb|4|l8-8|3-5|Devarim: Moses Speaks — Deuteronomy is mostly speeches by Moses, who retells the journey and asks the people to keep the covenant and choose life.
heb|4|l8-9|3-5|Mount Nebo and Joshua — The Torah ends with Moses seeing the land from Mount Nebo, dying there, and Joshua taking over as leader.
heb|4|l8-10|3-5|One Long Journey — Read together, the five books tell one journey, from Ur to Canaan to Egypt to Sinai to the Jordan, held together by a covenant.
heb|5|l9-1|3-5|A New Leader Named Joshua — The book of Joshua says Joshua led the people into the land after Moses died, and was told to be strong and brave.
heb|5|l9-2|3-5|The Walls of Jericho — The book of Joshua says the people marched around Jericho for seven days, and the walls fell.
heb|5|l9-3|3-5|Land for the Tribes — The book of Joshua says the land was divided among the tribes of Israel, and Joshua gave a farewell speech.
heb|5|l9-4|3-5|What a Judge Did — In the book of Judges, a judge was a leader who stepped up in a crisis, not a king.
heb|5|l9-5|3-5|Deborah Under the Palm Tree — The book of Judges says Deborah was a prophetess and judge who led the people with Barak against an enemy army.
heb|5|l9-6|3-5|Gideon's Small Army — The book of Judges says Gideon won with only three hundred men, so the people would trust God and not their numbers.
heb|5|l9-7|3-5|Samson's Strength — The book of Judges says Samson was set apart as a Nazirite from birth and was very strong.
heb|5|l9-8|3-5|Samson's Mistakes — The book of Judges says Samson gave away his secret, lost his strength, and made one last choice.
heb|5|l9-9|3-5|No King in Those Days — The book of Judges ends with a line that says there was no king, and everyone did what seemed right to them.
heb|5|l9-10|3-5|Courage, Trust and Choices — Jewish readers have found lessons about courage, trust and good choices in the stories of Joshua and Judges.
heb|5|l10-1|3-5|A Famine and a Journey — The book of Ruth says a family left Bethlehem in a famine, and Naomi came back with only Ruth beside her.
heb|5|l10-2|3-5|Where You Go, I Will Go — Ruth's promise to Naomi is one of the best-known lines in the Tanakh, and Jewish tradition reads it as a choice to join the people of Israel.
heb|5|l10-3|3-5|Gleaning in the Fields — The book of Ruth says Ruth gathered leftover grain, a practice the Torah set up to feed the poor, in the field of a man named Boaz.
heb|5|l10-4|3-5|Boaz the Redeemer — The book of Ruth says Boaz acted as a redeemer, married Ruth, and their great-grandson was King David.
heb|5|l10-5|3-5|Ruth and Shavuot — Jewish tradition reads the book of Ruth on Shavuot, the spring harvest holiday that also remembers the giving of the Torah.
heb|5|l10-6|3-5|Hannah's Prayer — The first book of Samuel says Hannah prayed quietly for a child, and the rabbis made her prayer a model for how to pray.
heb|5|l10-7|3-5|A Voice in the Night — The text says young Samuel heard a voice calling his name at night, and Eli taught him how to answer.
heb|5|l10-8|3-5|Samuel the Judge — The text says Samuel was the last of the judges, traveling from town to town to lead the people.
heb|5|l10-9|3-5|Give Us a King — The book of 1 Samuel says the people asked Samuel for a king like other nations, and Samuel warned them what a king would take.
heb|5|l10-10|3-5|Chesed, the Thread — The Hebrew word chesed, loyal kindness, runs through the stories of Ruth, Hannah and Samuel.
heb|6|l11-1|3-5|The People Want a King — The book of Samuel says the people asked for a king, and Samuel warned them what a king would cost.
heb|6|l11-2|3-5|Saul, the Tall, Shy King — Saul, the first king, started strong but lost his way, and the text says Samuel told him the crown would pass to another.
heb|6|l11-3|3-5|The Youngest Son Is Chosen — The text says Samuel went to Bethlehem to anoint a new king, and God chose the youngest son, a shepherd.
heb|6|l11-4|3-5|David and Goliath — The story of David and Goliath says a small shepherd with a sling beat a giant soldier, because he trusted and used what he knew.
heb|6|l11-5|3-5|David and Jonathan — The text says Saul's son Jonathan loved David like his own soul and protected him, even though David would be king instead of him.
heb|6|l11-6|3-5|A City for the King — The text says David became king of all Israel and made Jerusalem his capital, and Jewish tradition remembers it as the City of David.
heb|6|l11-7|3-5|Nathan and the Poor Man's Lamb — The text says David did a great wrong, and the prophet Nathan used a story to make the king see it and say he had sinned.
heb|6|l11-8|3-5|David in the Psalms and in Prayer — Jewish tradition connects David with the Psalms, and some of those songs are said in synagogues every day.
heb|6|l11-9|3-5|Solomon Asks for Wisdom — The text says Solomon asked God for an understanding heart, and the story of two mothers shows his wisdom at work.
heb|6|l11-10|3-5|The Temple in Jerusalem — The text says Solomon built the first Temple in Jerusalem, a stone house for the Ark, and it stood for about 400 years.
heb|6|l11-11|3-5|One Kingdom Becomes Two — After Solomon died, the text says the kingdom split into Israel in the north and Judah in the south.
heb|6|l12-1|3-5|A Messenger, Not a Fortune Teller — In the Tanakh a prophet, a navi, is a person who brings a message from God, usually one that people do not want to hear.
heb|6|l12-2|3-5|Elijah on Mount Carmel — The text says Elijah challenged the prophets of Baal on Mount Carmel to show which god would answer with fire.
heb|6|l12-3|3-5|The Still Small Voice — After his victory Elijah ran away afraid, and the text says God spoke to him not in wind, earthquake or fire, but in a still small voice.
heb|6|l12-4|3-5|Elisha Picks Up the Cloak — The text says Elisha took up Elijah's cloak and his work, and Jewish tradition still sets a place for Elijah at the Passover Seder.
heb|6|l12-5|3-5|Amos: Be Fair to the Poor — Amos, a shepherd from the south, said that worship means nothing if people cheat the poor; the text says justice should flow like water.
heb|6|l12-6|3-5|Isaiah in Jerusalem — Isaiah spoke in Jerusalem and said the same thing as Amos: care for the orphan and widow, and a day will come when swords become plows.
heb|6|l12-7|3-5|A Prophet Runs Away — The book of Jonah says a prophet ran from his message, was swallowed by a great fish and prayed from inside it.
heb|6|l12-8|3-5|Nineveh Says Sorry — The text says all of Nineveh, from the king down, turned back from evil, and Jonah was angry that God forgave them.
heb|6|l12-9|3-5|Jonah on Yom Kippur — Jewish communities read the whole book of Jonah on Yom Kippur, the Day of Atonement, because it is a story about turning back.
heb|6|l12-10|3-5|Jeremiah Warns the City — Jeremiah told Jerusalem that Babylon would come, and the text says people put him in a pit for saying it.
heb|6|l12-11|3-5|Far from Home: The Exile — In 586 BCE Babylon burned the Temple and took many people away; the text says they wept by the rivers of Babylon and did not forget Jerusalem.
heb|7|l13-1|3-5|Tehillim: 150 Songs — The book of Psalms is a collection of 150 songs and poems, and Jewish tradition calls it Tehillim, or praises.
heb|7|l13-2|3-5|The Shepherd Song — Psalm 23 pictures God as a shepherd who stays with the sheep, and people have sung it for comfort for a very long time.
heb|7|l13-3|3-5|Songs of Thanks and Praise — Psalms like 100 and 150 tell everyone and everything to praise God with singing and music.
heb|7|l13-4|3-5|Two Lines, One Idea — Hebrew poetry often says one idea twice in two different ways, and this is called parallelism.
heb|7|l13-5|3-5|A Psalm for Every Day — Jewish tradition gives each day of the week its own psalm, and the psalms give words to every feeling.
heb|7|l13-6|3-5|Mishlei: Short Wise Sayings — The book of Proverbs is a collection of short sayings that teach how to live wisely, and tradition connects it with King Solomon.
heb|7|l13-7|3-5|Wisdom Is a Tree of Life — Proverbs pictures wisdom as a woman calling in the street and as a tree of life, and Jewish prayer still uses these words.
heb|7|l13-8|3-5|Job Asks Why — The book of Job tells of a good man who loses everything and asks why bad things happen to good people.
heb|7|l13-9|3-5|Friends, Questions and a Storm — Job's friends say he must have done wrong, but the book ends with God speaking from a storm and asking Job questions instead of giving easy answers.
heb|7|l13-10|3-5|The Song of Songs — The Song of Songs is a love poem full of gardens and springtime, and Jewish tradition reads it on Passover.
heb|7|l14-1|3-5|A Queen in Persia — The book of Esther tells how a Jewish queen in Persia and her cousin Mordecai saved their people from Haman's plan.
heb|7|l14-2|3-5|For Such a Time as This — Mordecai's words to Esther ask whether she became queen so she could speak up, and readers have long noticed that God's name is never written in the book.
heb|7|l14-3|3-5|Purim: Reading the Megillah — On Purim, Jews read the Megillah, make noise at Haman's name, share treats, give to the poor and dress up.
heb|7|l14-4|3-5|Four Friends Far From Home — The book of Daniel tells of young Jews in Babylon who keep their ways while serving a foreign king.
heb|7|l14-5|3-5|The Lions' Den — Daniel keeps praying even when a new law forbids it, and the story says he is kept safe in the lions' den.
heb|7|l14-6|3-5|Lamentations and Tisha B'Av — Lamentations is a sad poem about Jerusalem after Babylon burned the Temple, and Jews read it on Tisha B'Av.
heb|7|l14-7|3-5|Ecclesiastes and Sukkot — Ecclesiastes says that life is short like a breath and that there is a time for everything, and Jews read it on Sukkot.
heb|7|l14-8|3-5|Cyrus Opens the Door — The book of Ezra says King Cyrus of Persia let the exiles go home to Jerusalem to rebuild the Temple.
heb|7|l14-9|3-5|Nehemiah's Wall and a Big Reading — Nehemiah led the people to rebuild Jerusalem's wall, and Ezra read the Torah aloud so that everyone could understand it.
heb|7|l14-10|3-5|The Last Book: Let Him Go Up — Chronicles retells the whole story and ends the Tanakh with Cyrus's call to go up to Jerusalem.
heb|8|l15-1|6-8|Torah, Nevi'im, Ketuvim — The Tanakh is three collections in one book: Torah (Teaching), Nevi'im (Prophets) and Ketuvim (Writings), twenty-four books in all.
heb|8|l15-2|6-8|Two Orders, Two Endings — Christians read the same books in a different order and count them differently, so the Old Testament ends with Malachi while the Tanakh ends with Chronicles.
heb|8|l15-3|6-8|How the Canon Settled — The Tanakh's list of books, its canon, formed in stages: Torah first, then Prophets, then Writings, and tradition remembers the rabbis at Yavneh discussing the last books.
heb|8|l15-4|6-8|Hebrew, Right to Left — Almost all of the Tanakh is written in Hebrew, read from right to left, in an alphabet of 22 letters that originally had no vowels.
heb|8|l15-5|6-8|Aramaic Pages and Targums — A few parts of the Tanakh are in Aramaic, a related language many Jews spoke after the exile, and later Aramaic translations called Targums helped listeners follow the Hebrew.
heb|8|l15-6|6-8|Adding the Vowels — Between about the 7th and 10th centuries CE, Jewish scholars called Masoretes added vowel points and accent marks so the text would be read the same way everywhere.
heb|8|l15-7|6-8|Notes in the Margins — The masorah is a set of margin notes that count letters and words and flag unusual spellings, so copyists could catch any change.
heb|8|l15-8|6-8|Two Famous Codices — The Aleppo Codex (c. 930 CE) and the Leningrad Codex (1008 CE) are the oldest and most complete Masoretic Bibles, and printed Hebrew Bibles today are based on them.
heb|8|l15-9|6-8|How a Torah Scroll Is Made — A Torah scroll is handwritten by a trained scribe on parchment from a kosher animal, with a quill and special ink, following rules that have changed little in a thousand years.
heb|8|l15-10|6-8|Checking and Retiring a Scroll — A scroll with an error or damage is unfit for public reading until fixed, and worn-out scrolls are not thrown away but stored or buried with respect.
heb|8|l15-11|6-8|Chanting the Text — The Tanakh is chanted, not just read, using melodies fixed by the te'amim, and the tunes differ by community and by the book being read.
heb|8|l16-1|6-8|The Weekly Portion — The Torah is divided into 54 portions, one read each Shabbat, so the whole Torah is read aloud in a year and begun again on Simchat Torah.
heb|8|l16-2|6-8|A Reading from the Prophets — After the Torah portion, a passage from the Prophets called the haftarah is chanted, chosen to echo the portion or the holiday.
heb|8|l16-3|6-8|Reading Together — The Torah is read in community: a quorum of ten, members called up in turn, and young people reading for the first time at a bar or bat mitzvah.
heb|8|l16-4|6-8|What Midrash Is — Midrash is the rabbis' way of searching a text, filling its gaps with stories and explanations that grow out of its words.
heb|8|l16-5|6-8|Abraham and the Idols — The famous story of young Abraham smashing his father's idols is not in the Torah; it is a midrash that answers the question of why God chose him.
heb|8|l16-6|6-8|Many Answers, One Verse — The rabbis often preserved several conflicting midrashim on one verse, treating the text as rich enough to carry more than one meaning.
heb|8|l16-7|6-8|Peshat and Derash — Peshat is the plain sense of a verse in its context; derash is the meaning a reader searches out beyond it; Jewish tradition values both.
heb|8|l16-8|6-8|Remez, Sod and the Orchard — Later Jewish readers named four levels, peshat, remez, derash and sod, whose first letters spell pardes, orchard, and used them to organize different ways of reading.
heb|8|l16-9|6-8|Rashi of Troyes — Rashi (1040–1105), a rabbi in northern France, wrote the most widely studied Jewish commentary on the Tanakh, mixing plain sense with chosen midrash.
heb|8|l16-10|6-8|The Page That Argues — The Mikraot Gedolot page prints the verse with Targum and several commentators around it, and the Talmud's habit of citing verses shows how the rabbis anchored every idea in Scripture.
heb|8|l16-11|6-8|Arguing Is Respect — Jewish tradition treats careful argument with and about the text as a form of honoring it, from Abraham questioning God to the rabbis ruling that the Torah is not in heaven.
heb|9|l17-1|6-8|Two Ways to Tell a Beginning — Genesis opens with two accounts of creation, and readers have long noticed that they tell the story in different orders and with different names for God.
heb|9|l17-2|6-8|The Garden and the First Brothers — Genesis 2 through 4 tells of a garden, a choice, and the first murder, and Jewish readers have drawn lessons about responsibility from each scene.
heb|9|l17-3|6-8|Noah's Flood and Gilgamesh's Flood — Genesis tells of a great flood, and an older Mesopotamian poem tells one too; comparing them shows what is shared and what Genesis says differently.
heb|9|l17-4|6-8|Babel and the Shape of Genesis — The tower of Babel ends the first part of Genesis, and the book's own structure shows a shift from the whole world to one family.
heb|9|l17-5|6-8|Abraham, Sarah and the Call — Genesis 12 begins with a command to go and a promise of land, people and blessing, and this covenant is the thread the rest of the Tanakh follows.
heb|9|l17-6|6-8|Hagar and Ishmael — Genesis gives Hagar, Sarah's Egyptian servant, her own encounter with God and her own promise, and readers across traditions have treated her story with care.
heb|9|l17-7|6-8|The Akedah: Reading the Silence — Genesis 22 tells the binding of Isaac in very few words, and Jewish tradition has read its gaps through midrash, liturgy and argument for two thousand years.
heb|9|l17-8|6-8|Rebekah, Jacob and Esau — Genesis 25 to 28 tells how the younger twin, Jacob, gets the birthright and the blessing, and readers have long debated how the text judges him.
heb|9|l17-9|6-8|Leah, Rachel and a Night of Wrestling — Jacob's two wives and their children become the twelve tribes, and a night of wrestling gives Jacob and the whole people the name Israel.
heb|9|l17-10|6-8|Joseph: A Story With a Shape — The Joseph story in Genesis 37–50 reads like a short novel, with dreams, a pit, a prison, a palace, and a scene where brothers finally recognize each other.
heb|9|l17-11|6-8|What Archaeology Can and Cannot Say — Historians place Genesis in the world of the ancient Near East and can describe that world, but they cannot confirm or deny the lives of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob.
heb|9|l18-1|6-8|Slavery and a Baby in a Basket — Exodus opens with the descendants of Jacob enslaved in Egypt and with five women whose choices save the baby who will become Moses.
heb|9|l18-2|6-8|The Name at the Bush — At a burning bush, Exodus says God calls Moses and gives a name that Jewish tradition treats with great care.
heb|9|l18-3|6-8|Ten Plagues and the First Passover — Exodus 7 through 12 tells of ten plagues and a night of departure, and Jewish families still mark that night each year with the Passover Seder.
heb|9|l18-4|6-8|The Sea and the Song — Exodus 14 and 15 tell of the crossing of the sea and give the oldest-sounding poetry in the book, which Jewish prayer still repeats every day.
heb|9|l18-5|6-8|Sinai and the Ten Sayings — At Sinai, Exodus says the people hear ten sayings, and Jewish, Catholic and Protestant traditions count the same words in three different ways.
heb|9|l18-6|6-8|The Covenant Code — Exodus 21 through 23 is a collection of laws for daily life, and rabbinic tradition reads even its harshest-sounding lines in a specific way.
heb|9|l18-7|6-8|The Golden Calf and the Thirteen Attributes — While Moses is on the mountain, the people make a golden calf, and the story that follows gives Jewish prayer its most repeated words about mercy.
heb|9|l18-8|6-8|Why Half the Book Is a Tent — Thirteen chapters of Exodus describe a portable sanctuary in detail, and readers have asked what that emphasis is meant to show.
heb|9|l18-9|6-8|The Haggadah: A Book That Retells a Book — Passover turns the Exodus story into something Jewish families do each year, and the Haggadah has grown over centuries as the script for that night.
heb|9|l18-10|6-8|Go Down, Moses — Enslaved African Americans took the Exodus story as their own, and its words became spirituals, sermons and a language of freedom in American history.
heb|9|l18-11|6-8|Exodus and the Historians — Historians can describe the Egypt of the story and note what is missing from the record, but they cannot settle whether the exodus happened as the Torah tells it.
qur|1|l1-1|k-2|The Holy Book of Muslims — The Qur'an is the holy book of people called Muslims.
qur|1|l1-2|k-2|Words from God, Muslims Believe — Muslims believe the words of the Qur'an came from God to a man named Muhammad.
qur|1|l1-3|k-2|Written in Arabic — The Qur'an is written in a language called Arabic.
qur|1|l1-4|k-2|Reading from Right to Left — Arabic is read from right to left, so a Qur'an opens from the other side.
qur|1|l1-5|k-2|A High Shelf and Clean Hands — Many Muslim families keep the Qur'an high up, wrapped or in a case, and wash before reading.
qur|1|l1-6|k-2|A Book That Is Sung — The Qur'an is read out loud, and the reading often sounds like singing.
qur|1|l1-7|k-2|Muslim Families in Chicago — Many Muslim families live in Chicago, and the Qur'an is read in homes all over the city.
qur|1|l1-8|k-2|Saying It the Fair Way — We say "Muslims believe" and "the Qur'an says" so everyone is treated fairly.
qur|1|l2-1|k-2|A Chapter Is a Surah — The Qur'an has 114 chapters, and each chapter is called a surah.
qur|1|l2-2|k-2|A Line Is an Ayah — Each line of a surah is called an ayah, a word that also means "sign."
qur|1|l2-3|k-2|The Opening, Seven Short Lines — The first surah is Al-Fatiha, The Opening, and it has just seven ayahs.
qur|1|l2-4|k-2|The Cow and the Bee — Some surahs are named for animals, like The Cow and The Bee.
qur|1|l2-5|k-2|The Ant and the Elephant — The Ant tells of an ant who warned her friends, and The Elephant tells of an army that was stopped.
qur|1|l2-6|k-2|Long Ones First, Short Ones Last — The long surahs are near the front of the Qur'an and the short ones are near the back.
qur|1|l2-7|k-2|A Shelf of Drawers — The Qur'an is like a shelf of 114 drawers, and children open the small drawers first.
qur|10|l19-1|6-8|Tawhid in Four Lines — Tawhid, the belief that God is one and has no partner, is the center of the Qur'an, and Surah 112 states it in four short verses.
qur|10|l19-2|6-8|The Ninety-Nine Names — Muslims describe God through names found in the Qur'an, traditionally counted as ninety-nine, and the names of mercy come first and most often.
qur|10|l19-3|6-8|The Throne Verse — Ayat al-Kursi, the Throne Verse (2:255), gathers the Qur'an's picture of God into one verse that Muslims memorize and recite for protection.
qur|10|l19-4|6-8|The Heavens and the Earth — The Qur'an describes God creating the heavens and the earth in six days, with no rest afterward, and describes creation as ongoing.
qur|10|l19-5|6-8|Signs in Nature — The Qur'an calls things in nature ayat, signs, and asks its hearers to look at them as evidence of one Creator.
qur|10|l19-6|6-8|Angels and Jinn — The Qur'an describes angels as God's messengers made of light and jinn as unseen beings made of fire who, like humans, can choose.
qur|10|l19-7|6-8|One Message, Many Messengers — The Qur'an names about twenty-five prophets, most of them figures from the Bible, and says they all taught the same message of one God.
qur|10|l19-8|6-8|Torah, Psalms, Gospel, Qur'an — The Qur'an names earlier scriptures, the Torah, the Psalms and the Gospel, and says it confirms them while Muslims believe it also corrects and completes them.
qur|10|l19-9|6-8|Judgment and the Scales — The Qur'an describes a Day of Judgment when the dead rise, deeds are weighed, and each person answers for what they did.
qur|10|l19-10|6-8|Gardens and Fire — The Qur'an describes paradise as gardens with rivers and hell as fire, and Muslim readers have taken these pictures both literally and as images.
qur|10|l19-11|6-8|Mercy Above All — Mercy is the attribute of God the Qur'an repeats most, and Muslim readers hold that it frames every other theme, including judgment.
qur|10|l20-1|6-8|Justice, Even Against Yourself — The Qur'an commands justice and honest witness even against oneself and one's family, and names justice among God's own attributes.
qur|10|l20-2|6-8|Parents, Neighbors, Honest Words — The Qur'an ties worship of God directly to kindness to parents, care for neighbors and honest speech.
qur|10|l20-3|6-8|Orphans and the Poor — Care for orphans and the poor runs through the earliest surahs, where ignoring them is treated as denying religion itself.
qur|10|l20-4|6-8|Zakat and Sadaqah — The Qur'an pairs prayer with zakat, a required share of wealth for the poor, and praises sadaqah, giving beyond what is required.
qur|10|l20-5|6-8|Family Law in Outline — The Qur'an sets rules for marriage, divorce and inheritance that Muslim jurists developed into detailed law, and that Muslims read differently today.
qur|10|l20-6|6-8|Halal and Haram — The Qur'an names a short list of forbidden foods and drinks, and Muslim jurists built the categories of halal and haram around it.
qur|10|l20-7|6-8|Fight Those Who Fight You — The Qur'an's verses on fighting came in Medina during a war with Mecca, and mainstream scholars read them with their limits and occasions.
qur|10|l20-8|6-8|No Compulsion in Religion — Verse 2:256 says there is no compulsion in religion, and Muslim scholars have read it as a rule against forced conversion.
qur|10|l20-9|6-8|One Life, All of Humanity — Verse 5:32 says that killing one person is like killing all people and saving one is like saving all, and readers debate what its context adds.
qur|10|l20-10|6-8|Patience and Forgiveness — The Qur'an praises sabr, patience, and forgiveness, and tells believers to answer evil with something better.
qur|10|l20-11|6-8|Qur'an, Hadith and Fiqh — Islamic law rests on three layers, the Qur'an, the hadith about Muhammad's Sunnah, and the reasoning of jurists, and each layer has a different authority.
qur|10|l20-12|6-8|Reading a Rule in Context — Muslim scholars read a legal verse with its occasion, its surrounding verses, related verses and the hadith, and disagree when those pull in different directions.
qur|11|l21-1|6-8|Reports About the Prophet — A hadith is a report of what Muhammad said, did or allowed, passed down by a chain of named people.
qur|11|l21-2|6-8|Bukhari, Muslim and the Sifting — In the 800s, collectors such as al-Bukhari and Muslim sifted huge numbers of reports and kept the ones they judged sound.
qur|11|l21-3|6-8|The Sunnah as Example — The Sunnah is the Prophet's way of living, known through the hadith, and Muslims use it to fill in what the Qur'an says briefly.
qur|11|l21-4|6-8|What Tafsir Is — Tafsir is commentary on the Qur'an, and al-Tabari's tenth-century commentary set the model of gathering reports and then judging between them.
qur|11|l21-5|6-8|Ibn Kathir and Qur'an by Qur'an — Ibn Kathir's fourteenth-century tafsir explains a verse first with other verses, then with hadith, and is still widely read today.
qur|11|l21-6|6-8|Clear Verses and Ambiguous Verses — Surah 3:7 says some verses are clear and others are ambiguous, and Muslims have long debated how to read the ambiguous ones.
qur|11|l21-7|6-8|Abrogation: A Debated Tool — Naskh, the idea that a later verse can replace an earlier ruling, is a tool some scholars use widely and others use hardly at all.
qur|11|l21-8|6-8|Sunni and Shia Readings — Sunni and Shia Muslims read the same Qur'an but differ on who may interpret it and on the meaning of certain verses.
qur|11|l21-9|6-8|Sufi Readings: The Inner Sense — Sufi readers look for an inner meaning beneath a verse's plain sense, and many hold that the two levels go together.
qur|11|l21-10|6-8|The Ulama and the Schools of Law — The ulama are Islam's trained scholars, and their schools of law turned the Qur'an and Sunnah into rules for daily life in more than one way.
qur|11|l21-11|6-8|One Verse, Two Commentaries — Reading a verse with two commentaries side by side shows that interpretation is a conversation across centuries, not a single verdict.
qur|11|l22-1|6-8|A Language Travels with a Book — Because Muslims recite the Qur'an in Arabic, the Arabic language and alphabet spread with Islam far beyond Arabia.
qur|11|l22-2|6-8|Calligraphy and Pattern — Because writing the Qur'an beautifully became a sacred art, calligraphy and geometric pattern became the main visual arts of the Muslim world.
qur|11|l22-3|6-8|The Mosque as a House of the Book — A mosque is built around the Qur'an: its niche points toward Mecca, its pulpit carries the sermon, and its walls carry the text.
qur|11|l22-4|6-8|Grammar for a Sacred Text — The need to read and recite the Qur'an correctly drove Muslims to write the first full grammar of Arabic.
qur|11|l22-5|6-8|Stars, Calendars and the House of Wisdom — Practical needs set by the Qur'an, the lunar calendar and the direction of prayer, helped drive Muslim work in astronomy and mathematics.
qur|11|l22-6|6-8|Madrasa: Schools of the Book — The madrasa, a school for Qur'an, hadith and law, spread across the Muslim world and trained the scholars who interpreted the text.
qur|11|l22-7|6-8|One Book, Many Tongues — From Persia to Indonesia, Muslims kept the Qur'an in Arabic but explained and translated it in their own languages.
qur|11|l22-8|6-8|From Latin to English — The Qur'an was first translated into Latin in 1143 and into English from Arabic in 1734, at first by outsiders who wanted to argue with it.
qur|11|l22-9|6-8|Muslim Translators in English — In the twentieth century Muslims began translating the Qur'an into English themselves, and Pickthall (1930) and Yusuf Ali (1934) became the most read.
qur|11|l22-10|6-8|Muslims in Chains — Tens of thousands of enslaved Africans brought to America were Muslims, and a few left writings that show the Qur'an lived in their memory.
qur|11|l22-11|6-8|Jefferson's Qur'an and the 1893 Parliament — The Qur'an entered American public life through a founder's library, a world's fair in Chicago, and later through the oath of a congressman.
qur|11|l22-12|6-8|The Qur'an in Chicago Today — Chicago's Muslim community is one of the largest and most varied in the country, and the Qur'an is read there in every way this course has described.
qur|12|l23-1|9-10|Seven Verses at the Door — Al-Fatiha, the first surah, is a seven-verse prayer that Muslims read as the doorway to the whole Qur'an.
qur|12|l23-2|9-10|A Prayer Said Every Day — Muslims recite Al-Fatiha in every unit of the daily prayer, so it is the most repeated text in Muslim life.
qur|12|l23-3|9-10|One Surah, Three Translators — Comparing Pickthall, Rodwell and Sale on Al-Fatiha shows how every translation is also an interpretation.
qur|12|l23-4|9-10|A Command to Read — Muslim tradition holds that 96:1-5 were the first verses revealed, in a cave near Mecca, beginning with the word "Read."
qur|12|l23-5|9-10|The Pen and the Rest of the Surah — Surah 96 continues past its famous opening into a warning against a man who forbids prayer, showing the early Meccan pattern of praise, warning and worship.
qur|12|l23-6|9-10|Ad-Duha: The Morning Hours — Surah 93 opens with an oath and speaks comfort to a person who feels abandoned; tradition reads it as consolation to Muhammad after a pause in revelation.
qur|12|l23-7|9-10|The Orphan and the Beggar — Ad-Duha ends by turning comfort into duty: the one who was sheltered must not oppress the orphan or turn away the beggar.
qur|12|l23-8|9-10|Al-'Asr: Three Lines, a Whole Ethic — Surah 103 says in three verses that every person is at a loss except those who believe, do good, and urge each other to truth and patience.
qur|12|l23-9|9-10|Oath, Rhyme and Sound — Meccan surahs share a recognizable style: opening oaths, short rhymed verses, urgent warnings and vivid images of the Last Day.
qur|12|l23-10|9-10|Al-Ikhlas: Saying One — Surah 112 states tawhid, the oneness of God, in four verses that Muslims consider a summary of belief.
qur|12|l23-11|9-10|The Surah as a Whole — Reading a surah as one unit of meaning, and knowing how it is used in prayer, changes how each verse is understood.
qur|12|l24-1|9-10|A Frame Around a Story — Surah 12 opens and closes with verses that say why the story is being told, framing the narrative as a lesson.
qur|12|l24-2|9-10|The Dream and the Pit — The story opens with Yusuf's dream of eleven stars, the sun and the moon bowing to him, which sets his brothers against him.
qur|12|l24-3|9-10|Egypt and the Governor's Wife — In Egypt, the wife of Yusuf's master tries to seduce him; the Qur'an tells the scene with a torn shirt, a witness and a banquet, and the ruler is called a king.
qur|12|l24-4|9-10|Dreams in Prison — In prison Yusuf interprets two prisoners' dreams and then the king's, and asks to be placed over Egypt's storehouses.
qur|12|l24-5|9-10|The Brothers Return — The famine brings Yusuf's brothers to Egypt; he tests them, forgives them, and the family is reunited as his childhood dream comes true.
qur|12|l24-6|9-10|Beside Genesis 37 to 50 — Read beside Genesis, Surah 12 keeps the same plot but shortens, reorders and adds scenes to fit its own purpose.
qur|12|l24-7|9-10|Zakariya's Quiet Prayer — Surah 19 opens with the old priest Zakariya praying in secret for a son and receiving Yahya, a scene shared with Luke 1.
qur|12|l24-8|9-10|Maryam and the Palm Tree — Surah 19 tells of Maryam withdrawing east, receiving news of a son, giving birth alone under a palm tree, and the infant Isa speaking in her defense.
qur|12|l24-9|9-10|Ibrahim, Musa and the Shape of the Surah — After Maryam, Surah 19 gives short scenes of Ibrahim, Musa, Ismail and Idris, tied together by a refrain, then turns to warning and judgment.
qur|12|l24-10|9-10|Allusion, Repetition and Speech — Qur'anic stories assume the audience knows the outline, repeat key phrases and objects, and give most of the action in direct speech inside a moral frame.
qur|12|l24-11|9-10|Muslim and Academic Readings — Commentators, Sufi poets and academic scholars have all read these surahs, each asking different questions of the same text.
qur|13|l25-1|9-10|A Medinan Surah — Al-Baqarah is the longest surah, revealed over several years in Medina, and its voice is the voice of a community learning to live by law.
qur|13|l25-2|9-10|The Opening Lines — Al-Baqarah opens with mysterious letters, a claim about the book itself, and a portrait of three kinds of listener.
qur|13|l25-3|9-10|Reading in Sequence — Al-Baqarah moves from creation and prophets to law and prayer; reading verses in order shows how each section prepares the next.
qur|13|l25-4|9-10|Adam and the Angels — Al-Baqarah tells the story of Adam as the first human, taught the names of things and placed on earth as God's viceroy.
qur|13|l25-5|9-10|The Children of Israel — Al-Baqarah retells Israel's covenant at length, praising its gifts and criticizing its breaking, as a lesson aimed at the new community.
qur|13|l25-6|9-10|Ibrahim and the New Direction — The surah presents Ibrahim as builder of the Ka'bah, then announces the turn from Jerusalem to Mecca as the direction of prayer.
qur|13|l25-7|9-10|Fasting and Pilgrimage — Al-Baqarah sets out fasting in Ramadan and the rules of pilgrimage, presenting both as practices earlier peoples also knew.
qur|13|l25-8|9-10|Family and Debt — Al-Baqarah gives detailed rules on marriage, divorce and lending, including the longest verse in the Qur'an, on writing down a debt.
qur|13|l25-9|9-10|When Fighting Is Allowed — Al-Baqarah's verses on fighting are tied to the war with Mecca and set limits; readers argue about how far those limits reach.
qur|13|l25-10|9-10|The Throne Verse — Ayat al-Kursi, 2:255, is the most recited single verse in the Muslim world; it describes God's knowledge, power and care in one sentence.
qur|13|l25-11|9-10|No Compulsion in Religion — Verse 2:256 says there is no compulsion in religion; commentators have read it as a report of an occasion, a limited rule, or a lasting principle.
qur|13|l25-12|9-10|The Closing Prayer — Al-Baqarah ends with a statement of what the believers accept and a prayer that asks not to be burdened beyond one's strength.
qur|13|l26-1|9-10|Orphans and Inheritance — An-Nisa opens with the care of orphans and sets fixed shares of inheritance, including shares for women, in a society that had few such rules.
qur|13|l26-2|9-10|Marriage and a Hard Verse — An-Nisa's rules on marriage include verses that readers have debated intensely, above all 4:34; the debate is part of the tradition.
qur|13|l26-3|9-10|Justice Against Yourself — An-Nisa 4:135 tells believers to stand firm for justice even against themselves and their families; it is one of the Qur'an's clearest ethical verses.
qur|13|l26-4|9-10|Food and Covenant — Al-Ma'idah is among the last surahs by tradition; it sets food rules, declares the religion complete, and allows the food of the People of the Book.
qur|13|l26-5|9-10|One Life, All Lives — Verse 5:32 says that killing one person is like killing all people and saving one is like saving all; readers ask what its context adds.
qur|13|l26-6|9-10|To Each a Law and a Way — Al-Ma'idah speaks of Jews and Christians in more than one tone, and 5:48 says God could have made all people one community but chose to test them in their differences.
qur|13|l26-7|9-10|Manners for a Community — Al-Hujurat, a short surah of about 630, gives rules of conduct: verify news, make peace, do not mock, do not spy, do not backbite.
qur|13|l26-8|9-10|That You May Know One Another — Verse 49:13 says God made people into nations and tribes to know one another, and that the noblest is the best in conduct; it is the Qur'an's most-quoted verse on human difference.
qur|13|l26-9|9-10|Context and Occasion — Classical commentators read Medinan verses through reports of their occasions; historians weigh those reports and set the surahs in the years 622 to 632.
qur|13|l26-10|9-10|Classical and Modern Readers — On the verses about other faiths, classical commentators and modern scholars often reach different emphases from the same text; both belong to the Muslim tradition of reading.
qur|14|l27-1|9-10|What the Qur'an Assumes You Know — The Qur'an retells stories from the Bible in short, pointed form, often assuming its first hearers already knew the outline.
qur|14|l27-2|9-10|Three Texts and Their Translations — To compare fairly, use translations you can cite: KJV (1611) and JPS (1917) for the Bible, Pickthall (1930) for the Qur'an.
qur|14|l27-3|9-10|Adam: One Garden, Two Emphases — Genesis and the Qur'an both tell of Adam's creation and the garden, but the Qur'an stresses the angels' bow, Iblis's refusal and Adam's forgiveness.
qur|14|l27-4|9-10|Ibrahim: Friend of God, Builder of the Kaaba — Both books make Abraham the father of faith, but the Qur'an adds a Meccan chapter: Ibrahim and Ismail raising the Kaaba.
qur|14|l27-5|9-10|Ismail and Isaac: The Unnamed Son — Genesis names Isaac as the son Abraham was told to sacrifice; the Qur'an leaves the son unnamed, and Muslim scholars came to read him as Ismail.
qur|14|l27-6|9-10|Yusuf: The Best of Stories — Joseph is the one biblical figure whose story the Qur'an tells in full, in a single surah, and comparing it with Genesis shows how the Qur'an shapes a story.
qur|14|l27-7|9-10|Musa: The Most Mentioned Prophet — Moses appears more often than anyone else in the Qur'an, with the burning bush, Pharaoh, the sea and Sinai, told as scenes and speeches rather than one long narrative.
qur|14|l27-8|9-10|Dawud and Sulayman: Psalms, Ants and a Queen — In the Qur'an David and Solomon are prophets and kings who receive a book and command birds and jinn, while the Bible remembers their reigns, their psalms and their faults.
qur|14|l27-9|9-10|Yunus: The Prophet Who Ran — Jonah's story is nearly the same in both books, but the Qur'an stresses his prayer inside the fish and his people's repentance rather than his anger at Nineveh.
qur|14|l27-10|9-10|Maryam: The Only Woman Named — Mary is the only woman the Qur'an names, and Surah 19 tells her story with a birth scene and a speaking infant that the Gospels do not have.
qur|14|l27-11|9-10|Isa: Prophet and Messiah, Not Divine — The Qur'an honors Jesus as a prophet, a word from God and the Messiah, while denying that he is God or God's son, which is the central difference between Islam and Christianity.
qur|14|l27-12|9-10|The Cross: 4:157 and Its Readers — Most Muslims read Qur'an 4:157 to mean Jesus was not crucified but was raised to God, while Christians hold the crucifixion as history and as the center of faith.
qur|14|l28-1|9-10|Torah, Psalms and Gospel in the Qur'an — The Qur'an names the Torah, the Psalms and the Gospel as books God sent down, and presents itself as confirming them.
qur|14|l28-2|9-10|People of the Book — The Qur'an calls Jews and Christians Ahl al-Kitab, People of the Book, and speaks of them in praise, criticism and argument, depending on the passage and its occasion.
qur|14|l28-3|9-10|Tahrif: The Charge of Alteration — The Qur'an says some People of the Book altered or hid words; Muslim scholars have long divided over whether that means the texts were changed or only their meaning.
qur|14|l28-4|9-10|Baghdad: Arguing from Reason — In Abbasid Baghdad, Muslims, Jews and Christians debated scripture in Arabic, wrote refutations of one another, and translated each other's books.
qur|14|l28-5|9-10|Córdoba and Beyond: Polemic and Philosophy — In Muslim Spain, Ibn Hazm attacked the Bible, Judah Halevi defended Judaism, and Maimonides wrote in Arabic; later Europe answered with translation and polemic of its own.
qur|14|l28-6|9-10|Reading the Other's Book for a Purpose — Medieval readers approached other scriptures to find prophecies, to answer arguments, or to borrow methods, and each purpose shaped what they saw.
qur|14|l28-7|9-10|Chicago, 1893: The Parliament of Religions — The World's Parliament of Religions in Chicago in 1893 was the first large public gathering where representatives of many faiths, including Islam, spoke on one stage.
qur|14|l28-8|9-10|Scriptural Reasoning and Reading Together — Since the 1990s, Jews, Christians and Muslims have met in small groups to read short passages from all three scriptures together, a practice called scriptural reasoning.
qur|14|l28-9|9-10|Comparing Without Ranking — Scholars of religion compare scriptures by describing what each says, who reads it and how, and by attributing every claim, never by declaring one book truer.
qur|15|l29-1|11-12|The Word Tafsir — Tafsir is the Muslim discipline of explaining the Qur'an verse by verse, and it began, Muslims say, with the Prophet and his Companions.
qur|15|l29-2|11-12|Tradition and Reasoned Opinion — Classical scholars sorted commentary into two kinds, tafsir by transmitted report and tafsir by reasoned opinion, and argued about how far opinion could go.
qur|15|l29-3|11-12|The Commentator's Toolbox — Classical commentators shared a set of tools: Arabic grammar, the occasions of revelation, the doctrine of abrogation, and the distinction between clear and ambiguous verses.
qur|15|l29-4|11-12|Al-Tabari Gathers the Reports — Al-Tabari's commentary set the model for report-based tafsir: collect every transmitted reading, then weigh them and state a reasoned choice.
qur|15|l29-5|11-12|Al-Zamakhshari and the Grammar of Meaning — Al-Zamakhshari's Kashshaf reads the Qur'an through grammar and rhetoric, and through the rationalist theology of the Mu'tazila, which later readers admired and disputed.
qur|15|l29-6|11-12|Al-Razi Asks Every Question — Fakhr al-Din al-Razi turned tafsir into a place for philosophy and theology, listing objections and answers on each verse in his Mafatih al-ghayb.
qur|15|l29-7|11-12|Ibn Kathir and the Return to Reports — Ibn Kathir explained the Qur'an first by the Qur'an, then by hadith, then by the Companions, and his method became the most widely printed tafsir of modern times.
qur|15|l29-8|11-12|Shia Commentary and the Imams — Shia tafsir shares most tools with Sunni tafsir but gives a special place to the Imams as interpreters, as al-Tabrisi's Majma' al-bayan shows.
qur|15|l29-9|11-12|Sufi Readings and Inner Meaning — Sufi commentators such as al-Qushayri read the Qur'an for an inner meaning alongside the outer one, and later thinkers like Ibn Arabi built whole systems on that reading.
qur|15|l29-10|11-12|One Verse in Four Commentaries — Reading the Light Verse through al-Tabari, al-Zamakhshari, al-Razi and a Sufi reader shows that method, not the text alone, decides what a commentary says.
qur|15|l30-1|11-12|Print, Empire and the Commentary — Printing, European rule and new schools changed who read the Qur'an and why, and modern commentaries answered questions the classical ones never faced.
qur|15|l30-2|11-12|Abduh, Rida and al-Manar — Muhammad Abduh and Rashid Rida's Tafsir al-Manar read the Qur'an as guidance for reform, stressing reason, the general reader and the needs of the age.
qur|15|l30-3|11-12|Qutb and Maududi — Sayyid Qutb and Abul A'la Maududi wrote commentaries that read the Qur'an as a program for society and politics, and their books shaped Islamist movements.
qur|15|l30-4|11-12|Fazlur Rahman's Double Movement — Fazlur Rahman, teaching in Chicago, proposed reading each ruling in its seventh-century context to find its principle, then applying that principle to the present.
qur|15|l30-5|11-12|Iqbal and Reconstruction — Muhammad Iqbal, poet and philosopher, read the Qur'an as a call to dynamic thought and human effort, and urged Muslims to reconstruct their religious ideas.
qur|15|l30-6|11-12|Women Reading the Qur'an — Amina Wadud and Asma Barlas argued that the Qur'an read as a whole supports the equality of women and that classical readings reflected their male authors' world.
qur|15|l30-7|11-12|Science in the Text — Scientific exegesis claims the Qur'an anticipates modern discoveries; its Muslim critics say the text is guidance, not a science book, and that tying it to today's theories is risky.
qur|15|l30-8|11-12|The Translation Debate — Muslims have long held that the Qur'an is untranslatable in the full sense, so English versions are called meanings or interpretations, and each one makes choices readers should notice.
qur|15|l30-9|11-12|The Qur'an on a Screen — Websites and apps have put the Arabic text, recitations, many translations and classical commentaries in every pocket, changing how people study and who can weigh in.
qur|15|l30-10|11-12|Reading in America — Muslims in the United States, diverse in origin and school, read the Qur'an in many ways, and the story of that reading in Chicago goes back to 1893.
qur|16|l31-1|11-12|A Book That Is Not a Code — The Qur'an gives guidance and some rules, but it is not a law code, so Muslim scholars built a method to reason from it.
qur|16|l31-2|11-12|Four Sources, In Order — Sunni usul al-fiqh names four sources of law in rank: the Qur'an, the Sunnah, consensus and analogy.
qur|16|l31-3|11-12|Reading Commands and Prohibitions — Jurists sorted every act into five rulings and read the Qur'an's words closely to decide which one applied.
qur|16|l31-4|11-12|Four Sunni Schools — By about 900, Sunni law had settled into four schools, each named for an early scholar, that differ in method more than in belief.
qur|16|l31-5|11-12|The Ja'fari School and Shia Law — Shia law follows the Ja'fari school, which adds the teaching of the Imams to the sources and gives reason a larger place than analogy.
qur|16|l31-6|11-12|Ijtihad and Taqlid — Ijtihad is a qualified scholar's independent reasoning; taqlid is following a school's settled view, and the balance between them is an old debate.
qur|16|l31-7|11-12|Family, Money and Contracts — Most of the Qur'an's legal verses concern worship, family and fair dealing, and jurists built detailed rules of marriage, inheritance and trade on them.
qur|16|l31-8|11-12|Crime and Punishment: The Hudud — The Qur'an fixes penalties for a handful of offenses, the hudud, and classical jurists surrounded them with strict conditions of proof.
qur|16|l31-9|11-12|The Purposes of the Law — The maqasid theory holds that the law exists to protect faith, life, mind, family and property, and modern reformers use it to argue for change.
qur|16|l31-10|11-12|Sharia in the Headlines and in America — The word sharia means different things to scholars, states and headlines, and Muslim jurists in the United States apply the tradition mostly to personal and community life.
qur|16|l32-1|11-12|Permission and Its Limits — The Qur'an's first verses on fighting frame it as a defense against those who attack, and 2:190 forbids beginning hostilities.
qur|16|l32-2|11-12|The Sword Verse in Context — Qur'an 9:5, the so-called sword verse, addresses treaty-breaking Meccan tribes in 631, and its own surrounding verses set conditions and exceptions.
qur|16|l32-3|11-12|The People of the Book and the Poll Tax — Qur'an 9:29 commands fighting certain People of the Book until they pay the jizya, and jurists built from it the status of protected non-Muslim communities.
qur|16|l32-4|11-12|Incline to Peace: Rules of War — Qur'an 8:61 tells the Prophet to accept peace when offered, and classical jurists built rules of war that limit who may fight, why and whom they may harm.
qur|16|l32-5|11-12|The Meanings of Jihad — Jihad means striving, and the Qur'an and Muslim tradition use it for inner effort, for speaking truth and for fighting under rules, in senses that Muslims weigh differently.
qur|16|l32-6|11-12|Rulers, Consultation and Justice — The Qur'an gives no form of government but praises consultation, commands justice even against oneself, and ties authority to trust.
qur|16|l32-7|11-12|Stewards of the Earth — The Qur'an calls humans God's khalifa, or steward, on earth, sets a balance in creation, and warns against waste, verses that Muslim environmental thinkers now cite.
qur|16|l32-8|11-12|How Extremists Read, and How Scholars Answer — Extremist groups quote verses out of context and bypass the tradition's rules; mainstream scholars answer by restoring the occasion, the surrounding verses and the law.
qur|16|l32-9|11-12|Amman and A Common Word — Two modern documents, the Amman Message of 2004 and A Common Word of 2007, show Muslim leaders publicly stating how they read the Qur'an on who is a Muslim and on love of God and neighbor.
qur|17|l33-1|11-12|The Challenge Verses — In several passages the Qur'an dares its doubters to produce something like it, and Muslims read these verses as the root of the doctrine of i'jaz, the book's inimitability.
qur|17|l33-2|11-12|How the Scholars Argued — Classical scholars gave different answers to what makes the Qur'an inimitable: its arrangement of words, its sound, its knowledge, or God's turning people away from trying.
qur|17|l33-3|11-12|A Book Meant to Be Heard — The name Qur'an means recitation, and Muslim tradition says its first audience judged it by ear, which is why sound has always been part of its meaning.
qur|17|l33-4|11-12|Tajwid, the Rules of Sound — Tajwid is the set of rules for pronouncing the Qur'an, governing the length of vowels, nasal sounds and pauses, which reciters learn from a teacher by ear.
qur|17|l33-5|11-12|The Readings: Hafs and Warsh — The Qur'an is recited in several accepted readings, the qira'at, that differ in small points of pronunciation and wording; two of them are heard most widely today.
qur|17|l33-6|11-12|Murattal and Mujawwad — Reciters perform in two main styles, the measured murattal and the slow, ornamented mujawwad, and the great Egyptian reciters of the twentieth century made both famous through radio and recordings.
qur|17|l33-7|11-12|Competitions and the Hafiz — International recitation competitions, held since the 1960s, honor young hafiz who have memorized the whole Qur'an, and they show how memory, tajwid and voice are judged together.
qur|17|l33-8|11-12|From Hijazi to Kufic — The earliest Qur'an manuscripts use a slanting script called Hijazi and then the angular Kufic, written on parchment, and they show the book becoming a visual object.
qur|17|l33-9|11-12|The Six Pens — From the 900s calligraphers developed rounded scripts governed by proportion, and Naskh, Thuluth and their relatives became the standard hands of the Qur'an for the next thousand years.
qur|17|l33-10|11-12|The Qur'an on Buildings — Since the 600s Muslims have written Qur'anic verses on mosques, shrines and tombs, so that the walls themselves speak the text to anyone who can read them.
qur|17|l33-11|11-12|The Qur'an in Poetry — Persian poets such as Rumi and Hafez wove Qur'anic verses into their poems, and through them the Qur'an reached readers far outside the mosque, including Goethe in Germany.
qur|17|l33-12|11-12|Fiction, Film and the Screen — Novelists and filmmakers have brought the Qur'an to audiences who may never open it, while working within Muslim reluctance to show the Prophet.
qur|17|l33-13|11-12|Listening and Looking as Reading — For Muslims, hearing the Qur'an recited and seeing it written are ways of receiving the text, and a student can learn to notice what each way adds to reading for meaning.
qur|17|l34-1|11-12|Picking Your Ayah — A strong capstone starts with one short passage and one real question that the passage and its readers can help answer.
qur|17|l34-2|11-12|Two Translations Side by Side — Reading a verse in two English translations shows where translators made choices, and those choices point to questions worth asking.
qur|17|l34-3|11-12|Words and Their Roots — Most Arabic words grow from a three-letter root, so looking up the root of a key word shows the range of meanings a translator had to choose from.
qur|17|l34-4|11-12|The Surah Around the Verse — An ayah sits inside a surah with its own shape, setting and themes, so reading the verses around it is the first step in reading it well.
qur|17|l34-5|11-12|The Occasion of Revelation — Reports called asbab al-nuzul tell when and why some verses were revealed, and commentators use them, carefully, to read a verse.
qur|17|l34-6|11-12|The Qur'an Explaining Itself — Classical scholars held that the best explanation of one verse is another verse, so a capstone traces the key words of its ayah across the whole book.
qur|17|l34-7|11-12|The Classical Commentaries — A handful of classical commentaries, Sunni and Shia, are the standard places to see how a verse has been read over a thousand years.
qur|17|l34-8|11-12|A Modern Reader — Modern commentators read the Qur'an with new questions, and adding one modern voice shows how an old verse speaks to a new age.
qur|17|l34-9|11-12|Acknowledging Other Readings — A fair essay names the readings it does not follow and explains them accurately, which makes its own argument stronger.
qur|17|l34-10|11-12|Writing a Thesis — A thesis for the capstone makes a claim about what the verse says and how it has been read, and every part of it can be checked against sources.
qur|17|l34-11|11-12|Citing, Presenting and the Rubric — Clear citations, a short presentation and a shared rubric let others check your work and see how it will be judged.
qur|17|l34-12|11-12|Studying Scripture in a Public School — U.S. public schools may teach about religion, including a sacred text, when the teaching is academic and does not promote or reject any faith.
qur|2|l3-1|k-2|Mecca and the Big Box — Mecca is a desert city with a square building called the Kaaba in the middle.
qur|2|l3-2|k-2|Camels and Caravans — Mecca was a trading city, and lines of camels called caravans came and went.
qur|2|l3-3|k-2|Born Long Ago — Muslims say Muhammad was born in Mecca about 1,450 years ago.
qur|2|l3-4|k-2|Grandfather and Uncle — Muhammad's parents died when he was small, so his grandfather and then his uncle cared for him.
qur|2|l3-5|k-2|Sheep and Quiet Places — As a boy, Muslims say Muhammad cared for sheep and camels and liked quiet places.
qur|2|l3-6|k-2|The Trustworthy — People in Mecca called Muhammad "the trustworthy" because he was honest.
qur|2|l3-7|k-2|Khadijah — Muhammad married a trader named Khadijah, and Muslims remember her with honor.
qur|2|l3-8|k-2|A Cave on a Mountain — Muhammad liked to go alone to a cave on a mountain near Mecca to think.
qur|2|l4-1|k-2|An Angel Comes — Muslims believe an angel named Jibril came to Muhammad in the cave with words from God.
qur|2|l4-2|k-2|The Word "Read" — Muslims say the first word the angel said was "Read!", which in Arabic is Iqra.
qur|2|l4-3|k-2|Afraid and Shaking — Muhammad was afraid, so he ran home to Khadijah, and she comforted him.
qur|2|l4-4|k-2|Khadijah Believes — Muslims say Khadijah was the first person to believe that the words were from God.
qur|2|l4-5|k-2|Words Over Many Years — Muslims believe more words came to Muhammad for about 23 years, a little at a time.
