qur|2|l4-6|k-2|Friends Learn by Heart — Muhammad's friends learned the words by heart and wrote them down, and they became the Qur'an.
qur|2|l4-7|k-2|What a Prophet Is — Muslims call Muhammad a prophet, a person who brings a message from God, and add a blessing after his name.
qur|3|l5-1|k-2|The Bismillah — The Bismillah is a line from the Qur'an that means "In the name of God, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful."
qur|3|l5-2|k-2|When Muslims Say Bismillah — Many Muslims say Bismillah before they start something, like eating, working or going on a trip.
qur|3|l5-3|k-2|Alhamdulillah, Thanks Be to God — Alhamdulillah means "thanks be to God" or "praise be to God," and it is the second line of the Qur'an.
qur|3|l5-4|k-2|Allah Means God — Allah is the Arabic word for God; Muslims believe there is only one God.
qur|3|l5-5|k-2|Peace Be Upon You — As-salamu alaykum is a greeting that means "peace be upon you," and the answer is "and upon you, peace."
qur|3|l5-6|k-2|Be Kind to Parents — The Qur'an says to be kind to your parents and to speak to them gently.
qur|3|l5-7|k-2|Share and Tell the Truth — The Qur'an says to share with people who need help and to say what is true.
qur|3|l6-1|k-2|Nuh and the Big Boat — The Qur'an tells about Nuh (Noah), who built a big boat and was saved from a flood.
qur|3|l6-2|k-2|Yunus and the Big Fish — The Qur'an tells about Yunus (Jonah), who was swallowed by a big fish and then set safe on land.
qur|3|l6-3|k-2|Musa in the River — The Qur'an says Musa's (Moses's) mother put him in a basket on the river, and he was found and kept safe.
qur|3|l6-4|k-2|Musa at the Sea — The Qur'an says the sea opened for Musa and his people, and they walked across to safety.
qur|3|l6-5|k-2|Ibrahim and Ismail Build the Kaaba — The Qur'an says Ibrahim (Abraham) and his son Ismail built the Kaaba, the square building in Mecca.
qur|3|l6-6|k-2|Maryam and Baby Isa — The Qur'an tells about Maryam (Mary) and her baby Isa (Jesus), and a whole surah is named for Maryam.
qur|3|l6-7|k-2|Sulayman and the Ant — The Qur'an says an ant warned the other ants about Sulayman's (Solomon's) army, and Sulayman smiled at her words.
qur|3|l6-8|k-2|Stories Many Families Share — Many stories in the Qur'an are about people the Bible tells about too, so many families share them.
qur|4|l7-1|3-5|Surahs and Ayahs — The Qur'an has 114 surahs (chapters) made of about 6,200 ayahs (verses).
qur|4|l7-2|3-5|Thirty Parts for Thirty Nights — The Qur'an is also split into 30 equal parts, called juz', so it can be read in one month.
qur|4|l7-3|3-5|Why the Long Ones Come First — The Qur'an is arranged roughly from longest surah to shortest, not in the order Muslims believe the verses came.
qur|4|l7-4|3-5|Mecca and Medina — Each surah is labeled Meccan or Medinan, after the city where Muslims believe it was revealed.
qur|4|l7-5|3-5|The Opening — Al-Fatiha, the first surah, is a seven-line prayer that Muslims recite in every daily prayer.
qur|4|l7-6|3-5|The Throne Verse — The Throne Verse (2:255) describes God as the one living, all-knowing ruler, and many Muslims memorize it early.
qur|4|l7-7|3-5|Four Lines on One God — Surah 112, Al-Ikhlas, states in four short lines the Muslim belief that God is one and has no equal.
qur|4|l7-8|3-5|In the Name of God — Every surah but one begins with the Bismillah, a line that names God as gracious and merciful.
qur|4|l7-9|3-5|A Book Meant to Be Heard — The name Qur'an means "the recitation," because the book was heard aloud before it was written.
qur|4|l7-10|3-5|Finding Your Way — With surah numbers, ayah numbers and juz' marks, a reader can find any spot in the Qur'an quickly.
qur|4|l8-1|3-5|Spoken First — Muslims believe the Qur'an was spoken and heard for years before it was gathered into a written book.
qur|4|l8-2|3-5|Saying It Right — Tajwid is the set of rules for reciting the Qur'an correctly, sound by sound.
qur|4|l8-3|3-5|A Whole Book by Heart — A person who has memorized the entire Qur'an is called a hafiz, and there are millions of them around the world.
qur|4|l8-4|3-5|Ramadan Nights — During Ramadan, many Muslims hear the whole Qur'an recited in the mosque, one part each night.
qur|4|l8-5|3-5|The Written Copy — A written copy of the Qur'an is called a mushaf, and Muslims handle it with special care.
qur|4|l8-6|3-5|Dots and Marks — Early Arabic writing had few dots or vowel marks; later scribes added them so every reader could say the words the same way.
qur|4|l8-7|3-5|Writing as Art — Calligraphy, beautiful handwriting, is the main art of the Qur'an, and its styles have names and long histories.
qur|4|l8-8|3-5|Words Instead of Pictures — Most Muslims do not make pictures of God or the prophets, so the words of the Qur'an became the art of mosques and books.
qur|4|l8-9|3-5|The Qur'an in English — The Qur'an has been translated into many languages, and Muslims usually call a translation "the meaning," not the Qur'an itself.
qur|4|l8-10|3-5|Why the Arabic Stays — Muslims everywhere recite the Qur'an in Arabic, because they believe the Arabic words themselves are the revelation.
qur|5|l9-1|3-5|Tribes and Camel Roads — Before Islam, Arabia was a land of tribes, and Mecca was a busy trade city on the camel roads.
qur|5|l9-2|3-5|The Kaaba and Many Gods — Mecca's holy place, the Kaaba, was full of idols before Islam, and people came from far away to visit it.
qur|5|l9-3|3-5|A Boy Called Trustworthy — Muslim tradition tells of Muhammad's early life in Mecca: an orphan, a shepherd, a trader people trusted, and a man who went to a cave to think.
qur|5|l9-4|3-5|Read! — Muslims believe that in about 610, in the cave of Hira, the angel Jibril brought Muhammad the first words of the Qur'an.
qur|5|l9-5|3-5|One God, and Be Fair — The early Meccan surahs teach that there is one God and that people must be fair and care for orphans and the poor.
qur|5|l9-6|3-5|The First Muslims — Muslim tradition names the first believers: Khadijah, the boy Ali, the friend Abu Bakr, and Bilal, who was enslaved.
qur|5|l9-7|3-5|Mecca's Leaders Say No — When Muhammad began to teach in public, the leaders of Quraysh grew angry and made life hard for the Muslims.
qur|5|l9-8|3-5|Safe with a Christian King — Muslim tradition says some early Muslims went to Abyssinia, where a Christian king heard the Qur'an and protected them.
qur|5|l9-9|3-5|The Boycott and the Sad Year — Quraysh cut off Muhammad's clan for about three years, and soon after, Muhammad lost both Khadijah and Abu Talib.
qur|5|l9-10|3-5|The Night Journey — Muslims tell of a night when Muhammad was carried to Jerusalem and up through the heavens, a story linked to Surah 17.
qur|5|l10-1|3-5|An Invitation from the North — People from Yathrib met Muhammad in Mecca, made promises to protect him, and invited the Muslims to their town.
qur|5|l10-2|3-5|The Hijra — In 622 the Muslims moved from Mecca to Yathrib; Muslims call this the hijra, and their calendar counts years from it.
qur|5|l10-3|3-5|A Mosque and New Brothers — In Medina the Muslims built a simple mosque, and the people who moved and the people who helped them became one community.
qur|5|l10-4|3-5|An Agreement for the City — Tradition says Muhammad made a written agreement with the tribes of Medina, Muslim and Jewish, to keep peace and defend the city together.
qur|5|l10-5|3-5|Turning Toward Mecca — In Medina, Muslims believe, a verse told them to face the Kaaba in Mecca when they pray, instead of Jerusalem.
qur|5|l10-6|3-5|Rules for a Community — Many Medinan verses give rules for daily life, like fasting in Ramadan and giving to the poor, because the Muslims now had a city to run.
qur|5|l10-7|3-5|Badr and Uhud — In 624 the Muslims won a small battle at Badr, and in 625 they were hurt badly at Uhud; the Qur'an speaks about both.
qur|5|l10-8|3-5|The Trench and the Treaty — The Muslims dug a trench to save Medina in 627, and in 628 they made a peace treaty with Quraysh at Hudaybiyyah.
qur|5|l10-9|3-5|Back to Mecca — In 630 Muhammad returned to Mecca with a large group, the city gave in almost without a fight, and the idols were taken out of the Kaaba.
qur|5|l10-10|3-5|The Farewell Sermon — On his last pilgrimage in 632, tradition says, Muhammad gave a speech about fairness and equality that Muslims still quote.
qur|5|l10-11|3-5|The Prophet Dies — Muhammad died in Medina in 632; Muslims believe the Qur'an was complete, and Abu Bakr became the first leader after him.
qur|5|l10-12|3-5|Gathering the Book — Muslim tradition says the Qur'an was written down in one book after Muhammad's death, first under Abu Bakr and then in a standard copy under Uthman.
qur|6|l11-1|3-5|What a Prophet Is — Muslims believe God sent many prophets to bring people the same message, and the Qur'an names 25 of them.
qur|6|l11-2|3-5|Stories Shared by Three Books — The Qur'an, the Hebrew Bible and the Christian Bible tell about many of the same people, each in its own way.
qur|6|l11-3|3-5|Adam, the First Person — The Qur'an says God made Adam, taught him the names of things, and told the angels to bow to him.
qur|6|l11-4|3-5|Adam in Two Books — The Bible and the Qur'an both tell about Adam and the garden, but they tell some parts differently.
qur|6|l11-5|3-5|Nuh and the Ark — The Qur'an says Nuh warned his people for a long time, built an ark, and was saved from a flood with those who believed.
qur|6|l11-6|3-5|Ibrahim and the Idols — The Qur'an says Ibrahim broke his people's idols to show they had no power, and God kept him safe from the fire.
qur|6|l11-7|3-5|The Test With His Son — The Qur'an says Ibrahim dreamed he must give up his son, both agreed to obey God, and God gave a ram in the son's place.
qur|6|l11-8|3-5|Building the Kaaba, and Eid al-Adha — The Qur'an says Ibrahim and Ismail built the Kaaba, and Muslims remember Ibrahim's test each year at Eid al-Adha.
qur|6|l11-9|3-5|The Well and Egypt — Surah 12 tells how Yusuf's brothers put him in a well, and how he was carried to Egypt and wrongly put in prison.
qur|6|l11-10|3-5|Dreams, Prison and the King — In prison, Yusuf explained dreams, and the king's dream about cows brought him out to save Egypt from hunger.
qur|6|l11-11|3-5|The Brothers Return — The Qur'an ends Yusuf's story with forgiveness: he tells his brothers there is no blame on them, and the family is together again.
qur|6|l12-1|3-5|A Baby in a Basket — The Qur'an says Musa's mother put him in the river to save him, and Pharaoh's own family took him in.
qur|6|l12-2|3-5|The Burning Bush and Pharaoh — The Qur'an says God spoke to Musa from a fire, gave him signs, and sent him with his brother Harun to Pharaoh.
qur|6|l12-3|3-5|The Sea and the Mountain — The Qur'an says God parted the sea for the Children of Israel, and Musa later received God's words on the mountain.
qur|6|l12-4|3-5|A Surah Named for Maryam — Maryam (Mary) is the only woman named in the Qur'an, and Surah 19 tells about her childhood and her son.
qur|6|l12-5|3-5|The Birth of Isa — The Qur'an says an angel told Maryam she would have a son, Isa, and that he spoke as a baby to defend her.
qur|6|l12-6|3-5|Isa, the Messiah, in the Qur'an — Muslims believe Isa was a great prophet who healed and taught by God's power, but not the son of God.
qur|6|l12-7|3-5|Jews and Christians in the Qur'an — The Qur'an calls Jews and Christians People of the Book, praises some, argues with some, and tells Muslims to speak with them kindly.
qur|6|l12-8|3-5|One Line of Prophets — Muslims believe the prophets from Adam to Muhammad brought one message, and the three religions share many of the same figures.
qur|6|l12-9|3-5|The Seal of the Prophets — Muslims believe Muhammad was the last prophet, and the Qur'an calls him the seal of the prophets.
qur|6|l12-10|3-5|Three Religions, One Timeline — The prophets of the Qur'an can be placed on one timeline that Jews, Christians and Muslims each read in their own way.
qur|7|l13-1|3-5|One God, Not Many — Muslims believe there is only one God, and the Qur'an calls this idea tawhid.
qur|7|l13-2|3-5|Surah Al-Ikhlas Close Up — Surah 112 says God is one, needs nothing, has no children and has no equal.
qur|7|l13-3|3-5|The 99 Names — Muslims call God by many names from the Qur'an, and the best known are the Merciful and the Kind.
qur|7|l13-4|3-5|Honor Your Parents — The Qur'an tells people to be good to their parents, especially when the parents grow old.
qur|7|l13-5|3-5|Honest Words, Kept Promises — The Qur'an says to tell the truth, give fair measure and keep every promise you make.
qur|7|l13-6|3-5|Orphans and the Poor — The Qur'an says again and again to care for orphans, the poor and travelers in need.
qur|7|l13-7|3-5|Do Not Boast — The Qur'an says not to brag, not to strut, and not to look down on other people.
qur|7|l13-8|3-5|A Father and a Son — Surah 31 tells how the wise man Luqman gave his son advice, and Muslims read it as a lesson for all children.
qur|7|l13-9|3-5|Seven Pieces of Advice — Luqman's advice covers one God, parents, prayer, doing right, patience, not boasting and a soft voice.
qur|7|l13-10|3-5|Angels — Muslims believe in angels, and the Qur'an names Jibril (Gabriel), who Muslims believe brought its words.
qur|7|l13-11|3-5|The Day of Judgment and Paradise — Muslims believe that one day every person will answer for what they did, and that good people will live in paradise.
qur|7|l14-1|3-5|Five Pillars Hold Up a House — Muslims describe five main practices as pillars, and each one has roots in the Qur'an.
qur|7|l14-2|3-5|The Shahada: Words of Faith — The shahada says there is no god but God and Muhammad is God's messenger, and saying it makes a person a Muslim.
qur|7|l14-3|3-5|Prayer Five Times a Day — Muslims pray five times a day facing Mecca, and the Qur'an tells them to keep up prayer and which way to face.
qur|7|l14-4|3-5|Zakat: Giving a Share — Zakat is a share of one's savings given each year to people in need, and the Qur'an often pairs it with prayer.
qur|7|l14-5|3-5|Fasting in Ramadan — Surah 2:185 names Ramadan as the month the Qur'an came down, and tells Muslims to fast in it.
qur|7|l14-6|3-5|Hajj: The Journey to Mecca — Muslims who are able travel to Mecca once in their lives for the hajj, which the Qur'an calls a duty to God.
qur|7|l14-7|3-5|Washing Up and the Mosque — Before prayer Muslims wash in a set way called wudu, which Surah 5:6 describes, and many pray together in a mosque.
qur|7|l14-8|3-5|Friday Prayer — On Fridays Muslims gather at midday for a talk and a prayer, as Surah 62:9 tells them to do.
qur|7|l14-9|3-5|Two Eids — Muslims have two main holidays: Eid al-Fitr at the end of Ramadan and Eid al-Adha at the time of the hajj.
qur|7|l14-10|3-5|Halal Food — The Qur'an allows most foods, forbids a few, and Muslims call what is allowed halal.
qur|7|l14-11|3-5|A Muslim Child's Day and Year — For many Muslim children the pillars shape each day and each year, in ways that differ from family to family.
qur|7|l14-12|3-5|Muslims in Chicago and the World — About 1.9 billion people are Muslim, in every part of the world, and Chicago has a large, varied Muslim community.
qur|8|l15-1|6-8|One Hundred Fourteen Chapters — The Qur'an has 114 surahs, arranged roughly from longest to shortest, and each surah is made of ayahs, or verses.
qur|8|l15-2|6-8|How Surahs Got Their Names — Surah names usually come from a striking word or story inside the chapter, and many surahs have more than one name.
qur|8|l15-3|6-8|Parts for Reading: Juz' and Hizb — Besides surahs, the Qur'an is divided into 30 equal parts (juz') so the whole book can be read in a month, and each juz' into halves and quarters.
qur|8|l15-4|6-8|Short Surahs, Strong Beats — Surahs that Muslim tradition places in Mecca tend to be short, rhythmic and urgent, calling people to one God and warning of judgment.
qur|8|l15-5|6-8|Oaths and the Last Day — Many Meccan surahs open with oaths sworn by things in nature, a form early Arabic listeners knew, leading into a warning or a promise.
qur|8|l15-6|6-8|Medina: Law and Community — Surahs placed in Medina are longer and speak in a calmer voice about law, family, the new community and its dealings with other peoples.
qur|8|l15-7|6-8|A Refrain That Repeats — Surah 55, Ar-Rahman, repeats one question 31 times, a refrain that shapes the whole surah like a chorus.
qur|8|l15-8|6-8|Parables and Signs — The Qur'an teaches through parables (amthal), short comparisons drawn from everyday life, and by pointing to signs in nature.
qur|8|l15-9|6-8|The Mysterious Letters — Twenty-nine surahs open with single Arabic letters whose meaning is not known; Muslim scholars have offered many readings and no single answer.
qur|8|l15-10|6-8|Seven Oft-Repeated Verses — Al-Fatiha, seven short verses, opens the book and is recited in every unit of daily prayer; tradition calls it the Seven Oft-Repeated.
qur|8|l15-11|6-8|Why Not in Date Order? — The Qur'an is not arranged in the order Muslims say it was revealed; tradition holds the surah order was fixed early, and scholars have built separate lists of the order of revelation.
qur|8|l16-1|6-8|A Recitation Before a Book — The word Qur'an means recitation; Muslims say its words were spoken and heard for years before they were gathered into a single written book.
qur|8|l16-2|6-8|Memorizers and Scribes — In the Prophet's lifetime, by Muslim tradition, companions memorized the Qur'an and scribes wrote pieces of it on whatever they had, so the text lived in two forms at once.
qur|8|l16-3|6-8|Abu Bakr's Collection — Muslim tradition says the first caliph, Abu Bakr, had the Qur'an gathered into one set of sheets after many memorizers died in battle, c. 633.
qur|8|l16-4|6-8|Uthman's Standard Copy — Around 650, by Muslim tradition, the caliph Uthman had a standard copy made, sent copies to major cities and ordered other versions destroyed, giving the text one form.
qur|8|l16-5|6-8|Old Manuscripts Speak — Early Qur'an manuscripts, such as the Birmingham leaves radiocarbon-dated to the seventh century, let historians check the tradition against physical evidence.
qur|8|l16-6|6-8|The Sana'a Palimpsest — A manuscript found in Yemen in 1972 has an erased lower text with some differences from the standard Qur'an, giving scholars a rare look at an early non-standard copy.
qur|8|l16-7|6-8|Dots and Vowel Marks — Early Arabic script had few dots and no vowel signs; over the first Islamic centuries, scholars added marks so readers who did not already know the text could read it correctly.
qur|8|l16-8|6-8|The Readings: Hafs and Warsh — Within the Uthmanic text, Muslim scholars recognize several accepted ways of reciting, the qira'at; most of the world uses the reading of Hafs, and North and West Africa use Warsh.
qur|8|l16-9|6-8|The Rules of Tajwid — Tajwid is the set of rules for pronouncing the Qur'an exactly, learned from a teacher, so that every sound comes out as tradition says it should.
qur|8|l16-10|6-8|The Cairo Edition — In 1924 a committee in Cairo printed a carefully checked Qur'an in the Hafs reading; it became the model for most printed copies in the world.
qur|8|l16-11|6-8|Sound First, Still Today — Even with printed and digital copies everywhere, Muslims still treat the Qur'an as sound first: memorized, recited aloud and heard.
qur|9|l17-1|6-8|A Land of Tribes and Trade — Arabia around 600 was a land of tribes and trade routes, where a person's safety came from the tribe and a city's wealth came from the caravans.
qur|9|l17-2|6-8|Two Empires and Many Faiths — Arabia lay between the Byzantine and Persian empires, and Jews, Christians and followers of the old Arabian gods all lived there.
qur|9|l17-3|6-8|The Kaaba and Its Gods — Before Islam, the Kaaba in Mecca was a shrine to many gods, and the tribe of Quraysh gained wealth and honor by guarding it.
qur|9|l17-4|6-8|Where the Story Comes From — The story of Muhammad's life comes from the sira, written down more than a century after his death, and from the Qur'an itself.
qur|9|l17-5|6-8|A Merchant of Quraysh — Tradition says Muhammad was born in Mecca c. 570, orphaned young, raised by his grandfather and uncle, and became a trusted merchant married to Khadijah.
qur|9|l17-6|6-8|The Cave and the First Words — Muslims believe the Qur'an's first revelation came to Muhammad c. 610 in a cave on Mount Hira, beginning with the word "Read."
qur|9|l17-7|6-8|The Message and the Opposition — Tradition says Muhammad preached one God, judgment and care for the weak; a few Meccans believed, but the leaders of Quraysh turned against him.
qur|9|l17-8|6-8|Boycott, Abyssinia and the Year of Sorrow — Tradition tells of a boycott, a group of Muslims sheltered by a Christian king in Abyssinia, and the deaths of Khadijah and Abu Talib in c. 619.
qur|9|l17-9|6-8|One God and Nothing Beside Him — The Meccan surahs' central theme is tawhid, the oneness of God, stated most tightly in Surah 112.
qur|9|l17-10|6-8|Judgment and Justice for the Weak — Meccan surahs warn of a Day of Judgment and tie true religion to how a person treats the orphan, the beggar and the poor.
qur|9|l17-11|6-8|The Night Journey — Muslim tradition tells of a night journey from Mecca to Jerusalem and up through the heavens, tied to Qur'an 17:1 and remembered every year.
qur|9|l17-12|6-8|What Historians Can and Cannot Confirm — Outside evidence confirms that Muhammad lived, that Arabs conquered widely soon after, and that the Qur'an is early; the details of the Meccan story rest on tradition.
qur|9|l18-1|6-8|The Hijra and the Calendar — In 622 Muhammad and the Muslims moved from Mecca to Yathrib, later called Medina; Muslims count their calendar from this hijra.
qur|9|l18-2|6-8|The Agreement of Medina — Tradition preserves an agreement between Muhammad, the Muslims and the tribes of Medina, including Jewish tribes, that made them one community for defense.
qur|9|l18-3|6-8|The Mosque and the Ummah — In Medina the Muslims built a mosque, paired emigrants with helpers, turned to face Mecca in prayer, and took on the practices that define Muslim life.
qur|9|l18-4|6-8|Badr and Uhud — Tradition tells of a surprising Muslim victory at Badr in 624 and a costly setback at Uhud in 625; the Qur'an names Badr and reflects on both.
qur|9|l18-5|6-8|The Trench and the Jewish Tribes — In 627 Medina withstood a siege at the Trench; in these years relations with three Jewish tribes of Medina broke down, ending in expulsion and, for one tribe, execution.
qur|9|l18-6|6-8|Hudaybiyyah and the Return to Mecca — A truce with Mecca in 628 that seemed like a loss led, within two years, to the peaceful entry into Mecca in 630 and the clearing of the Kaaba.
qur|9|l18-7|6-8|The Farewell Pilgrimage and After — In 632 Muhammad made a final pilgrimage and gave a sermon on fairness and equality; he died in Medina that year, and the Qur'an was gathered into one book after him.
qur|9|l18-8|6-8|Why a Verse Came Down — Asbab al-nuzul are reports about the situation in which a verse was revealed; scholars collected them to help readers understand what a verse was answering.
qur|9|l18-9|6-8|One Verse, Two Readings — Knowing the reported occasion of a verse such as 2:256 changes how readers weigh it, and readers still disagree about how far the occasion limits the meaning.
qur|9|l18-10|6-8|Verses on Fighting in Context — The Medinan verses on fighting each have reported occasions, and mainstream scholars read them with their context, limits and the verses around them.
qur|9|l18-11|6-8|Medinan Themes: Law, Family and the People of the Book — Medinan surahs are long and deal with law, family, community and the Qur'an's relationship to Jews and Christians, whom it calls People of the Book.
tal|1|l1-1|k-2|Many Books, One Name — The Talmud is a very big set of Jewish books, not just one book.
tal|1|l1-2|k-2|Teachers Called Rabbis — The Talmud is full of Jewish teachers, called rabbis, talking to each other.
tal|1|l1-3|k-2|Asking Out Loud — In the Talmud, rabbis ask questions and answer each other.
tal|1|l1-4|k-2|Words in the Middle, Words Around — A Talmud page has old words in the middle and helpers' words all around.
tal|1|l1-5|k-2|Two by Two — People study the Talmud in pairs and talk out loud.
tal|1|l1-6|k-2|Who Reads It? — Jewish people study the Talmud, and anyone can learn about it.
tal|1|l1-7|k-2|How We Talk About It — We say "the rabbis said" and "Jewish tradition" to tell whose words they are.
tal|1|l2-1|k-2|Five Books on a Scroll — The Torah is the first five books of the Bible, kept as a long scroll.
tal|1|l2-2|k-2|Moses on the Mountain — Jewish tradition says Moses received the Torah at Mount Sinai.
tal|1|l2-3|k-2|What a Mitzvah Is — A rule in the Torah is called a mitzvah.
tal|1|l2-4|k-2|Shabbat, the Seventh Day — Shabbat is a day of rest that comes every week on the seventh day.
tal|1|l2-5|k-2|A Rule Needs Explaining — A short rule like "do no work" makes people ask what work means.
tal|1|l2-6|k-2|The Rabbis Worked It Out — The Talmud is where the rabbis asked and answered questions about the rules.
tal|1|l2-7|k-2|A Good Question Is Learning — Asking a good question is a way of learning.
tal|10|l19-1|6-8|From When Do We Recite? — The Mishnah opens with a question about the earliest time to say the evening Shema, and answers it with a picture of priests eating.
tal|10|l19-2|6-8|Where Does the Teacher Stand? — The Gemara's first move is to ask what the Mishnah assumes, and it finds the answer in a verse from Deuteronomy.
tal|10|l19-3|6-8|Three Ways to See Night — The Gemara compares several teachings that mark nightfall by priests, by stars and by a poor man's supper, and asks whether they agree.
tal|10|l19-4|6-8|Forty Less One — The Mishnah lists thirty-nine main categories of work that Jewish law forbids on Shabbat, from sowing to carrying.
tal|10|l19-5|6-8|Why the Tabernacle? — The Talmud explains the thirty-nine labors as the kinds of work used to build the tabernacle in the wilderness.
tal|10|l19-6|6-8|Carrying and the Eruv — Carrying between a private and a public space is one of the thirty-nine labors, and the eruv is the rabbis' way of making a shared space count as one home.
tal|10|l19-7|6-8|What Is Hanukkah? — The Talmud tells the story of one jar of oil that burned eight days, while the books of Maccabees tell of the same festival without the oil.
tal|10|l19-8|6-8|Hillel and Shammai on the Lights — The House of Shammai lights eight candles the first night and fewer each night; the House of Hillel starts with one and adds, because holiness should grow.
tal|10|l19-9|6-8|We Light Like Hillel — Jewish practice follows the House of Hillel and places the lamp where passersby can see it, because the point is to make the miracle known.
tal|10|l19-10|6-8|The Sons Who Came Home Late — The Mishnah's first page shows a strict rule, midnight, made not to punish but to keep people from putting things off.
tal|10|l19-11|6-8|Time Is the First Subject — The Talmud opens with time because Jewish law, as the rabbis shaped it, turns ordinary hours into a pattern of duties and rest.
tal|10|l19-12|6-8|The Moves of a Sugya — Both sugyot in this chapter follow the same pattern of question, proof, objection and answer, which is the pattern of the whole Talmud.
tal|10|l20-1|6-8|The Mishnah's Four Fathers — The Mishnah sorts harm into four main kinds, the ox, the pit, the grazing animal and the fire, and asks who is responsible.
tal|10|l20-2|6-8|The Pit in the Street — The Torah makes the person who digs and leaves open a pit pay for an animal that falls in, and the rabbis ask what counts as a pit.
tal|10|l20-3|6-8|Fire That Spreads — Fire is a father of damage because it spreads beyond the one who lit it, so the one who lit it must be careful where it goes.
tal|10|l20-4|6-8|The Innocent Ox and the Warned Ox — An ox with no history of goring pays half the damage, while an ox known to gore pays full damage, because its owner was warned.
tal|10|l20-5|6-8|An Eye for an Eye — The Talmud reads "an eye for an eye" as a rule about paying the value of the injury, not about harming the one who injured.
tal|10|l20-6|6-8|Five Payments for an Injury — The Mishnah says a person who injures another pays for five things: the damage, the pain, the healing, lost work and the shame.
tal|10|l20-7|6-8|Two Hold a Cloak — When two people both grab a found cloak and each claims it, the Mishnah has each swear and they divide it.
tal|10|l20-8|6-8|Lost Objects and Giving Up Hope — The Torah tells a finder to return lost things, and the rabbis ask when an owner has given up hope, which the Talmud calls ye'ush.
tal|10|l20-9|6-8|Wronging With Words — The Mishnah teaches that hurting someone with words is a real wrong, which it calls ona'at devarim.
tal|10|l20-10|6-8|Love Your Neighbor — Rabbi Akiva called "love your neighbor as yourself" a great principle of the Torah, and other rabbis offered other great principles.
tal|10|l20-11|6-8|The Porters' Wages — The Talmud tells of a judge who told a rich man to pay poor workers even after they broke his barrels, beyond what the strict law required.
tal|11|l21-1|6-8|The Houses of Hillel and Shammai — Hillel and Shammai were two teachers around 30 BCE, and the schools named for them disagreed on hundreds of rules.
tal|11|l21-2|6-8|"These and Those" — The Talmud in Eruvin 13b says a heavenly voice called both Houses' words "the words of the living God" but ruled with Beit Hillel.
tal|11|l21-3|6-8|Why the Law Follows Hillel — The Gemara says Beit Hillel earned the ruling by being humble and teaching the other side's view first.
tal|11|l21-4|6-8|The Oven and the Miracles — In Bava Metzia 59b, Rabbi Eliezer calls on a tree, a stream and walls to prove his ruling, and the sages refuse each one.
tal|11|l21-5|6-8|"It Is Not in Heaven" — Rabbi Joshua answers the heavenly voice with Deuteronomy 30:12, and Rabbi Jeremiah explains that the law is decided by majority.
tal|11|l21-6|6-8|Elijah's Report — Rabbi Nathan meets the prophet Elijah, who says that God laughed and said, "My children have defeated me."
tal|11|l21-7|6-8|The Ban and the Wave — The second half of the story shows the majority hurting Rabbi Eliezer, and it sits inside a discussion of wronging people with words.
tal|11|l21-8|6-8|Rabbi Joshua and the Emperor's Daughter — In Ta'anit 7a, Rabbi Joshua answers an insult about his looks with a lesson about plain jars and good wine.
tal|11|l21-9|6-8|Keeping the Minority Voice — Mishnah Eduyot 1:4–6 explains why the opinions that lost are still written down.
tal|11|l21-10|6-8|For the Sake of Heaven — Pirkei Avot 5:17 says an argument for the sake of heaven lasts, and it names Hillel and Shammai as the example.
tal|11|l21-11|6-8|How Readers Read Akhnai Today — Orthodox, Conservative, Reform and academic readers all use the Akhnai story, and each draws a different lesson from it.
tal|11|l22-1|6-8|Stories Beside the Law — Aggadah is the Talmud's stories, sayings and ethics, woven between its legal debates.
tal|11|l22-2|6-8|A Chain of Teachers — Pirkei Avot opens with a chain of teachers that links the rabbis back to Moses, as rabbinic tradition holds.
tal|11|l22-3|6-8|Hillel's Three Questions — Hillel's saying balances caring for yourself, caring for others, and acting now.
tal|11|l22-4|6-8|You Do Not Have to Finish — Rabbi Tarfon taught that no one must finish the work alone, but no one is free to quit it.
tal|11|l22-5|6-8|Who Is Rich? — Ben Zoma turned four questions about success into four lessons about character.
tal|11|l22-6|6-8|Speech That Harms — The rabbis treated lashon hara, true but harmful talk about others, as a serious wrong.
tal|11|l22-7|6-8|Shame Is Like Bloodshed — The Gemara teaches that embarrassing someone in public is like shedding blood.
tal|11|l22-8|6-8|Turning Back — Teshuvah, returning, is the Talmud's path for making things right after a wrong.
tal|11|l22-9|6-8|The Rabbi Who Left — The Talmud keeps the story of Elisha ben Abuyah, a great teacher who left the rabbis' path.
tal|11|l22-10|6-8|Mercy Beside Argument — The Talmud pairs its sharp arguments with teachings about mercy.
tal|11|l22-11|6-8|Putting Avot to Work — Avot's sayings are short so that people can carry them into ordinary days.
tal|12|l23-1|9-10|A Tractate Called Blessings — Berakhot, meaning "blessings," is the first tractate of the Talmud, and it opens with the evening Shema.
tal|12|l23-2|9-10|Three Answers, All Kept — Mishnah Berakhot 1:1 gives three different end times for the evening Shema and keeps every opinion.
tal|12|l23-3|9-10|The Sons Come Home Late — The Mishnah tells how Rabban Gamliel's sons came home after midnight and he let them say the Shema, showing mercy inside the law.
tal|12|l23-4|9-10|The Gemara's First Question — The Gemara opens by asking what earlier source the Mishnah's teacher assumed when he asked "from what time."
tal|12|l23-5|9-10|Two Roots for One Rule — The Gemara offers two sources for the evening Shema: the verse about lying down, and the creation story's "evening and morning."
tal|12|l23-6|9-10|Priests and Stars — The Gemara reads the priests' mealtime as a sign of nightfall, the time when the stars come out.
tal|12|l23-7|9-10|The Poor Man's Dinner — A teaching quoted in the Gemara marks nightfall by when a poor person sits down to bread and salt.
tal|12|l23-8|9-10|Three Watches or Four? — The Gemara records a disagreement over whether the night has three watches or four, and gives signs for each.
tal|12|l23-9|9-10|David's Harp at Midnight — On Berakhot 3b the Gemara tells that a harp over King David's bed played at midnight and woke him to study, linked to Psalm 119:62.
tal|12|l23-10|9-10|Rodkinson Against the Page — Michael Rodkinson's English Talmud (1896–1903) is public domain and readable, but it is partial and abridged, so readers check it against the page.
tal|12|l23-11|9-10|Mapping the Moves — Reading Berakhot 2a as a map of moves — question, proof, second proof, test — shows how a sugya works.
tal|12|l24-1|9-10|Hillel and Shammai — Hillel and Shammai were leading teachers of the late first century BCE, and the Talmud often sets Hillel's patience beside Shammai's strictness.
tal|12|l24-2|9-10|On One Foot — A man asked to learn the whole Torah on one foot; Shammai sent him away, and Hillel answered with a single rule of kindness.
tal|12|l24-3|9-10|Only the Written Torah — A second visitor accepted only the Written Torah, and Hillel showed him through the alphabet that reading itself depends on teachers.
tal|12|l24-4|9-10|The Man Who Wanted to Be High Priest — A third visitor wanted to become High Priest; Hillel let him study until he discovered the limit himself, and all three later praised Hillel's patience.
tal|12|l24-5|9-10|The Question on 21b — On Shabbat 21b the Gemara stops a discussion of lamp oils to ask, "What is Hanukkah?"
tal|12|l24-6|9-10|The Cruse of Oil — The Gemara's answer tells of one sealed jar of oil that lasted eight days, and of the holiday set the next year.
tal|12|l24-7|9-10|The Lamp at the Door — The Hanukkah lamp is placed at the door facing the street, to make the miracle public, but inside on a table in time of danger.
tal|12|l24-8|9-10|Up or Down? — The House of Shammai lights eight lights on the first night and one fewer each night; the House of Hillel starts with one and adds one.
tal|12|l24-9|9-10|The Reasons Behind the Numbers — The Gemara gives reasons for each house: days still to come or already gone, the Sukkot bulls, and "raise holiness, do not lower it."
tal|12|l24-10|9-10|Halakhah Beside Aggadah — Shabbat 21b and 31a mix law and story on the same pages, and each helps explain the other.
tal|12|l24-11|9-10|Two Translations Side by Side — Setting Rodkinson's older English beside a modern paraphrase shows what translators add, cut and change.
tal|13|l25-1|9-10|A Chapter About Fair Dealing — Bava Metzia 59b sits inside a chapter about ona'ah, wronging people in trade and in speech.
tal|13|l25-2|9-10|Shame Like Shedding Blood — The Gemara treats public shaming as a very grave harm, comparing it to shedding blood.
tal|13|l25-3|9-10|The Verses Behind the Rule — The rabbis ground verbal wronging in Leviticus 25:17 and care for the stranger in Exodus 22:20.
tal|13|l25-4|9-10|An Oven in Pieces — The debate began over whether an oven built from segments with sand between them could become impure.
tal|13|l25-5|9-10|Miracles on Trial — Rabbi Eliezer calls on a tree, a stream, the walls and a heavenly voice, and the sages refuse them all.
tal|13|l25-6|9-10|It Is Not in Heaven — Rabbi Joshua answers the heavenly voice with Deuteronomy 30:12, reading it to mean the law is decided on earth.
tal|13|l25-7|9-10|My Children Have Defeated Me — In the story, Elijah reports that God smiled and said, My children have defeated me.
tal|13|l25-8|9-10|The Ban on Rabbi Eliezer — After the vote, the sages burn what Rabbi Eliezer called pure and ban him, and Rabbi Akiva carries the news gently.
tal|13|l25-9|9-10|Harm in the World — The Gemara describes damage that follows Rabbi Eliezer's grief, including a storm that threatens Rabban Gamliel.
tal|13|l25-10|9-10|The Gates of Wounding — Imma Shalom's words close the story: every gate may be locked except the gate of those who are wounded.
tal|13|l25-11|9-10|Authority and Mercy Together — The passage holds two lessons at once: the majority decides the law, and the one who loses must still be treated with care.
tal|13|l25-12|9-10|How Modern Readers Disagree — Orthodox, Conservative, Reform and academic readers use the oven story in different ways without one reading replacing the others.
tal|13|l26-1|9-10|Warning the Witnesses — Mishnah Sanhedrin 4:5 describes how judges warned witnesses in capital cases about the weight of a human life.
tal|13|l26-2|9-10|Why One Person Was Created — The Mishnah teaches that humanity began with a single person, so that destroying one life is like destroying a world.
tal|13|l26-3|9-10|Two Versions of the Line — Manuscripts and printed editions differ on whether the line says one soul or one soul of Israel.
tal|13|l26-4|9-10|Twenty-Three Judges — The Mishnah requires a court of twenty-three for capital cases and builds rules that tilt toward acquittal.
tal|13|l26-5|9-10|Questioning the Witnesses — The Mishnah requires close questioning of witnesses, and any contradiction on key points ends the testimony.
tal|13|l26-6|9-10|If All Convict, Acquit — The Gemara records the striking view that if every judge votes to convict, the accused goes free.
tal|13|l26-7|9-10|Once in Seven Years — Mishnah Makkot 1:10 calls a court that executes once in seven years destructive, and some say once in seventy.
tal|13|l26-8|9-10|The Law Against Its Own Use — Scholars describe rabbinic capital law as a system written so strictly that it almost never allowed an execution.
tal|13|l26-9|9-10|Mercy and Justice in Tension — The sugyot keep two kinds of mercy in view: mercy for the accused and protection for possible victims.
tal|14|l27-1|9-10|Moses to the Great Assembly — Avot 1:1 tells a chain of teachers from Moses to the Men of the Great Assembly, which rabbinic tradition uses to link the rabbis to Sinai.
tal|14|l27-2|9-10|Three Sayings for Judges and Teachers — The Men of the Great Assembly give three sayings: judge slowly, raise many students and make a fence around the Torah.
tal|14|l27-3|9-10|Simeon the Just: Three Pillars — Avot 1:2 says the world stands on three things: Torah, worship and acts of loving-kindness.
tal|14|l27-4|9-10|Five Pairs of Teachers — Avot 1:4–12 lists five pairs of teachers, the zugot, ending with Hillel and Shammai.
tal|14|l27-5|9-10|Hillel's Sayings — Hillel's sayings in Avot 1:12–14 join love of peace and people with a question about oneself and others.
tal|14|l27-6|9-10|Shammai and Rabban Gamliel — Shammai and Rabban Gamliel add sayings about fixed study, a cheerful welcome and removing doubt.
tal|14|l27-7|9-10|Judah the Prince and the Light Mitzvah — Avot 2:1 records Judah the Prince, editor of the Mishnah, asking people to take small commandments as seriously as large ones.
tal|14|l27-8|9-10|Do Not Judge Until You Stand There — Hillel's sayings in Avot 2 ask people not to judge others until they reach their place and to act well where no one else will.
tal|14|l27-9|9-10|The Day Is Short — Rabbi Tarfon says the day is short and the work great, yet no one has to finish it alone, though no one may stop.
tal|14|l27-10|9-10|Avot on Shabbat Afternoons — Many communities read a chapter of Avot on Shabbat afternoons between Passover and Shavuot, so Avot is printed in the prayer book.
tal|14|l27-11|9-10|Taylor's 1877 Translation — Charles Taylor's 1877 English Avot is a public-domain translation, and reading it well means comparing it with the Hebrew and with newer translations.
tal|14|l28-1|9-10|A Tractate Called Fast — Tractate Ta'anit, meaning Fast, sets out the public fasts a community held when the rain did not come.
tal|14|l28-2|9-10|Sackcloth or Deeds? — On a public fast day the Mishnah has an elder remind the people that God saw Nineveh's deeds, not only its fasting.
tal|14|l28-3|9-10|Nakdimon and the Borrowed Water — Ta'anit 19b–20a tells how Nakdimon ben Gurion borrowed water for pilgrims and how rain came just in time.
tal|14|l28-4|9-10|Standing in the Circle — Mishnah Ta'anit 3:8 tells how Honi drew a circle and would not leave it until rain came in the right measure.
tal|14|l28-5|9-10|The Complaint of Simeon ben Shetach — The Mishnah keeps Simeon ben Shetach's criticism of Honi next to the miracle, so the argument stays in the text.
tal|14|l28-6|9-10|The Carob Tree and Seventy Years — In Ta'anit 23a Honi sleeps seventy years, and the story ends with the saying "either friendship or death."
tal|14|l28-7|9-10|The Day and the Sick — Mishnah Yoma 8 rules that a person in danger is fed on Yom Kippur and that rubble is cleared on Shabbat to save a life.
tal|14|l28-8|9-10|Where Do We Learn It? — In Yoma 85a–b the rabbis agree that saving a life overrides Shabbat and argue over which verse proves it.
tal|14|l28-9|9-10|Live by Them — Shmuel reads Leviticus 18:5 as "live by them, and not die by them," and Rava says this proof cannot be refuted.
tal|14|l28-10|9-10|What Yom Kippur Cannot Do — Mishnah Yoma 8:9 teaches that Yom Kippur atones for wrongs against God, but wrongs against people need reconciliation first.
tal|14|l28-11|9-10|Law, Story and Mercy Together — Ta'anit and Yoma mix law and story, and both keep asking what a rule protects: the hungry town, the person under rubble, the one who was wronged.
tal|15|l29-1|11-12|Rabbi Ishmael's Thirteen Rules — Rabbi Ishmael's thirteen rules are a rabbinic list of methods for drawing laws from the Torah's words.
tal|15|l29-2|11-12|From Light to Heavy — Kal va-chomer argues that if a rule holds in a lighter case, it holds all the more in a weightier one.
tal|15|l29-3|11-12|Analogy and General Rules — Gezerah shavah links two passages by a shared word, and the general-and-particular rules decide how a broad statement relates to a list.
tal|15|l29-4|11-12|Rabbi Akiva and Every Letter — Rabbinic tradition remembers Rabbi Akiva as reading meaning into even the smallest features of the Torah's text.
tal|15|l29-5|11-12|Mapping the Moves — A sugya moves by question, proof, objection and answer, and sometimes ends with the question still open.
tal|15|l29-6|11-12|Who Is Speaking, and When — Knowing whether a voice is a tanna, an amora or the anonymous Gemara helps a reader weigh each step.
tal|15|l29-7|11-12|The Brisker Method in Brief — The Brisker method analyzes a law by splitting it into precise concepts to explain why authorities disagree.
tal|15|l29-8|11-12|The Geonim and the Rif — The Geonim and then Alfasi began turning Talmudic discussion into rulings people could follow.
tal|15|l29-9|11-12|Maimonides's Mishneh Torah — Maimonides's Mishneh Torah, completed c. 1180, organized all of Jewish law by topic without the arguments.
tal|15|l29-10|11-12|The Tur and the Shulchan Arukh — The Tur's four parts shaped the Shulchan Arukh of 1565, and Isserles's gloss added Ashkenazi practice.
tal|15|l30-1|11-12|What a Responsum Is — A responsum is a written answer to a question of Jewish law, reasoned from the Talmud and later sources.
tal|15|l30-2|11-12|How a Responsum Is Built — A responsum states the question, gathers the sources, weighs the comparisons and then rules, often with a note of caution.
tal|15|l30-3|11-12|Respondents Across the Centuries — Great respondents in every age, from Babylonia to New York, applied the Talmud to the questions of their own time.
tal|15|l30-4|11-12|Minhag: The Custom of the Place — The Mishnah gives local custom real weight, and one clear case protects workers.
tal|15|l30-5|11-12|Different Customs, Same Law — Ashkenazi and Sephardi communities keep different customs while studying the same Talmud and codes.
tal|15|l30-6|11-12|Electricity on Shabbat — Authorities reason about electricity by comparing it to the Mishnah's kinds of Shabbat work, and they reach different answers.
tal|15|l30-7|11-12|Saving a Life: Organ Donation — The Talmud's rule that saving a life overrides almost every law shapes modern debates on organ donation and brain death.
tal|15|l30-8|11-12|Medicine and Technology — Rabbis weigh new medicine and technology by the value of each life and by Talmudic cases that seem closest.
tal|15|l30-9|11-12|Women's Torah Study and Ordination — Movements read the same sources on women's learning and leadership and reach different conclusions.
tal|15|l30-10|11-12|How Movements Read Authority — Orthodox, Conservative and Reform Jews share the Talmud but differ on how binding its law is and who may change it.
tal|15|l30-11|11-12|Reading a Responsum in Paraphrase — Reading a responsum means following its question, sources, comparisons and ruling, and noticing what it protects.
tal|16|l31-1|11-12|The Paris Disputation of 1240 — In 1240 the Talmud was put on trial in Paris, and rabbis were made to answer charges against it before Christian judges.
tal|16|l31-2|11-12|The Burning of 1242 — After the trial, cartloads of Talmud manuscripts were burned in Paris around 1242, a loss that Jewish communities mourned for centuries.
tal|16|l31-3|11-12|Censors and the Basel Talmud — For centuries censors cut and changed Talmud passages, and the Basel edition of 1578–81 shows how a text could survive in altered form.
tal|16|l31-4|11-12|Bomberg's Venice Talmud — Daniel Bomberg, a Christian printer in Venice, printed the first complete Babylonian Talmud in 1520–23 and fixed the page numbers still used today.
tal|16|l31-5|11-12|Rome 1553 and the Council of Trent — In 1553 the Talmud was burned in Rome, and later church rules allowed it only in censored form.
tal|16|l31-6|11-12|The Fixed Page and Its Frame — The printed page set the Mishnah and Gemara in the center with Rashi and Tosafot around them, so every reader sees a conversation across centuries.
tal|16|l31-7|11-12|Poland-Lithuania and the Yeshiva — From the 1500s on, Poland and Lithuania became great centers of Talmud study, with yeshivas where students learned in pairs.
tal|16|l31-8|11-12|The Vilna Gaon — Rabbi Elijah of Vilna, the Vilna Gaon, pressed students to read the Talmud closely and to correct its text, shaping Lithuanian learning.
tal|16|l31-9|11-12|The Vilna Shas — The Vilna Shas, printed by the Romm press in 1880–86, became the standard edition of the Babylonian Talmud.
tal|16|l31-10|11-12|Daf Yomi: A Page a Day — In 1923 Rabbi Meir Shapiro proposed Daf Yomi, one page a day for everyone, finishing the whole Talmud in about seven and a half years.
tal|16|l31-11|11-12|Destruction and the Survivors' Talmud — The Holocaust destroyed the yeshiva world of Europe, and survivors, with help from the U.S. Army, printed a Talmud in Germany around 1949.
tal|16|l32-1|11-12|Studying Judaism as History — In the 1800s, the Wissenschaft des Judentums movement began to study the Talmud with the tools of history and text criticism.
tal|16|l32-2|11-12|The Munich Codex — The Munich manuscript of 1342 is the only nearly complete handwritten Babylonian Talmud to survive, and scholars use it to check the printed text.
tal|16|l32-3|11-12|The Cairo Genizah — A storeroom of worn-out papers in an old Cairo synagogue held hundreds of thousands of fragments, including early copies of rabbinic texts.
tal|16|l32-4|11-12|Beruriah in the Talmud — The Talmud tells of Beruriah, a learned woman of the 100s CE whose sharp readings of Scripture it records with respect.
tal|16|l32-5|11-12|Women's Houses of Study — In the 20th century, women's schools for advanced Jewish text study grew, and views on women learning Talmud differ across movements.
tal|16|l32-6|11-12|The Siyum HaShas of 2020 — The 2020 celebrations of finishing the Talmud filled a stadium in New Jersey and, for women learners, a hall in Jerusalem.
tal|16|l32-7|11-12|The Talmud in Israeli Law — Israel's law draws on many sources, and a 1980 law points judges to Jewish heritage when other sources give no answer.
tal|16|l32-8|11-12|Talmud in Stories: Agnon and Potok — Writers like S. Y. Agnon and Chaim Potok brought the world of Talmud study into literature read by millions.
tal|16|l32-9|11-12|The Open Text Online — Websites like Sefaria have put the Talmud and its commentaries online for free, changing who can open the page.
tal|16|l32-10|11-12|Talmud Study in Chicago — Chicago has long been home to schools, synagogues and classes where the Talmud is studied, across several Jewish movements.
tal|16|l32-11|11-12|The Talmud in Law Classrooms — Some law schools and comparative law courses study Talmudic reasoning as one of the world's great legal traditions.
tal|17|l33-1|11-12|Two Verses, Two Duties — The Gemara reads two Torah verses as two different duties: honor (kibbud) and reverence (mora).
tal|17|l33-2|11-12|Three Partners in a Person — Kiddushin 30b compares honoring parents to honoring God and calls a person's parents God's partners.
tal|17|l33-3|11-12|Whose Money Pays? — The Gemara asks who pays for a parent's food and clothing and records two views before it rules.
tal|17|l33-4|11-12|Dama ben Netina — The Gemara makes a non-Jewish jeweler from Ashkelon its model of how far honoring a parent can reach.
tal|17|l33-5|11-12|Rabbi Tarfon's Hands — The rabbis praise extreme devotion to parents and still say it is only a part of what the duty asks.
tal|17|l33-6|11-12|Where Honor Stops — The Talmud limits the duty: a parent cannot command a child to break the Torah.
tal|17|l33-7|11-12|Three Years of Argument — Eruvin 13b says a heavenly voice declared both schools' words living, yet the law follows Beit Hillel.
tal|17|l33-8|11-12|Why Hillel Wins — The Gemara says Beit Hillel's law prevailed because they were kind, humble and taught the other side first.
tal|17|l33-9|11-12|Keeping the Losing View — The Mishnah records minority opinions on purpose, so a later court may rely on them.
tal|17|l33-10|11-12|The Moves of the Page — Every sugya can be mapped as a chain of moves: source, question, proof, objection, answer and ruling.

tal|17|l33-12|11-12|Two Sugyot Side by Side — Setting Kiddushin 31 beside Eruvin 13b shows one Talmudic habit: rules with stories, rulings with dissent.
tal|17|l34-1|11-12|Choosing Your Sugya — A good capstone sugya is short, has a clear dispute and asks what a ruling protects.
tal|17|l34-2|11-12|Reading in Chavruta — Chavruta is paired study in which partners read aloud, question each other and argue toward understanding.
tal|17|l34-3|11-12|Map the Moves — Before arguing, partners map the sugya: Mishnah, question, proof, objection, answer and ruling.
tal|17|l34-4|11-12|Rashi at Your Elbow — Rashi's commentary explains the plain sense of each line and sits beside the Gemara on the printed page.
tal|17|l34-5|11-12|Tosafot Push Back — Tosafot compare passages across the Talmud and often challenge Rashi, modeling respectful disagreement.
tal|17|l34-6|11-12|One Modern Reader — A modern reader, religious or academic, shows how the sugya is understood and used today.
tal|17|l34-7|11-12|A Thesis About Argument and Mercy — A strong thesis names what the argument decides and what the ruling protects, with a citation.
tal|17|l34-8|11-12|Presenting With Your Partner — In the presentation, partners take opposite sides, then state together what the sugya is about.
tal|17|l34-9|11-12|Studying a Sacred Text in a Public School — Studying the Talmud in a public school means describing what readers believe, not telling anyone what to believe.
tal|17|l34-10|11-12|Looking Back, Looking Ahead — The capstone gathers the course's threads: the page's moves, its mercy and a partner who disagrees well.
tal|2|l3-1|k-2|Who Was Hillel? — Hillel was a rabbi who lived about 2,000 years ago and was known for being patient.
tal|2|l3-2|k-2|Who Was Shammai? — Shammai was a rabbi who lived at the same time as Hillel, and the two often disagreed.
tal|2|l3-3|k-2|A Strange Request — A man asked to learn the whole Torah while standing on one foot, and Shammai sent him away.

tal|2|l3-6|k-2|Go and Learn — Hillel's rule starts with kindness, and learning is how you find out the rest.

tal|2|l4-3|k-2|The Shepherd — Rabbi Akiva started as a shepherd who could not read, and he began to learn as a grown-up.
