tal|3|l5-1|k-2|What a Chavruta Is — Jewish tradition studies the Talmud in pairs, out loud, with questions.
tal|3|l5-2|k-2|Get Yourself a Friend — Pirkei Avot says: find a teacher, and get yourself a friend to study with.
tal|3|l5-3|k-2|Two Houses That Argued — The students of Hillel and Shammai disagreed a lot, and still stayed friends.
tal|3|l5-4|k-2|Both Sides Can Be Right — The Talmud says both houses spoke words of the living God, and the rule follows Hillel.
tal|3|l5-5|k-2|Ask When You Are Stuck — Hillel said a shy person cannot learn, so a good partner asks questions.
tal|3|l5-6|k-2|Say Why, Gently — A study partner is a friend who makes you think, so you say why, not just no.
tal|3|l6-1|k-2|Visiting the Sick — The rabbis said visiting a sick person is a mitzvah, and it makes them feel better.
tal|3|l6-2|k-2|Welcoming a Guest — The Torah says Abraham ran to feed three strangers, and the rabbis made that a rule.
tal|3|l6-3|k-2|Honoring Parents — The Torah says honor your father and mother, and the Talmud tells how.
tal|3|l6-4|k-2|Do Not Shame Anyone — The rabbis said shaming someone in front of others hurts as much as a wound.
tal|3|l6-5|k-2|Returning Lost Things — The Torah says bring a lost thing back, and the Mishnah says how to find the owner.
tal|3|l6-6|k-2|A Share for Those in Need — Tzedakah is giving a share to people who need it, and the rabbis said everyone gives.
tal|3|l6-7|k-2|Feed the Animals First — The Talmud says to feed your animals before you eat, because they cannot ask.
tal|4|l7-1|3-5|The Torah on a Scroll — The Written Torah is the first five books of the Hebrew Bible, kept as a scroll and read aloud.
tal|4|l7-2|3-5|The Torah That Was Spoken — Rabbinic tradition holds that Moses also received an Oral Torah, the explanations, which was passed on by memory.
tal|4|l7-3|3-5|Why a Rule Needs Explaining — Short rules like rest on Shabbat raise questions, and the Oral Torah is where the rabbis worked out answers.
tal|4|l7-4|3-5|Ezra Reads to the Crowd — The Bible tells that Ezra read the Torah aloud to the people, and helpers explained it, around 450 BCE.
tal|4|l7-5|3-5|The Men of the Great Assembly — Rabbinic tradition names a group after Ezra, the Men of the Great Assembly, as a link in the chain of teachers.
tal|4|l7-6|3-5|The Second Temple — The Second Temple in Jerusalem stood for about 500 years and was the center of Jewish worship while the Oral Torah was passed on.
tal|4|l7-7|3-5|Pharisees and Sadducees — In the time of the Second Temple, the Pharisees accepted the Oral Torah and the Sadducees did not.
tal|4|l7-8|3-5|Hillel and Shammai — Hillel and Shammai, two teachers around the end of the first century BCE, were the last pair in the chain named in Avot.
tal|4|l7-9|3-5|How to Keep a Holiday — The Oral Torah explains how to keep the Torah's holidays, and the rabbis' disagreements about them are part of the tradition.
tal|4|l8-1|3-5|Rome Burns the Temple — In 70 CE the Roman army destroyed the Second Temple, and Jewish life had to find a new center.
tal|4|l8-2|3-5|Yavneh, a Town of Scholars — At Yavneh, Rabban Yochanan ben Zakkai and his students rebuilt Jewish teaching around study and prayer.
tal|4|l8-3|3-5|The Fear of Forgetting — War and persecution made the rabbis fear the Oral Torah would be forgotten, and that fear pushed them to gather it.
tal|4|l8-4|3-5|The Man Called Rabbi — Rabbi Judah the Prince, leader of the Jews in the Land of Israel around 200 CE, gathered the Oral Torah into the Mishnah.
tal|4|l8-5|3-5|Six Orders on a Shelf — The Mishnah is arranged in six orders by subject, and each order holds several tractates.
tal|4|l8-6|3-5|Short, Clear and in Hebrew — The Mishnah is written in short Hebrew sentences that state rules and record who disagreed.
tal|4|l8-7|3-5|A Book That Asks for More — Because the Mishnah states rules without reasons, later rabbis kept asking why, and their discussion became the Gemara.
tal|4|l8-8|3-5|What the Mishnah Protects — Many of the Mishnah's rules protect people who could be hurt: the worker, the poor, the stranger and the person on trial.
tal|4|l8-9|3-5|Two Chapters, One Chain — From Sinai to the Mishnah, rabbinic tradition tells one long chain of passing on and writing down.
tal|5|l9-1|3-5|After the Mishnah — The Gemara is the rabbis' long discussion of the Mishnah, and Mishnah plus Gemara equals Talmud.
tal|5|l9-2|3-5|The Land of Israel — In the Land of Israel, rabbis in Galilee towns like Tiberias studied the Mishnah under Roman rule.
tal|5|l9-3|3-5|Babylon, Today's Iraq — A large Jewish community had lived in Babylon since the exile, and it became the Talmud's other home.
tal|5|l9-4|3-5|Sura and Rav — Rav brought the Mishnah to Babylon and founded the academy at Sura, where Talmud study began there.
tal|5|l9-5|3-5|Pumbedita, Abaye and Rava — At Pumbedita, Abaye and Rava argued so well that later readers called all Talmud study "the debates of Abaye and Rava."
tal|5|l9-6|3-5|Travelers With Good Memories — Travelers carried teachings between the Land of Israel and Babylon, so the two centers kept answering each other.
tal|5|l9-7|3-5|The Jerusalem Talmud — The Jerusalem Talmud, finished around 400 CE, is the Gemara of the Land of Israel, though it was made mostly in Tiberias.
tal|5|l9-8|3-5|The Babylonian Talmud — The Babylonian Talmud, finished around 500 CE, is longer than the Yerushalmi and is the Talmud most people study.
tal|5|l9-9|3-5|Why Two, and Which One? — Both Talmuds discuss the same Mishnah, but the Bavli became the main one because the Babylonian community and its later teachers spread it.
tal|5|l10-1|3-5|One Leaf, Two Sides — A daf is one leaf with two sides, a and b, and every tractate starts at daf 2.
tal|5|l10-2|3-5|The Middle Column — The Mishnah and Gemara sit in the middle column of the page, with no vowels and no punctuation.
tal|5|l10-3|3-5|Words All Around — Rashi's explanation sits on the inside edge of the page and the Tosafot on the outside, so the page holds a conversation across centuries.
tal|5|l10-4|3-5|Rashi, the Teacher From Troyes — Rashi wrote a clear, step-by-step commentary on almost the whole Babylonian Talmud, and readers have leaned on it ever since.
tal|5|l10-5|3-5|Tosafot, the Questioners — The Tosafot compare one page of Talmud with another and ask why they seem to disagree.
tal|5|l10-6|3-5|Printers Who Fixed the Page — A printer in Venice set the page's shape in the 1520s, and the Vilna printing of the 1880s made it the page everyone uses.
tal|5|l10-7|3-5|Daf Yomi Begins — Daf Yomi, started in 1923, has people everywhere read one daf a day and finish the whole Talmud in about seven and a half years.
tal|5|l10-8|3-5|A Stadium Full of Readers — When a cycle ends, readers gather for a siyum, and in 2020 tens of thousands filled a stadium to celebrate.
tal|5|l10-9|3-5|Finding Berakhot Page 2 — Anyone can find Berakhot 2a, in a book or online, and read it best with a partner.
tal|6|l11-1|3-5|What the Oven Was — The argument in this story began over a real oven and a rule about what could make it unclean.
tal|6|l11-2|3-5|Who Was Arguing — Rabbi Eliezer stood on one side; Rabbi Joshua and the other sages stood on the other, at Yavneh about 100 CE.
tal|6|l11-3|3-5|Why the Oven Has a Name — The Talmud says the oven was called Akhnai because the rabbis wrapped it in arguments like a snake.
tal|6|l11-4|3-5|The Tree and the Stream — Rabbi Eliezer called on a carob tree and a stream, but the rabbis said wonders cannot prove a law.
tal|6|l11-5|3-5|The Walls Lean In — When the walls of the study house began to fall, Rabbi Joshua scolded them, and they stayed half leaning.
tal|6|l11-6|3-5|It Is Not in Heaven — Rabbi Joshua answered a voice from heaven with a Torah verse: the Torah was already given, so people must decide.
tal|6|l11-7|3-5|My Children Have Won — The story says God laughed and said, "My children have defeated me"; the Talmud reads this as joy, not anger.
tal|6|l11-8|3-5|A Sad Ending, Told Gently — The sages put Rabbi Eliezer outside the group, and the Talmud shows the pain that this caused on every side.
tal|6|l11-9|3-5|The Gate of Hurt Feelings — The sugya ends with a warning: words that hurt a person's feelings are always heard.
tal|6|l11-10|3-5|Disagreeing with Respect — Readers take different lessons from the story, but all of them see a model for arguing with care.
tal|6|l12-1|3-5|Water Wears Away Stone — Rabbinic tradition tells that Akiva began learning to read at forty, after seeing what dripping water did to a rock.
tal|6|l12-2|3-5|Rachel's Promise — Rachel married Akiva on one condition: that he go and study Torah.
tal|6|l12-3|3-5|Twenty-Four Years — Akiva studied for twenty-four years, and when he came home he said all his learning belonged to Rachel.
tal|6|l12-4|3-5|Thousands of Students — The Talmud says Rabbi Akiva's thousands of students were lost because they did not honor one another, and he began again with five.
tal|6|l12-5|3-5|A Great Principle — Rabbi Akiva called "love your neighbor as yourself" a great principle of the Torah.
tal|6|l12-6|3-5|Akiva's Courage — Rabbi Akiva kept teaching Torah when Rome forbade it, and the Talmud remembers his brave death.
tal|6|l12-7|3-5|A Learned Woman — Beruriah is a woman scholar whose words the Talmud quotes and whose opinions the rabbis respected.
tal|6|l12-8|3-5|Sins, Not Sinners — Beruriah read a Hebrew word in Psalm 104 as "sins" rather than "sinners," and Rabbi Meir changed his prayer.
tal|6|l12-9|3-5|Beruriah's Sharp Words — In Eruvin 53b Beruriah corrects a rabbi and a student, showing she knew the rabbis' rules well.
tal|6|l12-10|3-5|Sandals, Wood and Bricks — Many sages worked with their hands; the Talmud remembers a sandal-maker, a laborer and a builder.
tal|6|l12-11|3-5|Doctors and Charcoal — The Talmud shows sages healing the sick and working at dirty jobs, and it honors them for it.
tal|7|l13-1|3-5|Three Gates About Fairness — Bava Metzia, the middle gate, is one of three tractates about damages, property and fair dealing.
tal|7|l13-2|3-5|Two Hold One Coat — The tractate opens with two people holding one garment, and the rabbis' first answer is: promise, then share.
tal|7|l13-3|3-5|Bring It Back — The Torah tells a finder to return a lost animal or object, and the rabbis built a whole set of rules on that verse.
tal|7|l13-4|3-5|A Sign Tells Whose — A found thing with a sign is announced; a found thing with no sign, like loose coins, belongs to the finder.
tal|7|l13-5|3-5|When the Owner Gives Up — The Gemara asks whether an owner who gave up hope still owns the lost thing, and Abaye and Rava disagree.
tal|7|l13-6|3-5|Just Weights and Measures — Leviticus commands honest scales, and the Mishnah adds rules for keeping measuring tools clean and true.
tal|7|l13-7|3-5|No Overcharging — The Mishnah calls charging far too much ona'ah, wronging, and sets a clear line: one-sixth over the price.
tal|7|l13-8|3-5|Hurting With Words — The Mishnah says words can wrong a person as much as money can, and the Gemara says even more.
tal|7|l13-9|3-5|Pay Before the Sun Sets — The Torah says pay a hired worker the same day, and the Mishnah spells out when day and night workers are paid.
tal|7|l13-10|3-5|The Broken Barrels — A story in Bava Metzia shows a rabbi telling an employer to go beyond the strict law and treat his workers with mercy.
tal|7|l14-1|3-5|Tzedakah Means Justice — In Jewish tradition, giving to the poor is called tzedakah, from the word for justice, because the poor have a right to it.
tal|7|l14-2|3-5|Everyone Gives — The Talmud says even a poor person who lives on tzedakah gives tzedakah, and it sets a floor and a ceiling for giving.
tal|7|l14-3|3-5|The Secret Gift — The rabbis prized giving in secret, so the poor are not shamed, and Maimonides put it near the top of his ladder of giving.
tal|7|l14-4|3-5|Guests Before Everything — The Talmud says welcoming guests is even greater than greeting the Divine Presence, and it learns this from Abraham.
tal|7|l14-5|3-5|Visiting the Sick — The rabbis treated visiting the sick as a duty, and they told stories to show how much a visit can do.
tal|7|l14-6|3-5|Kindness Bigger Than Money — The Talmud says acts of kindness reach further than money, and it extends care to the poor and sick of every people.
tal|7|l14-7|3-5|Move the Stones — The Mishnah in Yoma says that when a life may be in danger, you break Shabbat to save it, even if you are not sure.
tal|7|l14-8|3-5|One Life, a Whole World — A famous Mishnah in Sanhedrin says that whoever saves a single life is counted as if they saved a whole world.
tal|7|l14-9|3-5|Rules for Mercy — The rabbis made mercy into clear rules, with a few firm limits, and Jewish communities today live by them.
tal|8|l15-1|6-8|The Center of the Page — The middle of a Talmud page holds the Mishnah and the Gemara, the two oldest layers of the text.
tal|8|l15-2|6-8|Rashi on the Inside — Rashi's commentary sits on the inner margin and explains the words and the flow of the Gemara.
tal|8|l15-3|6-8|Tosafot on the Outside — The Tosafot, on the outer margin, are questions and additions from Rashi's grandsons and their students in the 1100s and 1200s.
tal|8|l15-4|6-8|The Small Print at the Edges — Around the three main columns, later printers added short references that link a line to the Bible, to law codes and to other pages.
tal|8|l15-5|6-8|Two Languages on One Page — The Mishnah is mostly Hebrew and the Gemara mostly Aramaic, so reading the Talmud means moving between two related languages.
tal|8|l15-6|6-8|Studying Aloud, in Pairs — Jewish tradition studies the page with a partner, out loud, which is one reason the page keeps so many voices.
tal|8|l15-7|6-8|Bomberg's Venice Edition — Daniel Bomberg's Venice printing of 1520–23 fixed the page layout and page numbers that nearly every edition since has followed.
tal|8|l15-8|6-8|The Vilna Shas — The Romm press in Vilna printed the edition of 1880–86 that became the standard look of the Talmud page.
tal|8|l15-9|6-8|Editions and Translations Today — Modern editions and translations, in print and online, open the page to readers who do not know Hebrew or Aramaic.
tal|8|l15-10|6-8|How to Cite a Page — A Talmud citation names the tractate, the leaf number and the side; a Mishnah citation names the tractate, chapter and mishnah.
tal|8|l15-11|6-8|The Six Orders and Their Tractates — The Mishnah is arranged in six orders, and each order holds tractates on related subjects.
tal|8|l15-12|6-8|Where the Page Protects Someone — Even a lesson on layout leads to the Talmud's purpose: rules that protect the worker, the owner of a lost object and the person at risk.
tal|8|l16-1|6-8|A Unit of Discussion — A sugya is one complete discussion in the Gemara, and it usually moves through question, proof, objection and answer.
tal|8|l16-2|6-8|What Is the Reason? — The Gemara often opens by asking "mai ta'ama?" (what is the reason?), because it wants to know why a rule is true, not only what it says.
tal|8|l16-3|6-8|Proof from a Verse — A common move in the Gemara is to support a rule with a verse from the Hebrew Bible.
tal|8|l16-4|6-8|The Kushya — A kushya is an objection or difficulty, and raising one is how the Gemara tests every claim.
tal|8|l16-5|6-8|The Terutz — A terutz is an answer that resolves an objection, often by showing that two sources speak about different cases.
tal|8|l16-6|6-8|Teyku: Let It Stand — Sometimes the Gemara ends a question with teyku, "let it stand," and leaves it unresolved.
tal|8|l16-7|6-8|From Light to Heavy — Kal va-chomer is an argument from a lesser case to a greater one: if a rule holds in the easy case, it holds even more in the hard case.
tal|8|l16-8|6-8|A Matching Word — Gezerah shavah links two laws because the Torah uses the same word in both places.
tal|8|l16-9|6-8|A Teaching from Outside — A baraita is a teaching from the time of the Mishnah that was left out of it, and the Gemara often quotes one as a proof or an objection.
tal|8|l16-10|6-8|Rav and Shmuel — Rav and Shmuel were early amoraim in Babylonia whose disagreements run through the Talmud.
tal|8|l16-11|6-8|Abaye and Rava — Abaye and Rava were fourth-century amoraim whose debates became a symbol of Talmud study itself.
tal|8|l16-12|6-8|Why the Minority Is Kept — The Mishnah and the Gemara record views that lost, and they give reasons for doing so.
tal|9|l17-1|6-8|Greek Kings, Then Rome — For three centuries before the Talmud's rabbis, Judea lived under Greek-speaking kings and then under Rome.
tal|9|l17-2|6-8|Pharisees, Sadducees, Essenes — Jews in the Second Temple period belonged to different groups, and the rabbis of the Talmud grew out of the Pharisees.
tal|9|l17-3|6-8|Hillel and Shammai — Hillel and Shammai, teachers of about the 1st century BCE, gave the Talmud its first great pair of disagreeing schools.
tal|9|l17-4|6-8|The War With Rome, 66–70 CE — A revolt against Rome ended with the burning of the Second Temple in 70 CE, the turning point of Jewish history that the Talmud's rabbis lived after.
tal|9|l17-5|6-8|Yochanan ben Zakkai's Escape — Rabbinic tradition tells that Yochanan ben Zakkai left besieged Jerusalem and won permission to teach at Yavneh, and later rabbis both honored and questioned his choice.
tal|9|l17-6|6-8|Prayer in Place of Sacrifice — After 70 CE the sages taught that prayer, study and acts of kindness could serve where sacrifices once had.
tal|9|l17-7|6-8|The Court at Yavneh — At Yavneh the sages under Rabban Gamliel II settled the calendar, the prayers and many disputes, and the Talmud remembers both their authority and their quarrels.
tal|9|l17-8|6-8|Bar Kokhba and Rabbi Akiva — A second revolt, 132–135 CE, ended in defeat, and Rabbi Akiva, who had hoped in its leader, died as one of Rome's martyrs.
tal|9|l17-9|6-8|Usha, the Galilee and Judah the Prince — After 135 CE the sages moved north to the Galilee, where Rabbi Judah the Prince gathered the Mishnah in about 200 CE.
tal|9|l17-10|6-8|Reading the Sources Side by Side — Historians build the story of this period from Josephus, Roman writers, coins and ruins, and read the Talmud's stories as teaching written down later.
tal|9|l17-11|6-8|From Temple to Study House — Between 70 and 200 CE Judaism shifted its center from the Temple and its priests to the study house and its sages, and the Mishnah is the record of that shift.
tal|9|l18-1|6-8|The Tannaim: Teachers of the Mishnah — The Tannaim were the teachers whose words are gathered in the Mishnah, up to about 220 CE.
tal|9|l18-2|6-8|The Amoraim: Teachers of the Gemara — The Amoraim were the teachers who discussed the Mishnah from about 220 to 500 CE, and their talk became the Gemara.
tal|9|l18-3|6-8|Who Is Speaking? — The Gemara names its speakers, so a reader can tell a tanna from an amora and one generation from the next.
tal|9|l18-4|6-8|Tiberias and Caesarea — In the Land of Israel, the great schools of the Amoraim stood in cities like Tiberias and Caesarea.
tal|9|l18-5|6-8|Sura, Nehardea and Pumbedita — In Babylonia, the academies of Nehardea, Sura and Pumbedita shaped the Babylonian Talmud.
tal|9|l18-6|6-8|Two Talmuds — The Babylonian Talmud is longer and more widely studied than the Jerusalem Talmud, and both are Mishnah plus Gemara.
tal|9|l18-7|6-8|Rav and Shmuel — Rav and Shmuel, the first great Babylonian Amoraim, argued often, and later rulers of law followed each in his own field.
tal|9|l18-8|6-8|Rabbi Yochanan and Resh Lakish — The Talmud tells of study partners so close that one could not learn without the other's objections.
tal|9|l18-9|6-8|Abaye and Rava — Abaye and Rava argued across the Babylonian Talmud, and the law follows Rava in all but six cases.
tal|9|l18-10|6-8|Empires and the Exilarch — The Talmud grew under two empires: Christian Rome in the Land of Israel and Sasanian Persia in Babylonia.
tal|9|l18-11|6-8|Rav Ashi, Ravina and the Savoraim — Tradition names Rav Ashi and Ravina as the editors of the Babylonian Talmud, and the Savoraim finished its shaping.
tal|9|l18-12|6-8|The Geonim and the Letters — After the Talmud closed, the Geonim of Sura and Pumbedita taught it to Jews across the world through letters.
hin|1|l1-1|k-2|The Ramayana — The Ramayana is a very old story from India.
hin|1|l1-2|k-2|Rama, Sita and Lakshmana — Rama, Sita and Lakshmana are the main people in the story.
hin|1|l1-3|k-2|The King's Promise — Rama goes to the forest to keep his father's promise.
hin|1|l1-4|k-2|Into the Forest — Sita and Lakshmana choose to go with Rama.
hin|1|l1-5|k-2|A Deer That Is a Trick — The golden deer is a trick to take Rama away.
hin|1|l1-6|k-2|Ravana Takes Sita — Ravana, king of Lanka, carries Sita far away.
hin|1|l2-1|k-2|A Loyal Friend — Hanuman is Rama's loyal and brave helper.
hin|1|l2-2|k-2|The Monkey Army — Hanuman and the monkey army help Rama look for Sita.
hin|1|l2-3|k-2|The Great Leap — Hanuman leaps across the sea to find Sita.
hin|1|l2-4|k-2|Good News for Rama — Hanuman brings Rama good news about Sita.
hin|1|l2-5|k-2|The Stone Bridge — Everyone helps build a bridge, even a tiny squirrel.
hin|1|l2-6|k-2|Rama and Sita Come Home — Rama brings Sita home, and people light lamps to welcome them.
hin|1|l2-7|k-2|Lamps at Diwali — Many Hindus remember this homecoming at Diwali.
hin|10|l19-1|6-8|So It Was — Hindus call the Ramayana and Mahabharata itihasa, meaning 'so indeed it was', a genre of sacred history and epic.
hin|10|l19-2|6-8|Dates and Authors — Tradition names Valmiki and Vyasa as authors, while scholars date the epics' growth to about 400 BCE–400 CE.
hin|10|l19-3|6-8|Frame Stories — Both epics use frame stories, where one story is told inside another.
hin|10|l19-4|6-8|The Eighteen Parvas — The Mahabharata is arranged in eighteen books, called parvas, that trace a family feud from its start to its end.
hin|10|l19-5|6-8|The Seven Kandas — Valmiki's Ramayana has seven kandas, or books, that follow Rama from birth to his reign.
hin|10|l19-6|6-8|Stories Inside the Mahabharata — The Mahabharata holds many side stories, like Nala and Damayanti and Savitri, that teach about love and duty.
hin|10|l19-7|6-8|Hard Choices About Dharma — The epics show characters facing hard choices about dharma, and they often leave readers to think it through.
hin|10|l19-8|6-8|Kings, Friends and Loss — Besides dharma, the epics teach about kingship, friendship and grief.
hin|10|l19-9|6-8|Ganguli and Griffith in English — K.M. Ganguli's full English Mahabharata (1883–96) and Griffith's Ramayan (1870–74) are public-domain translations.
hin|10|l19-10|6-8|Many Ramayanas — The Ramayana has been retold in many languages, including Kamban's Tamil version and Tulsidas's Ramcharitmanas.
hin|10|l20-1|6-8|Old Stories, Big Books — The Puranas are large collections of stories, cosmology and worship, composed over many centuries.
hin|10|l20-2|6-8|The Bhagavata and the Devi Mahatmya — The Bhagavata Purana tells Krishna's life, and the Devi Mahatmya praises the Goddess.
hin|10|l20-3|6-8|The Ten Avatars of Vishnu — The Puranas describe Vishnu descending as avatars, and a common list names ten.
hin|10|l20-4|6-8|What Is Bhakti? — Bhakti is loving devotion to God, and it became a movement of poet-saints across India.
hin|10|l20-5|6-8|The Alvars and Nayanars — In Tamil lands, the Alvars sang to Vishnu and the Nayanars sang to Shiva, c. 6th–9th centuries CE.
hin|10|l20-6|6-8|Across Caste Lines — Bhakti poets often challenged caste barriers, though caste did not disappear.
hin|10|l20-7|6-8|Mirabai — Mirabai, a 16th-century Rajput woman devoted to Krishna, is one of the best-loved bhakti poets.
hin|10|l20-8|6-8|Kabir, Claimed by Many — Kabir, a 15th-century weaver poet of Varanasi, is claimed by Hindus, Muslims and Sikhs.
hin|10|l20-9|6-8|Tulsidas and Tukaram — Tulsidas in Hindi and Tukaram in Marathi carried bhakti to millions of homes.
hin|11|l21-1|6-8|The Indus Cities — The Indus civilization flourished c. 2600–1900 BCE, but its script is unread and its religion is uncertain.
hin|11|l21-2|6-8|Links and Limits — Scholars debate whether Indus practices link to later Hindu traditions, and they separate what is known from what is guessed.
hin|11|l21-3|6-8|The Vedic Period — Scholars place the Vedic period at c. 1500–500 BCE, when the Vedas were composed and passed down orally.
hin|11|l21-4|6-8|Upanishads, Buddha and Mahavira — Around 600–400 BCE, the Upanishads, the Buddha and Mahavira all asked new questions about karma, rebirth and freedom.
hin|11|l21-5|6-8|The Epics Take Shape — Scholars say the Ramayana and Mahabharata grew over centuries, c. 400 BCE–400 CE; tradition places their events in far older ages.
hin|11|l21-6|6-8|Manusmriti and Its Critics — The Manusmriti, a law book of c. 200 BCE–200 CE, set rules on caste and gender that many Hindus have strongly criticized.
hin|11|l21-7|6-8|The Gupta Age — Under the Gupta kings, c. 320–550 CE, temple building, Sanskrit learning and Purana writing flourished.
hin|11|l21-8|6-8|Temples and Later Centuries — After the Guptas, great temples and regional traditions grew, and the texts were carried in many languages.
hin|11|l21-9|6-8|Two Kinds of Dates — Hindu history is often told with both scholarly dates and traditional dates, and a careful reader names both.
hin|11|l22-1|6-8|Angkor Wat — Angkor Wat in Cambodia, built in the 12th century for Vishnu, has huge carvings of the Mahabharata and Ramayana.
hin|11|l22-2|6-8|Bali and Southeast Asia — On the island of Bali, most people are Hindu, and the epics live on in dance and shadow puppetry.
hin|11|l22-3|6-8|How Texts Travel — Hindu texts traveled with traders, priests, kings, translators and migrants, and they changed along the way.
hin|11|l22-4|6-8|Emerson and Thoreau — In the 1800s, the American writers Emerson and Thoreau read the Bhagavad Gita in English translation and admired it.
hin|11|l22-5|6-8|Vivekananda in Chicago — At the World's Parliament of Religions in Chicago in 1893, Swami Vivekananda introduced many Americans to Hindu thought.
hin|11|l22-6|6-8|Gandhi and the Gita — Mahatma Gandhi called the Bhagavad Gita his guide and read its battle as an inner struggle.
hin|11|l22-7|6-8|Hindu Americans — About 1 percent of Americans are Hindu, and temples like the one in Lemont keep the texts alive for families.
hin|11|l22-8|6-8|The Ramayan on TV — Ramanand Sagar's Ramayan TV series (1987–88) drew huge audiences and changed how many people know the epic.
hin|11|l22-9|6-8|Old Texts, New Readers — Today the Hindu texts are read in many ways: in worship, in schools, in yoga studios and in debates about how to live.
hin|12|l23-1|9-10|A Poem Inside an Epic — The Gita is eighteen chapters of the Mahabharata's Bhishma Parva, told by Sanjaya to a blind king.
hin|12|l23-2|9-10|Who Stands on the Field — Arjuna's crisis begins when he sees that the enemy is his own family and teachers.
hin|12|l23-3|9-10|Arjuna's Arguments — Arjuna gives reasons for refusing: killing kin is wrong, and it will destroy families and their traditions.
hin|12|l23-4|9-10|Krishna's First Reply — Krishna begins by telling Arjuna that the wise do not grieve for the living or the dead.
hin|12|l23-5|9-10|Never Born, Never Dying — In Gita 2.19 to 2.21 Krishna says the self neither kills nor is killed, because it is unborn and eternal.
hin|12|l23-6|9-10|The Clothing Image — Gita 2.22 compares the self changing bodies to a person changing worn-out clothes.
hin|12|l23-7|9-10|Your Right Is to the Work — Gita 2.47 says a person's concern is the action itself, not its fruits.
hin|12|l23-8|9-10|The Person of Steady Wisdom — In Gita 2.54 to 2.72 Arjuna asks what a person of steady wisdom is like, and Krishna describes one.
hin|12|l23-9|9-10|From Desire to Ruin — Gita 2.62 to 2.63 traces a chain from dwelling on things to desire, anger, confusion and ruin.
hin|12|l23-10|9-10|Arnold's Song Celestial — Edwin Arnold's 1885 verse translation brought the Gita to many English readers, including the young Gandhi.
hin|12|l23-11|9-10|An Outer War or an Inner One — Some readers take Kurukshetra as a real battle; others read it as the struggle inside every person.
hin|12|l24-1|9-10|Why Not Just Withdraw? — In Gita 3 Arjuna asks why he must act at all, and Krishna answers that no one can stay without action.
hin|12|l24-2|9-10|Work as Offering — Gita 3.9 and 3.19 teach acting without attachment, as an offering rather than for private gain.
hin|12|l24-3|9-10|One's Own Duty — Gita 3.35 says it is better to do one's own duty imperfectly than another's duty well.
hin|12|l24-4|9-10|When Dharma Declines — In Gita 4.7 to 4.8 Krishna says he comes into the world age after age to protect dharma.
hin|12|l24-5|9-10|Action in Inaction — Gita 4.18 says the wise see inaction in action and action in inaction.
hin|12|l24-6|9-10|The Fire of Knowledge — Gita 4 praises knowledge, jnana, as a fire that burns away the binding effects of action.
hin|12|l24-7|9-10|A Seat, Neither High nor Low — Gita 6 describes how a person of the text sets up for meditation and steadies the mind.
hin|12|l24-8|9-10|The Lamp and the Restless Mind — Gita 6.19 pictures the steady mind as a lamp in a windless place, and 6.34–6.35 admit the mind is hard to hold.
hin|12|l24-9|9-10|Gandhi's Gospel of Selfless Action — Gandhi read Gita 2 to 6 as a guide to selfless service and nonviolent action.
hin|12|l24-10|9-10|Duty and Conscience — Readers argue about whether the Gita's call to duty leaves room for conscience, and the text itself stages the debate.
hin|13|l25-1|9-10|The Royal Secret — Gita 9 presents devotion as a path open to everyone, which Krishna calls the royal knowledge and royal secret.
hin|13|l25-2|9-10|What Is Offered With Love — Gita 9.26 says Krishna accepts even a leaf, a flower, a fruit or water offered with devotion.
hin|13|l25-3|9-10|No One Is Turned Away — Gita 9.30 to 9.32 say devotion is open to all, though some verses reflect the social views of their time.
hin|13|l25-4|9-10|A Thousand Suns — Gita 11 shows Krishna's universal form through Sanjaya's words of light and wonder.
hin|13|l25-5|9-10|Time, the Destroyer — In Gita 11.32 Krishna names himself as Time, and J. Robert Oppenheimer later recalled this verse in his own words.
hin|13|l25-6|9-10|From Awe Back to Love — Arjuna asks Krishna to return to his gentle form, and the chapter ends by praising devotion.
hin|13|l25-7|9-10|Dear to Me — Gita 12.13 to 12.20 lists the qualities of a devotee whom Krishna calls dear to him.
hin|13|l25-8|9-10|Surrender: Gita 18.66 — Gita 18.66 asks Arjuna to take refuge in Krishna alone, and schools read the verse in different ways.
hin|13|l25-9|9-10|Three Paths, One Poem — Many readers map the Gita as teaching three paths: action, knowledge and devotion.
hin|13|l25-10|9-10|How the Gita Ends — Gita 18 closes with Arjuna's doubt gone and Sanjaya's joy, and the Gita then returns to the epic's war.
hin|13|l26-1|9-10|Texts at the End of the Veda — The Upanishads are the last layer of the Vedas, and many Hindus call them Vedanta, the end of the Veda.
hin|13|l26-2|9-10|How to Read a Dialogue — Upanishads often teach through dialogues, so readers ask who asks, who answers, and what image carries the idea.
hin|13|l26-3|9-10|A Boy at Death's Door — In the Katha Upanishad, a boy named Nachiketa goes to Yama, the lord of death, and is granted three wishes.
hin|13|l26-4|9-10|The Good and the Pleasant — Yama tests Nachiketa with riches, and praises him for choosing the good over the pleasant.
hin|13|l26-5|9-10|The Chariot — Katha Upanishad 1.3.3 to 1.3.4 pictures the self as the rider in a chariot drawn by the senses.
hin|13|l26-6|9-10|That Art Thou — In Chandogya Upanishad 6, Uddalaka teaches his son that the finest essence is the self of all, ending: tat tvam asi.
hin|13|l26-7|9-10|The Banyan Seed — In Chandogya 6.12, a seed broken open shows nothing visible, yet from that fine essence a great tree grows.
hin|13|l26-8|9-10|Salt in Water — In Chandogya 6.13, salt dissolved in water cannot be seen but can be tasted everywhere in it.
hin|13|l26-9|9-10|Enjoy by Renouncing — Isha Upanishad 1 says all this world is pervaded by the Lord, and one should enjoy by renouncing and not covet.
hin|13|l26-10|9-10|Seeing All in the Self — Isha 6 and 7 say that one who sees all beings in the self, and the self in all beings, feels no hatred or grief.
hin|14|l27-1|9-10|The Oldest Book of Hymns — The Rig Veda is a collection of 1,028 hymns in ten books, composed in an early form of Sanskrit and passed down orally.
hin|14|l27-2|9-10|Reading Ancient Poetry Carefully — Reading a Vedic hymn well means knowing its translation, its speaker and its images, and not rushing to modern meanings.
hin|14|l27-3|9-10|Neither Being nor Non-Being — Rig Veda 10.129 begins by describing a state before existence and non-existence, before air, sky, death or night.
hin|14|l27-4|9-10|Desire and the Seed of Mind — Verse 4 of the hymn says desire came first, the first seed of mind, and sages found the bond of being in non-being.
hin|14|l27-5|9-10|Who Really Knows? — The hymn ends in doubt: even the one who oversees creation from highest heaven surely knows, or perhaps does not.
hin|14|l27-6|9-10|The Cosmic Person — Rig Veda 10.90 describes a cosmic Purusha with a thousand heads, from whose sacrifice the world is made.
hin|14|l27-7|9-10|The Four Varnas in 10.90.12 — Verse 12 names four classes from Purusha's mouth, arms, thighs and feet, the only such list in the Rig Veda.
hin|14|l27-8|9-10|How the Verse Was Used, and Answered — Later law books used 10.90.12 to support birth-based caste, while reformers and scholars have answered in many ways.
hin|14|l27-9|9-10|A Question Beside a Structure — Reading 10.129 and 10.90 side by side shows two different ways ancient poets imagined the beginning.
hin|14|l27-10|9-10|Why These Hymns Still Matter — Both hymns are still recited, taught and debated, and each speaks to questions people ask today.
hin|14|l28-1|9-10|How the Game Was Lost — In the Sabha Parva, Yudhishthira accepts a dice challenge and loses everything, including his brothers, himself and Draupadi.
hin|14|l28-2|9-10|Draupadi's Question — Draupadi asks whether a man who had already lost himself could stake her, and the elders cannot answer.
hin|14|l28-3|9-10|What the Silence Means — Readers argue about the elders' silence and about Draupadi's protection, and the scene shapes the whole epic.
hin|14|l28-4|9-10|A Lake Guarded by Questions — In the Vana Parva, a Yaksha guarding a lake asks Yudhishthira riddles, and he answers them after his brothers fall.
hin|14|l28-5|9-10|The Greatest Wonder — Asked what is the greatest wonder, Yudhishthira answers that people see others die every day yet live as if they never will.
hin|14|l28-6|9-10|One Brother Back — Offered one brother's life, Yudhishthira chooses Nakula, son of his stepmother, to keep fairness between the two mothers.
hin|14|l28-7|9-10|The Son No One Knew — Karna is secretly Kunti's first son, raised by a charioteer, and he gives his loyalty to Duryodhana, who honored him.
hin|14|l28-8|9-10|Kunti's Secret, Karna's Choice — Before the war, Krishna and Kunti tell Karna the truth, but he stays with Duryodhana, choosing loyalty over kinship.
hin|14|l28-9|9-10|The Fire Ordeal — In the Yuddha Kanda, after Rama wins Sita back, she passes through fire to prove her purity, and Agni returns her unharmed.
hin|14|l28-10|9-10|Return to the Earth — In the Uttara Kanda, Sita is sent away, raises her sons, and finally returns to the earth, her mother.
hin|14|l28-11|9-10|Chandrabati's Sita — In the 1500s the Bengali poet Chandrabati retold the Ramayana from Sita's side, one of many voices that read the epic anew.
hin|15|l29-1|11-12|What a Bhashya Does — A bhashya is a formal commentary that explains a root text line by line and argues for one reading against others.
hin|15|l29-2|11-12|The Triple Foundation — Vedanta teachers wrote on three root texts together: the Upanishads, the Bhagavad Gita and the Brahma Sutras.
hin|15|l29-3|11-12|Not Two — Shankara taught Advaita, non-dualism: the self is not other than Brahman, and the sense of separateness comes from ignorance.
hin|15|l29-4|11-12|Shankara Reads "That Art Thou" — Shankara reads tat tvam asi as identity: stripped of limits, the self and Brahman are one and the same.
hin|15|l29-5|11-12|Shankara's Legacy — Tradition credits Shankara with founding monasteries across India, and his teaching shaped modern Hinduism deeply.
hin|15|l29-6|11-12|The Self as God's Body — Ramanuja taught Vishishtadvaita: Brahman, who is Vishnu, is one, and souls and the world are real as his body.
hin|15|l29-7|11-12|Ramanuja Reads the Same Words — Ramanuja reads tat tvam asi as God dwelling in the self, and Gita 18.66 as a call to surrender.
hin|15|l29-8|11-12|Five Differences — Madhva taught Dvaita, dualism: God, souls and matter are eternally distinct, and Vishnu alone is independent.
hin|15|l29-9|11-12|"Thou Art Not That" — Madhva's tradition reads the Chandogya phrase so it teaches difference, and reads the whole Upanishad through that lens.
hin|15|l29-10|11-12|Three Schools Side by Side — Laid side by side, the three schools differ on the self, the world, God and liberation, and each remains a living tradition.
hin|15|l30-1|11-12|Ram Mohan Roy and the Upanishads — Ram Mohan Roy used the Upanishads to argue for worship of one God and for ending sati, the burning of widows.
hin|15|l30-2|11-12|Vivekananda's Vedanta — Swami Vivekananda presented Vedanta to the world in Chicago in 1893 and taught that service to people is service to God.
hin|15|l30-3|11-12|Tilak's Gita of Action — Bal Gangadhar Tilak wrote Gita Rahasya in prison and read the Gita as a call to energetic action in the world.
hin|15|l30-4|11-12|Gandhi's Gita of Nonviolence — Gandhi read the Gita as an allegory of inner struggle and drew from it a discipline of selfless, nonviolent action.
hin|15|l30-5|11-12|Aurobindo's Inner Yoga — Sri Aurobindo moved from revolutionary politics to spiritual teaching, and read the Gita as a guide to a complete yoga.
hin|15|l30-6|11-12|Emerson's "Brahma" — Ralph Waldo Emerson read the Gita and Upanishads and echoed Katha Upanishad and Gita 2.19 in his 1857 poem "Brahma."
hin|15|l30-7|11-12|Thoreau at Walden — Henry David Thoreau took the Gita to Walden Pond and wrote of bathing his mind in its philosophy each morning.
hin|15|l30-8|11-12|Ambedkar's Critique — B.R. Ambedkar criticized the texts that supported caste, including the Manusmriti and parts of the Gita, and in 1956 became a Buddhist.
hin|15|l30-9|11-12|Many Readers, None Ranked — Modern readers inside and outside the tradition read the same texts for reform, freedom, spirit and critique.
hin|16|l31-1|11-12|Two Different Words — Varna names the four classes in the texts; jati names the many birth communities of lived social life.
hin|16|l31-2|11-12|The Manusmriti — The Manusmriti is an ancient law book with strict rules on caste and women, and its authority has always been debated.
hin|16|l31-3|11-12|Shambuka and Ekalavya — Two epic episodes, Shambuka in the Ramayana and Ekalavya in the Mahabharata, are central to modern debates about caste in the texts.
hin|16|l31-4|11-12|Basava and the Vachanas — Basava, a 12th-century Shiva devotee in Karnataka, rejected caste distinctions and founded a community of equal devotees.
hin|16|l31-5|11-12|Ravidas and Begampura — Ravidas, a bhakti poet from a leatherworking family, imagined a city without sorrow or caste, and his hymns are in the Guru Granth Sahib.
hin|16|l31-6|11-12|Jyotirao Phule — Jyotirao Phule and Savitribai Phule opened schools for girls and lower castes and challenged Brahmin authority in the 1800s.
hin|16|l31-7|11-12|Mahad, 1927 — At Mahad in 1927, Ambedkar led a march for water rights and a public burning of the Manusmriti.
hin|16|l31-8|11-12|The Constitution of 1950 — India's Constitution, drafted under Ambedkar's leadership, abolished untouchability and guaranteed equality before the law.
hin|16|l31-9|11-12|Gargi and Maitreyi — The Brihadaranyaka Upanishad shows two women, Gargi and Maitreyi, questioning the great teacher Yajnavalkya.
hin|16|l31-10|11-12|Mirabai and Modern Scholars — Mirabai defied custom out of devotion to Krishna, and today women scholars and chanters are reshaping the study of the texts.
hin|16|l32-1|11-12|Comparison Without Ranking — Comparing scriptures means placing them side by side to see each more clearly, not deciding which one is true or better.
hin|16|l32-2|11-12|Reading Across Traditions — Each scripture has its own language, setting and community, so a fair comparison names them for every text.
hin|16|l32-3|11-12|Arjuna and Job — Arjuna and Job both face terrible situations and question, and both receive a vast vision of the divine in reply, as their texts tell it.
hin|16|l32-4|11-12|Action and Its Fruits in the Dhammapada — The Dhammapada, like the Gita, teaches that the mind shapes action, but it grounds this in the Buddha's teaching of no fixed self.
hin|16|l32-5|11-12|Role and Rightness in the Analects — The Analects and the Gita both link right action to one's role, but Confucius stresses learning, ritual and relationships.
hin|16|l32-6|11-12|Ahimsa in Jain and Buddhist Texts — Jain and Buddhist texts teach ahimsa, non-harming, with great seriousness, and Gandhi drew on these traditions too.
hin|16|l32-7|11-12|The Sermon on the Mount — The Sermon on the Mount teaches loving enemies and not returning evil for evil, and it shaped Tolstoy, Gandhi and King.
hin|16|l32-8|11-12|Bhakti and the Psalms — The Gita's bhakti and the Psalms both speak of trust and longing for God, in different words and settings.
hin|16|l32-9|11-12|Bhakti and Sufi Poetry — Sufi poets and Hindu bhakti poets both sang of longing for the divine, and in India they sometimes shared audiences and images.
hin|17|l33-1|11-12|Ramlila — Ramlila, the staged retelling of the Rama story, is performed across North India each autumn and is on UNESCO's intangible heritage list.
hin|17|l33-2|11-12|Kathakali and Bharatanatyam — Classical dance forms such as Kathakali and Bharatanatyam tell the epics through gesture, expression and music.
hin|17|l33-3|11-12|The Texts in Stone — Temple carvings from India to Cambodia and Indonesia tell the epics in stone, for worshippers and visitors to read.
hin|17|l33-4|11-12|Amar Chitra Katha — Amar Chitra Katha comics, founded in 1967, taught generations of children the epics and Puranas in picture form.
hin|17|l33-5|11-12|The Ramayan on Television — Ramanand Sagar's Ramayan (1987 to 1988) drew huge audiences and shaped how many people picture the story.
hin|17|l33-6|11-12|Hindu Americans and Their Temples — Hindu Americans have built temples, schools and festivals, like the Hindu Temple of Greater Chicago in Lemont, that carry the texts into new settings.
hin|17|l33-7|11-12|Diwali in Public — Diwali is now marked in schools, city halls and the White House, and Hindu Americans discuss what public recognition means.
hin|17|l33-8|11-12|Yoga, Om and Appreciation — Many Hindu Americans welcome interest in their traditions but object when sacred symbols are used carelessly.
hin|17|l33-9|11-12|Representation in Schools — Hindu American students and scholars have asked for accurate, respectful teaching about Hinduism, and they do not all agree on what that means.
hin|17|l34-1|11-12|Choosing a Passage — A good capstone passage is short, much discussed and read in more than one way by named readers.
hin|17|l34-2|11-12|Establishing the Text — Before arguing, state exactly which text, verse and translation you use, and note any important differences between versions.
hin|17|l34-3|11-12|Setting the Context — Context means the passage's place in its text, its speaker and listener, and its date, both scholarly and traditional.
hin|17|l34-4|11-12|Gathering Three Readings — Gather a classical commentator, a modern reader and a scholar, and state each reading in words its holder would accept.
hin|17|l34-5|11-12|Why Readers Differ — Readers differ because of their schools, settings, questions and methods, and naming these reasons is the heart of your analysis.
hin|17|l34-6|11-12|Building the Argument — A strong capstone states a thesis about why readings differ, supports it with cited evidence and answers an objection.
hin|17|l34-7|11-12|Citing and Attributing — Every quotation needs an exact citation, and every belief needs to be attributed to the person or tradition who holds it.
hin|17|l34-8|11-12|Presenting to the Class — A clear presentation shows the passage, the three readings and your thesis, and invites respectful questions.

hin|3|l6-6|k-2|Ahimsa: Not Hurting — Ahimsa means not hurting any living thing.
hin|3|l6-7|k-2|Seva: Helping Others — Seva means helping others without asking for anything back.
hin|4|l7-1|3-5|Many Books, Many Voices — Hinduism has no single holy book but a large library of sacred texts.
hin|4|l7-2|3-5|The Names Hindu and Sanatana Dharma — Many Hindus call their tradition Sanatana Dharma, the eternal way.
hin|4|l7-3|3-5|Different Favorites — Different Hindus treasure different texts.
hin|4|l7-4|3-5|Seers Who Heard — Hindus hold that the shruti texts were heard by ancient seers called rishis.
hin|4|l7-5|3-5|The Four Vedas — There are four Vedas, and the Rig Veda is the oldest.
hin|4|l7-6|3-5|The Upanishads — The Upanishads are shruti texts that ask big questions about the self and the world.
hin|4|l7-7|3-5|Texts People Remember and Retell — Smriti texts were remembered and passed on by people, and many Hindus know them best.
hin|4|l7-8|3-5|The Two Great Epics — The Ramayana and the Mahabharata are the two great Hindu epics.
hin|4|l7-9|3-5|The Gita Inside the Epic — The Bhagavad Gita is a short, much-loved text inside the Mahabharata.
hin|4|l7-10|3-5|Puranas and Law Books — The Puranas tell stories of the gods, and the law books give rules for living.
hin|4|l8-1|3-5|Sanskrit — Most of the old Hindu texts are in Sanskrit, an ancient language of India.
hin|4|l8-2|3-5|Words We Borrowed — English has borrowed many words from Sanskrit.
hin|4|l8-3|3-5|Many Scripts — Sanskrit can be written in many scripts, and today it is often written in Devanagari.
hin|4|l8-4|3-5|Before Writing — The Vedas were passed down by memory for many centuries before they were written.
hin|4|l8-5|3-5|Chanting Patterns — Special chanting patterns helped keep every sound the same.
hin|4|l8-6|3-5|A World Treasure — UNESCO honored Vedic chanting as world heritage in 2003.
hin|4|l8-7|3-5|Guru and Student — Hindu texts were passed on from guru to student.
hin|4|l8-8|3-5|Palm-Leaf Books — Many texts were later written on dried palm leaves.
hin|4|l8-9|3-5|Retold in Many Languages — The stories were retold in Tamil, Hindi and many other languages.
hin|4|l8-10|3-5|Comics, TV and Apps — Today the texts travel through comic books, TV and apps too.
hin|5|l9-1|3-5|Valmiki, the First Poet — Hindu tradition honors Valmiki as the first poet and the author of the Ramayana.
hin|5|l9-2|3-5|Seven Books — Valmiki's Ramayana has seven books, called kandas, and about 24,000 verses.
hin|5|l9-3|3-5|How Old Is the Ramayana? — Tradition and scholars give different dates for the Ramayana.
hin|5|l9-4|3-5|A King and Four Sons — King Dasharatha of Ayodhya has four sons, and Rama is the eldest.
hin|5|l9-5|3-5|Kaikeyi's Two Wishes — Queen Kaikeyi uses two old promises to change who will be king.
hin|5|l9-6|3-5|Rama Chooses Exile — Rama accepts exile calmly so that his father's word is kept.
hin|5|l9-7|3-5|Bharata's Choice — Bharata refuses the throne and rules in Rama's name, with Rama's sandals on the throne.
hin|5|l9-8|3-5|Sages and Simple Living — In the forest, Rama, Sita and Lakshmana live simply and meet wise sages.
hin|5|l9-9|3-5|Dharma: Right for Your Role — In the Ramayana, dharma means doing what is right for your role in life.
hin|5|l9-10|3-5|Tulsidas Tells It Again — In the 1500s, the poet Tulsidas retold the Ramayana in a form of Hindi.
hin|5|l10-1|3-5|Sita in Lanka — In Lanka, Sita stays strong and refuses Ravana.
hin|5|l10-2|3-5|Hanuman's Journey — Hanuman leaps to Lanka, finds Sita and brings back word to Rama.
hin|5|l10-3|3-5|The Bridge to Lanka — Rama's army builds a bridge across the sea, and Ravana's brother joins Rama.
hin|5|l10-4|3-5|The Battle — Rama defeats Ravana in a great battle and frees Sita.
hin|5|l10-5|3-5|Coming Home — Rama returns to Ayodhya and is crowned king, and many Hindus remember this at Diwali.
hin|5|l10-6|3-5|Rama Rajya — Rama Rajya, "the rule of Rama," is an ideal of fair and peaceful government.
hin|5|l10-7|3-5|Hard Questions About Sita — Readers ask hard questions about how Sita is treated, and they answer in different ways.
hin|5|l10-8|3-5|Sita's Side of the Story — Many tellings, including women's songs and a Bengali Ramayana by a woman poet, put Sita at the center.
hin|5|l10-9|3-5|The Ramayana Across Asia — The Ramayana traveled to Southeast Asia, where it has its own tellings.
hin|5|l10-10|3-5|Ramlila: The Story on Stage — In the Ramlila, towns act out the Ramayana, often over many nights before Dussehra.
hin|6|l11-1|3-5|One of the Longest Poems — The Mahabharata is often called the longest poem in the world.
hin|6|l11-2|3-5|Vyasa and Ganesha the Scribe — Tradition credits Vyasa with the Mahabharata, and a famous legend says Ganesha wrote it down.
hin|6|l11-3|3-5|When Was It Made? — Tradition and scholars give different dates for the Mahabharata.
hin|6|l11-4|3-5|The Five Pandavas — The five Pandava brothers are the heroes of the Mahabharata.
hin|6|l11-5|3-5|The Hundred Kauravas — The Kauravas are the Pandavas' hundred cousins, led by Duryodhana.
hin|6|l11-6|3-5|Draupadi — Draupadi, the Pandavas' wife, is one of the strongest voices in the epic.
hin|6|l11-7|3-5|The Dice Game — In a rigged dice game, Yudhishthira loses everything, and Draupadi is shamed in the hall.
hin|6|l11-8|3-5|Years in Exile — The Pandavas spend twelve years in the forest and one year in disguise.
hin|6|l11-9|3-5|The War at Kurukshetra — When peace talks fail, the cousins fight an eighteen-day war at Kurukshetra.
hin|6|l11-10|3-5|Everyone Has a Reason — The Mahabharata shows hard choices where many people have reasons for what they do.
hin|6|l12-1|3-5|The Song of the Lord — The Bhagavad Gita is an 18-chapter teaching inside the Mahabharata.
hin|6|l12-2|3-5|Arjuna Puts Down His Bow — Arjuna does not want to fight his own relatives and teachers.
hin|6|l12-3|3-5|Krishna the Charioteer — Krishna, Arjuna's charioteer, becomes his teacher, and many Hindus honor him as God.
hin|6|l12-4|3-5|Do Your Duty, Not for the Reward — Krishna teaches Arjuna to do his duty without being greedy for the reward.
hin|6|l12-5|3-5|The Self That Does Not Die — Krishna teaches that the true self, the atman, is never born and never dies.
hin|6|l12-6|3-5|Many Ways to Read the Gita — Hindus and others read the Gita in many different ways.
hin|6|l12-7|3-5|Savitri and Satyavan — Savitri chooses Satyavan as her husband, even though she knows he will die soon.
hin|6|l12-8|3-5|Savitri Wins — With patience and wise words, Savitri wins Satyavan back from Yama.
hin|6|l12-9|3-5|Nala and Damayanti — Nala and Damayanti lose everything through dice, and their love and courage bring them back together.
hin|6|l12-10|3-5|"What Is Here Is Everywhere" — The Mahabharata claims to contain everything, and many readers find their own lives in it.
hin|7|l13-1|3-5|Dharma: The Right Way to Live — Hindus use the word dharma for duty and the right way to live, and it can be different for each person.
hin|7|l13-2|3-5|Karma: Actions Have Results — Karma means action, and Hindus believe every action has results.
hin|7|l13-3|3-5|Four Aims of Life — Hindu texts name four aims of a good life: dharma, artha, kama and moksha.
hin|7|l13-4|3-5|Samsara: The Wheel of Rebirth — Many Hindus believe in samsara, a cycle of birth, death and rebirth.
hin|7|l13-5|3-5|Moksha: Freedom — Many Hindus believe the highest goal is moksha, freedom from the cycle of rebirth.
hin|7|l13-6|3-5|Karma in a Story You Know — Hindu stories often show how actions lead to results, which helps readers remember karma.
hin|7|l13-7|3-5|Brahman: One Reality, Many Forms — Many Hindus speak of one ultimate reality, Brahman, honored in many forms such as Vishnu, Shiva and Devi.
hin|7|l13-8|3-5|Three Paths — The Bhagavad Gita describes several paths toward moksha: action, knowledge and devotion.
hin|7|l13-9|3-5|Putting the Ideas Together — Dharma, karma, samsara and moksha fit together as one picture of life in many Hindu texts.
hin|7|l14-1|3-5|Why the Dates Move — Most Hindu festivals follow a calendar based on both the moon and the sun, so their dates change each year.
hin|7|l14-2|3-5|Festivals Remember Stories — Many Hindu festivals remember a story from the epics or the Puranas.
hin|7|l14-3|3-5|Pongal: A Harvest Thank-You — Pongal is a Tamil harvest festival in January that gives thanks to the sun.
hin|7|l14-4|3-5|Diwali: Lights and Lakshmi — Diwali, the festival of lights, celebrates light over darkness with lamps, sweets and prayer.
hin|7|l14-5|3-5|Holi: Colors of Spring — Holi is a spring festival of colors that remembers the story of Prahlad and the playful love of Krishna.
hin|7|l14-6|3-5|Navaratri and Durga Puja — Navaratri, the nine nights, honors the Goddess, and in Bengal it is celebrated as Durga Puja.
hin|7|l14-7|3-5|Janmashtami and Ganesh Chaturthi — Janmashtami celebrates Krishna's birth, and Ganesh Chaturthi celebrates Ganesha.
hin|7|l14-8|3-5|Puja at Home — Puja is Hindu worship, and many families do it every day at a home shrine.
hin|7|l14-9|3-5|Inside a Temple — In a Hindu temple, priests care for the murtis and lead worship such as arati.
hin|7|l14-10|3-5|The Temple in Lemont — The Hindu Temple of Greater Chicago in Lemont, Illinois, has a Rama temple and serves many families.
hin|8|l15-1|6-8|The Oldest Hindu Text — The Rig Veda is a collection of 1,028 hymns in ten books, and scholars date it to about 1500–1000 BCE.
hin|8|l15-2|6-8|Hymns as Poems and Ritual Words — The Rig Veda's hymns are both poetry and words spoken in rituals.
hin|8|l15-3|6-8|Griffith's English Rig Veda — Ralph T.H. Griffith published a full English translation of the Rig Veda in the 1890s, now in the public domain.
hin|8|l15-4|6-8|Agni, the Fire — Agni, the fire, is praised as a priest and messenger who carries offerings to the other gods.
hin|8|l15-5|6-8|Indra and Varuna — Indra is the warrior god who frees the waters, and Varuna is the guardian of cosmic order.
hin|8|l15-6|6-8|Ushas, the Dawn — The hymns to Ushas, the goddess of dawn, are among the Rig Veda's most admired poems.
hin|8|l15-7|6-8|The Gayatri Mantra — The Gayatri mantra, Rig Veda 3.62.10, is a prayer to the sun that many Hindus recite daily.
hin|8|l15-8|6-8|The Creation Hymn — Rig Veda 10.129 asks how the world began and ends by wondering whether anyone truly knows.
hin|8|l15-9|6-8|Hymns About Society — The Purusha hymn, Rig Veda 10.90, names four social groups, and readers have long argued about what it means.
hin|8|l15-10|6-8|When Was It Composed? — Scholars and traditional Hindus give different dates for the Rig Veda, and the debate continues.
hin|8|l16-1|6-8|Rig, Sama, Yajur and Atharva — Hindus recognize four Vedas, each with its own job: praise, song, ritual formulas and everyday prayers.
hin|8|l16-2|6-8|Four Layers in Each Veda — Each Veda grew in layers: Samhitas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas and Upanishads.
hin|8|l16-3|6-8|Shruti and Smriti — Hindus often divide sacred texts into shruti, what is heard, and smriti, what is remembered.
hin|8|l16-4|6-8|Yajna: Offerings to the Fire — The Vedic yajna is a fire ritual in which offerings are given to the gods through Agni.
hin|8|l16-5|6-8|The Vedas at Weddings and Life Rites — Vedic verses are still chanted at Hindu weddings and other life-cycle rites called samskaras.
hin|8|l16-6|6-8|Changing Views of Ritual — Over time, Hindu thinkers asked what ritual means, and many turned inward toward knowledge and devotion.
hin|8|l16-7|6-8|Memory Before Writing — The Vedas were passed down orally for many centuries before they were written down.
hin|8|l16-8|6-8|The Patha Methods — Vedic reciters use patterned methods, called pathas, that make mistakes easy to catch.
hin|8|l16-9|6-8|Who Could Learn the Vedas? — In the past, Vedic learning was limited mostly to men of certain groups, but that has changed in many places.
hin|8|l16-10|6-8|Vedas in the Wider World — Vedic chanting is honored today as world heritage and heard in homes, temples and recordings.
hin|9|l17-1|6-8|Sitting Near a Teacher — The Upanishads are texts of teachings and dialogues, the oldest composed c. 800–200 BCE by scholarly estimate.
hin|9|l17-2|6-8|Vedanta: The End of the Veda — The Upanishads are called Vedanta, the end of the Veda, and they became the basis of the Vedanta schools.
hin|9|l17-3|6-8|Max Müller's Translations — F. Max Müller translated twelve principal Upanishads into English in the Sacred Books of the East (1879 and 1884).
hin|9|l17-4|6-8|Atman: The Self — The Upanishads use the word atman for the true self, which they describe as deeper than body and mind.
hin|9|l17-5|6-8|Brahman: Ultimate Reality — The Upanishads use Brahman for the ultimate reality that is the ground of everything.
hin|9|l17-6|6-8|That Art Thou — The Chandogya Upanishad's teaching, often rendered 'That art thou' (6.8.7), links the self to ultimate reality.
hin|9|l17-7|6-8|Shankara: Not Two — Shankara's Advaita Vedanta reads the Upanishads to say the self and Brahman are not two but one.
hin|9|l17-8|6-8|Ramanuja and Madhva — Ramanuja and Madhva read the same Upanishads as teaching a loving relationship between the soul and a personal God.
hin|9|l17-9|6-8|Lead Me from the Unreal — A short prayer from the Brihadaranyaka Upanishad (1.3.28) is still recited widely by Hindus today.
hin|9|l17-10|6-8|The Upanishads and the World — The Upanishads have shaped Hindu thought and have drawn readers from around the world.
hin|9|l18-1|6-8|A Boy Who Asks — In the Katha Upanishad, young Nachiketa questions Yama, the lord of death, about what happens after death.
hin|9|l18-2|6-8|The Good and the Pleasant — Yama teaches that people must choose between what is pleasant and what is truly good.
hin|9|l18-3|6-8|The Chariot — The Katha Upanishad compares a person to a chariot, with the self as the rider and the mind as the reins.
hin|9|l18-4|6-8|Svetaketu Learns Again — In Chandogya Upanishad 6, Uddalaka teaches his proud son Svetaketu through everyday examples.
hin|9|l18-5|6-8|Satyakama Tells the Truth — In Chandogya 4.4, Satyakama honestly admits he does not know his father's name, and the teacher accepts him for his truthfulness.
hin|9|l18-6|6-8|Questioning as Learning — The Upanishads present asking questions as a respected way to learn.
hin|9|l18-7|6-8|Gargi at the King's Court — In Brihadaranyaka Upanishad 3, Gargi Vachaknavi questions the sage Yajnavalkya in a public debate.
hin|9|l18-8|6-8|Maitreyi's Question — In the Brihadaranyaka, Maitreyi asks Yajnavalkya what will make her immortal, and he teaches her about the self.
hin|9|l18-9|6-8|Reading the Stories Today — Hindus and scholars today read the Upanishad stories as teachings about curiosity, honesty and who may learn.
